The Harvest of Leaves

The Harvest of Leaves

Riarhin VI

Part One — The Landing at Carrickane

Chapter One — The Pot

Davra put in at Carrickane because the wind had gone round to the east and because she had forty barrels of salt fish to take aboard, and she told Riarhin the second reason first.

“The fish is paid for,” she said. “The wind isn’t. If it comes back to the north tomorrow we sail on the evening tide. If it doesn’t, you may as well stretch your legs. Don’t go farther than you can hear the bell.”

She was a short, square woman with a voice that had been worn flat by shouting over weather, and she had taken the company off Muirenn’s boats beyond the point at Scarrow two days before with the air of somebody accepting a delivery of livestock. She had counted them. She had counted their bags. She had looked at Berent being handed up the side and said that if he died aboard her ship she would charge for the canvas.

Now she stood at the rail with a slate under her arm and watched the landing come up out of the morning.

It was a small place. A stone quay, a slipway green with weed, a row of houses with their backs to the hill, and above them, on a shoulder of turf where the hill began in earnest, one more house by itself with smoke going straight up from its chimney into the still air. There were nets spread on the wall. There were two boats drawn up. There was a man standing on the quay with his arms folded, watching Davra’s ship come in as though it had already done something to annoy him.

“Who’s that?” Riarhin asked.

“The harbour officer. Or he says he is.” Davra made a mark on the slate. “The king’s man at the landing. He’ll want to know who we are and what we want and whether we mean to eat.”

“Do we?”

“My crew do. They always do.” Davra did not look up. “Your people can please themselves.”

The dispute began before the gangplank was down.

The harbour officer wanted the names of everyone aboard. Davra gave him the ship’s name and her own, and the count of her crew, and said that she was carrying passengers under an arrangement with the landing at Rossan and did not keep their names on her slate. The officer said that the king’s peace required it. Davra said that the king’s peace had never yet required her to write down a name she had not been told. A woman came down from the row of houses with a child on her hip and stood at the head of the quay and listened, and then another, and an old man with a pipe, and by the time the plank was down there was a small crowd.

It was not, Riarhin gathered after a while, about names. It was about food. The landing had a custom that a ship which put in for longer than a tide was fed by the houses, and a ship which put in to load was not, and Davra’s ship was doing both, and nobody could agree which custom governed. The harbour officer thought the houses should feed the crew and the king should repay them. The woman with the child said the king had never repaid anybody for anything in her lifetime. The old man said that in his father’s time the ships fed themselves and were glad to. Davra said her crew would eat what they were given and pay for what they were sold and she did not much care which it was so long as somebody decided before noon.

Riarhin stood at the rail and listened. She found that she was enjoying it. It was the first quarrel in a month that had nothing to do with her.

Beside her Amari leaned on the rail with his arms crossed.

“They’ll feed them,” he said.

“How do you know?”

“The woman with the child has already sent the girl up the hill.”

Riarhin looked. A girl of about twelve was going up the path from the houses towards the one house on the shoulder, fast, in bare feet, with her skirt held up out of the wet.

The girl came back down half an hour later with a pot.

It was an iron pot with a lid, and it was heavy, and she carried it with both arms wrapped round it and her chin on the lid, and she walked straight through the crowd on the quay, which parted for her, and up to the foot of the gangplank, and set it down there. Steam came out round the lid. It smelled of fish and onion and something green.

“From Enid,” she said.

The quarrel stopped.

The harbour officer looked at the pot, and then up at the house on the shoulder of the hill, and then at Davra.

“That settles it, then,” said the woman with the child.

“It settles nothing,” said the officer. “It’s one pot.”

“It’s Enid’s pot,” said the old man, as though that were a different thing, and the officer seemed to agree that it was. He unfolded his arms. He said that he supposed the houses might see to the rest, as there was a pot, and that he would write to the king’s steward about the principle. Nobody objected to that. The woman with the child went back up to the houses and began shouting at somebody through a door.

The girl was still standing at the foot of the plank.

“She says,” said the girl, looking at nobody, “that somebody’s to bring back the pot.”

There was a silence of a different kind. Riarhin saw the crew look at each other. She saw Davra look at the slate.

“I’ll bring it,” said Riarhin.

The girl looked at her properly for the first time. She had a thin brown face and very light eyes and a scab on her chin.

“When it’s empty,” she said. “Not before. She doesn’t want it back full.”

Muirenn had told her about Enid.

It had been at Scarrow, the night before the boats, in the storehouse by the slip, while they were counting water barrels. Riarhin had asked where Davra’s ship would put in on its way south, if it put in at all, and Muirenn had said Carrickane, probably, for the fish, and then had stopped counting and stood with her hand on a barrel.

“There’s a woman there,” she had said. “Above the landing. If you’re held.”

“What sort of woman?”

“The sort you go to when you’ve made a mess of something and the kind ones have run out of ideas.” Muirenn had gone back to the barrels. “My mother went to her once, before I was born. She never said what for. She said Enid fed her first and told her the price after, and told it once, and never said it again.” She had looked up. “You’ve a thing you mean to do over the water.”

“Yes.”

“Then you might want it priced.” Muirenn had shrugged. “Or you might not. Some don’t. My mother said it was the worst afternoon of her life and she’d not have missed it.”

Riarhin had not said anything. She had not meant to go. She had put it with the other things she had been told at Scarrow and not meant to use, and gone on counting barrels.

Then there had been the letter.

It had come aboard at Scarrow with the last of the water, in a packet of Fenra’s, and Riarhin had not known it was there until the evening before, when Davra’s purser sorted the landing mail on the chart table and found a small square of oilcloth addressed in a hand Riarhin knew as well as her own.

It had come from Ubral by the long road: Tavish’s carter to Deirdre’s, Deirdre’s to Sorley at Coracle Harbor, Sorley’s to the landing at Rossan, where Fenra had held it two days, then sent it on to Scarrow in the barrel-cart with a note on the outside in her own square capitals: Came Tuesday. Didn’t want it to miss you.

Riarhin had turned it over in her hands for some time. It was thicker than Ailsa’s letters usually were. There was something folded inside it that was not paper; she could feel the edge through the oilcloth.

She had not opened it. She had put it inside her coat, in the pocket on the right, and gone up on deck and stood at the rail in the dark until the cold drove her down again. She had told herself that she would read it somewhere that was not a ship, where she could sit down and put it on a table and not have it blown out of her hands.

She had written to Ailsa from the quay at Scarrow, three days before. I am going away again. It is not the last time. She had written it plainly, and a little stiffly, and she had meant every word, and it had gone south by Thursday with Sorley’s cart, and this letter had passed it somewhere on the road going the other way. Ailsa had not read hers when she wrote this.

Riarhin did not want to read it on a ship. She did not want to read it at all, and she wanted to read nothing else, and she had carried it against her ribs all night without opening it, the way she had once carried a small hard shape the size of a coin.

The company ate from Enid’s pot on the quay, sitting on the nets, because the cabin was too small and the day had turned fine.

It was very good. It was fish and onion and some green thing Aurine named at once and Cadan asked her to spell. Tavin said it was the best thing he had eaten since the Hall and that he intended to go up the hill and propose marriage. Nessa said the woman was probably seventy. Tavin said that was no obstacle to a man of his temperament. Penny, the magpie, stole a piece of fish from Berent’s bowl while he was looking at the sea, and he did not notice, and Nessa, who did, gave him half of hers without saying why.

Saikou ate standing at the end of the quay, looking up at the house on the hill.

“You are going,” he said, when Riarhin came to stand beside him with the empty pot.

“I said I’d take this back.”

“Yes.”

“That’s all.”

He looked at the pot, and then at her.

“Muirenn told you about her,” he said. It was not a question. “Muirenn told me also. She said that you would probably not go. I said that you probably would. I did not say why.”

“Why?”

“Because you have been carrying something inside your coat since last night,” said Saikou, “and you have touched it eleven times this morning, and you have not opened it.” He looked back at the hill. “And because you are going to kill a queen, and I think you would like somebody to tell you what it costs who is not one of us.”

She did not answer. Behind them Tavin was telling the crew about a ferryman who did not exist.

“Do you want me to come?” said Saikou.

“No.”

“Good. I should not have come. It is the sort of house one goes to alone.” He looked up at the smoke, which was still going straight up. “I will tell Davra where you are, if the wind changes.”

“Tell her I can hear the bell.”

“I will tell her,” said Saikou, “that you said so.”

She went up the hill with the pot.

Chapter Two — Eat First

The door was open. Riarhin stopped on the step anyway, with the pot against her hip, and waited to be seen.

The room inside was low and long and smelled of peat and warm wool. There was a hearth at the far end with a kettle on a crane, a dresser with plates standing on edge, a table scrubbed pale, and at the table a woman sewing a glove. She was not seventy. She might have been fifty, or a hard sixty. She had grey hair pulled back tight enough to lift the corners of her eyes, and big red hands, and she was working a needle through leather with a thimble on her middle finger and the glove held close to her face.

She did not look up.

“Put it by the door,” she said. “On the flag, not the mat. It sweats.”

Riarhin put the pot on the flag.

“Now come in or go down. You’re letting the heat out.”

Riarhin came in. She shut the door behind her. The woman finished her stitch, drew the thread through, bit it, and only then looked up.

It was a plain look. It went over Riarhin from her boots to her hair and back to her face, without hurry, the way a woman at a market looks at a horse she has not yet decided to price. It stopped briefly at the hilt of Remorse over her left shoulder, and at her right sleeve, where Toothpick lay, and at the coat pocket on the right, and came back to her face.

“You’re off Davra’s ship.”

“Yes.”

“You brought the pot yourself. Not one of the lads.”

“Yes.”

“Then you want something.” The woman set the glove down. “Sit. There’s bread and there’s the rest of the fish. You’ll eat before we talk. A frightened person hears the remedy and misses the rest.”

“I’m not frightened.”

“Then you’ll eat faster.” She pointed with the needle. “That chair. Mind the back leg.”

Her name was Enid. She said it while she was cutting the bread, as though it were one more thing that needed to be put on the table. Riarhin gave her own name back without thinking about it, and heard it go into the room, and saw Enid take it and put it away somewhere without comment.

The fish was the same fish that had come down the hill in the pot. The bread was brown and heavy and still warm in the middle. There was butter with salt in it and a jug of buttermilk, and Enid ate with her, slowly, in silence, and did not ask anything, and after the first few mouthfuls Riarhin found that she was hungrier than she had known. She had not eaten properly since Scarrow. She had not, if she was honest, eaten properly since before the wedding.

Enid watched her finish the second piece of bread and pushed the loaf across the table.

“There’s more.”

“Thank you.”

“Don’t thank me for bread. You’ll thank me for the other thing or you won’t.”

The girl came in while Riarhin was on the third piece. It was the girl from the quay, with the scab on her chin. She stopped in the doorway and looked at Riarhin in the chair with the bad back leg, and her mouth went tight.

“She needed a chair,” said Enid. “Sit on the settle.”

“It’s cold on the settle.”

“Then fetch a shawl.”

The girl fetched a shawl from a peg by the door, wrapped it round herself, sat on the settle by the hearth, and stared at Riarhin with the concentrated resentment of the displaced. Riarhin moved to the next chair. The girl looked at the empty chair, and at Riarhin, and at Enid, and after a moment came and sat in it without a word.

“My sister’s girl,” said Enid. “Her mother’s at the fish. She comes up in the day.” She picked up the glove again. “She’s to hear nothing you don’t want heard. Brona. When I tell you, you go down.”

“Yes, Aunt.”

“Now.” Enid put the needle through the leather. “Before you say what you’ve come for, I’ll say what it costs to say it. So you know. You’re under my roof and you’ve had my bread, and that’s given, and it’s not a bargain and it puts you under nothing. The bargain begins when you say it does.”

Riarhin put down the bread.

“The talking is a mark,” said Enid. “Silver. One. For the afternoon, however long the afternoon is. That’s for my time, and the peat, and Brona’s mother’s fish. If I tell you anything you don’t like, it’s still a mark. If I tell you nothing useful, it’s still a mark. If, after, you want a thing done, a working, I’ll tell you what that costs separately, before, all of it, and you’ll say yes or no. You can say no to that and still have had the afternoon. You can walk out of that door now and owe me nothing but the pot, which you’ve brought.” She drew the thread through. “That’s the whole of it. I won’t say it again.”

Riarhin took a mark out of her purse and put it on the table.

Enid looked at it. She did not pick it up.

“You’ve not said.”

“Yes,” said Riarhin. “The afternoon.”

Enid nodded once. She swept the coin into her palm and put it in her apron.

“Brona,” she said. “Go down to your mother.”

It was not what she had come for. She knew that as soon as she began, and could not stop, and did not try very hard.

She began with Lyra.

She had not meant to. She had meant to ask about the thing she was going to do, which was plain and in front of her. But Enid said, “Well?” with the needle going in and out, and Riarhin opened her mouth and what came out was a friend of hers in a prison in Valora, and a supper, and a thing said at the supper that should not have been said.

She told it carefully. She told it the way Lyra had told it to her, in the room above the seamstress’s shop, with the spilled water on the table: that Lyra had gone to a supper intending to say very little; that she had decided, at the table, not to say more; that she had been asked whether a friend had sent for anyone who might reassure him; that she had said yes, and said where, and felt glad to say it. That she had not been drunk, or ill, or confused. That she had been relieved. That afterwards, outside in the street, she had found the thing she had said and not known when she had decided to say it.

“And then?”

“Then it was used.” Riarhin looked at the table. “There was a knight who had gone north to find someone. To find me. The court learned where he had gone, from what she said, and made it part of a case against the man who sent him. He was executed.”

Enid’s needle stopped.

“The knight?”

“The man who sent him. The knight is on Davra’s ship.” Riarhin heard herself. “His name is Berent. He came all the way to Ubral with a letter. To the house where I was staying.”

“In Ubral.”

“Yes.”

“Your people?”

“My mother’s.” She did not know why she was saying it. The bread, perhaps; the warmth; the needle going in and out. “There is somebody there I would like to go back to. I haven’t.”

Enid looked at her for a moment and then went back to the glove. She did not ask the name. Riarhin found that she had been braced for it, and let the brace go.

“Your friend in Valora,” said Enid. “She thinks it was done to her.”

“The woman who gave the supper admitted it. A charm, to encourage confidence. Without leave.”

“Admitted it to whom?”

“To me.”

“And you told your friend.”

“I wrote it. I don’t know what she made of it.” Riarhin put her hand flat on her coat, over the inside pocket on the left, where there were other papers besides the letter she had not opened. “She wrote back. A little. I’m not going to tell you what she said.”

“I haven’t asked.”

“I know. I’m telling you I won’t.”

“Then don’t.” Enid bit a thread. “It’s hers.”

Riarhin sat back. She had expected, she found, to have to fight for that. She had come up the hill with the pot and with the whole of Lyra’s scrap in her head, word for word, the tooth is still missing, tell Orrel the squirrel has excellent judgement, I would like to see somebody I know, and had been ready to guard it against a woman who would want it as the price of an answer. Enid had not wanted it. The readiness had nowhere to go.

“What I want to know,” she said, “is whether it was hers. What she said. Whether she chose it.”

Enid put the glove down and folded her red hands on top of it.

“I can tell you what kinds of working would do that,” she said. “There are four I know, and some I don’t. One makes a person trust the one who’s asking. One makes the room feel safe, so anybody in it would say more. One takes a thing a person already wants to do and makes the wanting louder than the not. One makes the not quieter. They feel different from the outside, to someone who knows. They feel the same from the inside. From the inside it feels like wanting to.”

“Then how would anyone know?”

“Mostly they don’t.” Enid’s voice was quite flat. “Your friend knows because the woman told you. Without that she’d have what she has now, which is a memory of deciding not to and then doing it, and being glad. People have that memory every day with no working in it at all. They decide not to have the last cup and they have the last cup. They decide not to say the cruel thing and they say it.”

“That’s not the same.”

“I didn’t say it was. I said it feels the same.” Enid looked at her. “You want me to tell you whether she was free. I can’t. I wasn’t there, and I’m not her, and I’ve never met the woman who did it, and all I have is your telling of her telling. You’re an honest teller. I can hear that. It’s still two tellings from the thing.”

Riarhin said nothing.

“Here’s what I can tell you,” said Enid. “She could have stopped. The mouth was hers. Nothing held it. If she’d bitten her tongue she’d have bitten it. That’s true.” She paused. “And it settles nothing. A man with a knife at your child’s throat doesn’t hold your hand when you sign. You could stop. Nobody would call it free.”

“Is that what this was? A knife?”

“No.” Enid picked the glove up again. “And it wasn’t a friend pouring the wine, either, or a warm room, or a kind voice, though all of those were there too, I’d guess, and did some of the work. That’s the other thing. Don’t let anybody tell you it’s all one. It isn’t. A kind voice is a kind voice. A room is a room. A working is a working. They can sit at the same table and still not be the same thing.”

The needle went in.

“She was made to want it,” said Enid, “by somebody who knew she didn’t. That’s all I know. How much of the wanting was hers to start with, nobody knows. Not you. Not her. Not the woman who did it, probably, though she’ll have an opinion.”

Riarhin looked at the fire.

She thought of Lyra at the table in the room above the seamstress’s shop, turning her cup between her palms. Because I wanted to tell her. She had said it almost angrily. Riarhin had thought at the time that the anger was for Jaennis. She thought now that some of it had been for the wanting itself, which Lyra had recognised, and could not disown, and could not make her own.

“That’s not much,” she said.

“It’s a mark’s worth.” Enid did not smile. “You didn’t come for that anyway. Did you.”

“No.”

“Then eat the rest of that bread,” said Enid, “and we’ll get to it.”

Chapter Three — A Small Hard Shape

They were interrupted twice before she could begin again.

The first time it was a man from the landing, who put his head round the door to say that the harbour officer had written his letter to the king’s steward and wanted Enid to witness that he had written it. Enid said she would witness that he had written a letter and not a word more. The man went away and came back to say that would do. The second time it was the girl, Brona, who had been sent down and had come back up for her shawl, which she had left on the chair, and who stood in the doorway looking from Enid to Riarhin with the expression of somebody who knew what she had interrupted and was in no hurry to leave.

“Take it and go,” said Enid.

“It’s cold.”

“Then you’ll walk quicker.”

When the door had shut again Enid put more peat on the fire and swung the kettle over, and they sat for a while and listened to it begin to tick.

“The queen,” said Enid.

Riarhin looked up.

“That’s what you came about. You said Valora. You said a court and a case and a man executed. You’ve a sword on your back and two knives I can see and I’d guess two I can’t. It’s the queen.”

“Yes.”

“Then say it how you want to say it. Not how you think I want to hear it.”

Riarhin looked at the kettle for some time.

“I brought her a thing,” she said at last.

She had not known she would begin there either.

“A gift. From the south. I carried it a long way. I found it in a dead city in a place where the water had gone, and I carried it out, and I was asked to carry it north, to her, as a gift from the people whose country it was found in. I did. I gave it to her in a room in Valora, before I went north. She wears it.” She put her hand to her own throat, where there was nothing. “She wore it the last time I saw her. On a black ribbon. She never takes it off.”

“What is it?”

“A brass case. With a green glass in it. The size of a coin.”

“That’s what it looks like. I asked what it is.”

“I don’t know.” Riarhin heard her own voice go level. “Nobody knows. There’s a man on Davra’s ship who has spent three years finding out and he says he doesn’t know either. He has a theory. He calls it consistent with. He won’t say more than that.”

“And you think it’s done something.”

“I think since she’s worn it everything she wants has come to her.” It came out harder than she meant. “Not all at once. Nothing you could point to. The grain arrives the week it’s needed. The enemy’s bridge goes out in a flood. A duke who might have made trouble falls down a stair. The people who might have stopped her decide, one after another, for good reasons, not to. She was always capable. She was always going to do a great deal. But she was never this—” She stopped. “Fortunate.”

“And you brought it to her.”

“Yes.”

Enid said nothing. The kettle ticked.

“That isn’t what you want to ask me either,” said Enid.

No. It wasn’t.

Riarhin looked down at her own hands on the scrubbed table. The left one had a scar across the back of the knuckles, old and white. The right one had ink on the second finger from the letter she had not finished writing on Davra’s ship.

“I carried it for months,” she said. “Before I gave it to her. In my coat. In a pocket on the right side, inside, wrapped in a piece of linen a woman tore off a longer piece for me with her foot braced against a table.” She could see the table, and the tear running across the weave. “I didn’t think about it much. It was a thing I was carrying. There were other things to think about.”

Enid waited.

“There was a night,” Riarhin said, “on the way back. In the south, in the flat country, near the end. There was a woman I was travelling with.” She said the name, and then wished she had not, and then let it stand. “Sada. We were in her tent. She was asleep. The wind was moving the canvas. The camels were groaning outside; they always did, all night, as though somebody were making them do it. And there was something under my hip.”

She could feel it. She could feel it now, sitting at Enid’s table, as exactly as if she were lying on the blanket in the dark: the small round hardness through the cloth of the coat, pressing into the bone.

“I put my hand down and found it through the coat. The amulet. I’d rolled onto it. I took it out, still wrapped, still tied with the thread. I didn’t unwrap it. I held it a moment. It was no warmer than my hand.” She stopped. “Then I put it down on the blanket by my head. On the other side from Sada. So I wouldn’t roll onto it again. And I turned over and went to sleep.”

“That’s all?”

“That’s all.”

“Then why are you telling me?”

“Because I don’t know why I put it on the other side from her,” said Riarhin.

The words came out very quietly. She had not said them aloud before. She had not said them to herself, exactly; she had only turned the memory over, on bad nights, the way one turns a coin one has been given in change and suspects is not good.

“I remember every part of it,” she said. “I remember the canvas, and her breathing, and the smell of the camels, and the thread round the linen, and which way I turned. I remember it the way I remember everything. I can’t forget things. I’ve tried. And I remember putting it down on the far side. Away from her. And I don’t know whether that was because the near side was where she was and there was no room, or because I didn’t want it near her.”

She looked at Enid.

“And if it was because I didn’t want it near her, I don’t know whether that was because I knew something, or because it did. Whether I’d already felt it. Whether it wanted to be kept away from her, and I did what it wanted. Whether it wanted to go to the queen, and every day of that whole road north I was doing what it wanted, and thought it was me.”

The kettle began to sing. Enid got up and swung it off the fire and did not make anything with it. She stood by the hearth with her hand on the crane.

“You want me to tell you,” said Enid, “whether a thing in your pocket, all those years ago, was working on you.”

“Yes.”

“From the colour of it.”

“I haven’t told you the colour.”

“You said green. You said brass. You said the size of a coin.” Enid turned round. “I can’t. I’ve never seen it. If I had it on this table in front of me I could tell you some things it isn’t. I couldn’t tell you what it is. I don’t know what’s in the south. I don’t know what was in your dead city. I know what’s on this island, and what comes to this door, and some of what goes out of it.”

“Then what good—”

“I didn’t say I was good for that.” Enid came back and sat down. “I said I’d talk for a mark. You’ve had your bread. Listen now.”

Riarhin listened.

“You remember it the way you remember everything,” said Enid. “All of it. The canvas. The camels. Which way you turned. I believe you. I can hear it in you; you’re telling it as if you were reading off a page.” She put a finger on the table. “And all that remembering gives you is what you noticed. It doesn’t give you why. Nobody gets why. Not with the best memory under the sky. You noticed you put it on the far side. Good. That’s a true thing and you have it. Whether it was the thing wanting, or you wanting, or you having no room, or you being a woman who didn’t want a lump of brass in the bed with her lover and would have done the same with a bit of flint—” She lifted her hand. “That’s not in the memory. It never was. It’s not there to find.”

“Then I’ll never know.”

“Probably not.” Enid said it without softness. “Most people never know why they did most things. They make up a reason after and live in it like a house. You’ve got a better memory than most, so your house has more rooms. It’s still built after.”

Riarhin sat very still.

She thought of Lyra, turning the cup. I remember deciding not to say more. Lyra had the deciding, and the saying, and not the thing between. Riarhin had the canvas and the camels and the hard shape and the far side of the blanket, and not the thing between.

“It’s the same question,” she said slowly. “As hers.”

“It’s like it,” said Enid. “It’s not the same. Hers had a woman at the table who did a thing and said so. Yours has a lump of brass that’s said nothing to anybody. You don’t know that it did anything at all. You don’t know that it didn’t. You’ve got a bad night in a tent and a lot of luck in a queen and a man on a ship who says consistent with.” She almost smiled; it went away before it arrived. “I’d not hang anybody on that.”

“I’m not going to hang anybody.”

“No,” said Enid. “You’re going to do the other thing.”

She picked the glove up again. There was only the thumb left to do. She threaded the needle against the window, squinting, and got it on the second try.

“So let’s talk about that,” she said. “What you mean to do now.”

Chapter Four — The Price of Succeeding

Before she answered, Riarhin took out the letter she had been writing on the ship.

It was to Glenruan. She had begun it the first night out of Scarrow, on the chart table, while Davra’s purser snored in the next berth, and she had got as far as the second side and stopped. She did not know why she took it out now. Perhaps because Enid had asked what she meant to do, and the letter was the one place she had tried to write it down, and had not.

She laid it on the table beside the bread. The ink on her finger matched the ink on the page.

“To my mother’s people,” she said. “In Ubral. I haven’t said in it what I’m going to do. I’ve said I’m well, and where I’ve been, and that Berent’s leg is mending. I’ve said I’ll write again.”

“Why haven’t you said?”

“Because letters are read.”

“That’s one reason.”

Riarhin did not answer. Enid went on with the thumb of the glove.

Enid put the finished glove on. The thumb bent stiffly. She closed her hand twice, then took it off and laid it beside its mate.

“You mean to kill her.”

Riarhin watched the needle lying across the leather.

“Yes.”

“And him?”

“If he stands in the way.”

“He has stood there a long time.”

Outside, someone called up from the landing. Enid went to the door. Riarhin heard a man asking whether the girl had left the pot, and Enid asking whether he intended to return it before or after the next winter. He laughed. Enid did not. When she came back she stood behind her chair rather than sitting.

“I think you can do it.”

“You haven’t seen either of them fight.”

“No.”

Riarhin waited.

“I have seen you come away from it.”

The words might have meant a dozen things. She let none of them show on her face.

“With what?”

Enid drew out the chair and sat.

“There. That is a better question.”

Riarhin’s letter still lay on the table. She moved it away from a damp ring left by the bowl.

“The woman in Ubral,” Enid said. “Will you go to her?”

“How do you know about her?”

“You told me there was someone you wanted to go back to.”

Riarhin kept Ailsa’s name to herself.

“Perhaps.”

“Who keeps her door?”

“She does.”

“And when men come asking whether you have slept behind it?”

Riarhin’s hand stopped on the letter.

“Veralyn will be dead.”

“Yes.”

From the landing came the scrape of a boat being dragged clear of stone. The men were arguing again. This time Riarhin could distinguish no words.

“And her children?” Enid said.

“With their father.”

“Do they know what you mean to do?”

“No.”

“And your northern kin?”

Riarhin looked up.

“They can look after themselves.”

“They can decide whether to receive you. Will you let them decide before you arrive?”

Tavish would open the door. She knew the movement of his arm, the breadth of him standing aside to let her pass. Elspeth would ask whether she had eaten. There would be time, while somebody found a plate, to tell them why she had come.

“I could stay away.”

“You could.”

“Then they need never answer for me.”

Enid shook her head.

“They know where the knight went to find you.”

“Berent.”

“You named him earlier.” Enid left her hands flat on the table. “His road does not disappear because you take another.”

Riarhin remembered the arrangements Tavish had made: horses, places in a train, a man willing to take them south. She had been grateful for how little she had needed to ask.

“They were in danger before he came,” she said.

“Yes.”

“The queen won’t leave Ubral alone because I leave her alone.”

“No.”

“Then don’t tell me I can keep them safe by staying here.”

“I haven’t. Kill her, and the people looking for you will have that road to follow. They will want names at the end of it.”

Riarhin folded the letter, although it was already folded.

“What are you asking for?”

“Nothing beyond what you paid me.”

“You call this a price.”

“It will cost them. Whether I call it anything or not.”

“And you can prevent it?”

“No.”

The refusal came without ceremony. Riarhin had been waiting for the additional demand: a service, a season, something she could agree to and discharge herself.

Enid watched her put the letter into her coat.

“The queen has a child.”

“I’ve seen her.”

“Who will tell the child what you did?”

Riarhin pushed back her chair. One leg caught in a worn place between the flags and stopped her halfway to standing. She settled it before she rose.

“I didn’t come here to be told she has a daughter.”

“No. You know that part.”

There was enough anger in her now to make leaving easy. At the door she found the girl’s shawl still hanging from its peg. A sleeve had caught beneath it; she freed the sleeve without thinking.

“If I leave her alive?”

Enid had begun putting away the needle and thread. She looked up.

“What do you think she will do?”

Riarhin thought of the maps. The fed horses. Men arriving before anyone had sent for them. She came back to the chair.

“She’ll go on.”

“Yes.”

“And if I kill her, all of it comes apart.”

“Some of it. Some men will be very quick to pick up what she leaves.”

“Which men?”

“I don’t know.”

“You said you saw me come away.”

“I did.” Enid closed the sewing box. “I didn’t say I saw an empty world waiting for you.”

Riarhin sat down.

After a while she said, “Can it be delayed?”

“Which part?”

She had to think before she answered.

“The part where it comes to them,” she said at last. “The ones in Ubral. If I did it later. If I got word to them first. If they had a season to—” She did not know how to end it. To move? To hide? To be somewhere else, when men came up the road from Valora with Berent’s route written down and a list of whose door he had knocked on?

“You could delay it,” said Enid. “All of it. Go south. Go anywhere. Come back in a year, in two, when you’ve got word to everyone you love and they’ve all agreed to be somewhere safe.” She looked at Riarhin across the table. “Is there such a somewhere?”

Riarhin thought of Glenruan in the rain, with the stream loud behind Ailsa’s house and the cup standing in its usual place. Of Tavish with his boots in the mud. Of Iona, who wanted letters about something besides a fence. None of them would go anywhere because a letter told them to. None of them should.

“And the queen,” said Enid, “in that year?”

The man on Davra’s ship with his clerk’s report had read it out on the shingle at Scarrow. A general muster has been called for the spring. It is understood that it is directed at Ecktoria. And behind that, in the same flat hand, the valleys. Four hundred villages in the hills behind Serrat that had not declared. Riarhin had heard, at the Hall, from a merchant who had it from a carter who had it from a soldier, what was being proposed for them, and had not been able to find anyone who would say it had been refused.

“She’d go on,” she said again.

“She would.” Enid put the sewing box on the dresser. “Delay’s a choice. It’s not the choice of nothing. You’d be choosing her spring instead of their winter. You might be right. People have been right to wait before now.” She turned. “You asked me what it costs if you do it. I’ve told you. I’ll tell you what it costs if you don’t, too, for the same mark: it costs whatever she does next, and the road to Ubral is still there, and she still knows who went down it.”

Riarhin looked at her hands.

“That’s no choice at all.”

“It’s a choice. It’s not a good one.” Enid came and sat. “Most of them aren’t. I don’t say which. I don’t know which. I’m not you, and I’m not going over the water, and I won’t be the one who wakes up after.” She put her hands flat again. “You came up here wanting to be told the price. You’ve been told. There isn’t a way through where nobody pays. There’s only who, and how much, and whether you’ll look at them while it’s paid.”

She stood up to go when the light in the window had begun to yellow.

She put her coat on. She checked the knives by habit, the left sleeve and the right, and Remorse on her back, and the pocket on the left side with Lyra’s scrap and the unfinished letter to Glenruan in it. Then she reached into the pocket on the right for her purse, to leave the girl something for the chair, and the purse came out, and the second letter came out with it.

It fell. Riarhin caught it before it reached the floor and laid it beside her purse on the table.

It was the small square of oilcloth with Ailsa’s hand on it. Fenra’s square capitals ran along one edge: Came Tuesday. It was still sealed. Riarhin had not opened it. She had carried it against her ribs all night and up the hill with the pot, and she had not said a word about it to anybody, and she had not said a word about it to Enid.

Enid looked at it.

“She will be safe.”

Riarhin looked at her for a moment.

“And the price for that safety?”

“Not borne by you.”

“Is this a mercy?”

“No.”

Chapter Five — Blue Cloth

She did not ask anything else. Enid did not offer anything else. Riarhin picked up the letter and her purse and put a coin on Brona’s chair, and Enid watched her do it and said nothing about that either, and Riarhin went out and shut the door behind her so that the heat would stay in.

There was a bench outside, against the wall, facing the sea. It was made of a single plank on two stones and it had been sat on for a long time. She sat on it. Below, the landing had gone quiet; the fish had been loaded, and Davra’s crew were sitting on the nets eating whatever the houses had sent down, and somebody had lit a fire on the shingle. The ship lay at the quay with her sails furled. The smoke from the fire went straight up. The wind had not changed.

She broke the seal.

R.,

Tavish says I am to write small because paper costs and the carter charges by the weight. I have told him the carter charges by the packet, and he says that is what the carter wants me to think. You see how things are.

The blue came. Tavish paid for it in the end, all of it, after Elspeth stood in the door of the hall and asked him in front of the drover whether he meant to be known as a man who promised cloth and didn’t pay. He paid the same afternoon. I have put a bit of it in here so you can see the colour. It is the colour of the loch in the morning when there is no wind. I am making it into a coat for Iona, who has grown out of everything and says she will not wear her mother’s old one because it smells of her mother. I have told her it smells of a person who loved her, and she says that is worse.

Your knife is still here. I have not ruined it. I have used it every day for the willow and it is the best knife I have ever had and I would like you to know that I am not giving it back. You should allow me some mysteries. You said so.

The baskets go well. Too well. Mairi has been sending people to me from the market at Kilrenny and I have had to take on Donnel’s girl, from the crossing household, the one whose uncle went down with the cattle. She is slow and very neat and she talks the whole time, and I did not think I would like it, and I do. We have had orders from as far as the coast. A man came up from the harbour with a cart and took forty. I have never in my life had forty of anything.

So I have taken a room. It is over the chandler’s at Kilrenny, by the harbour, one room with a window and a bed and a table, and I am going to keep the stock there and go down twice in the week for the market. It has a good fire. It looks at the boats.

I would like you in it.

I am not asking you to stop. I know what you are. I am saying that there is a room, and a bed, and a window that looks at the boats, and that if you come by sea you will come in at Kilrenny and not at the loch, and I would like to know which boat, so I can be on the quay. I would like some notice. Not much. A week. So I can clean the room and not have you see it the way it is now, which is full of willow.

Everybody here is well. Tavish says to tell you the bridge is holding. Elspeth says to tell you to eat. Iona says to tell you nothing, because you owe her a letter about something besides a fence, and she is keeping count.

I think of you in the mornings. That is when I have the time.

A.

Folded inside the letter, flat, was a strip of cloth the length of her finger. It was blue. It was exactly the blue of the loch in the morning when there was no wind.

Riarhin sat on the bench for a long time.

She held the letter in her lap with the blue strip on top. A gust lifted the cloth and she caught it against the paper. Tavish had paid for it at last. She could see Elspeth in the doorway, waiting while he fetched his purse.

Ailsa had written it before she had Riarhin’s letter from the quay. She had written it to somebody who had not yet said I am going away again. Somewhere on the long road between Kilrenny and Scarrow the two letters had passed each other, and hers had gone on to Ubral saying it is not the last time, and this one had come on to Theona saying I would like you in it, and neither of them had known.

Riarhin found that she was laughing. It was a small sound, the one she had made in Ailsa’s doorway once, when Ailsa told her she should allow her some mysteries. She put her hand over her mouth and it went on for a little while behind her hand, and then stopped.

I want to go.

It was very simple. It sat in her chest like the first warm mouthful of something after a cold day. She wanted to come in at Kilrenny on a boat and see Ailsa on the quay in the morning with her sleeves pushed up, and go up the stair over the chandler’s, and find the room full of willow because Ailsa had not had a week after all, and not care. She wanted the window that looked at the boats. She wanted to hear Donnel’s girl talk the whole time. She wanted to sit at the table and eat whatever there was and be told by Elspeth, from forty miles off, to eat.

She laid the cloth over the back of her hand, where Ailsa would have held it to judge the colour.

She will be safe.

Riarhin turned the packet over. There was no sender’s name on the outside. She tucked it beneath her palm.

And not borne by you. And no.

She had agreed to nothing. Enid had said no and put away her sewing. Riarhin could still hear the flatness of her voice.

It did not say the Rockes would be safe. It did not say Iona, or Tavish, or Elspeth, or Mairi, or Donnel’s girl who talked the whole time, or Ailsa’s children in their father’s house. It said she.

Riarhin folded the strip of blue cloth into the letter and put the letter back into the pocket on the right.

She wrote the answer on the bench, on the back of the unfinished letter to Glenruan, with the stub of pencil she kept in her sleeve beside Toothpick.

A.,

Your letter came to me on a ship. I read it on a bench outside a stranger’s house with the sea in front of me, and I laughed, which I have not done in some time. Thank you for the blue. It is the right colour. You are right about the knife.

By now you will have had mine from Scarrow and you will know I have gone away again. I am sorry that yours and mine passed each other on the road. I would rather you had read yours first.

She stopped. She looked at the sea. Then she wrote:

I don’t know when I can come. I want to.

She read it, set the pencil to the next line, and lifted it again, leaving a small dark point.

Because that was the other half of it. Enid had said: will you let them decide before you arrive? And the answer, sitting on the bench with the pencil in her hand, was that she did not know how. She could write there is danger. Every letter from Ubral had danger in it; they lived in it; Tavish would read it and nod and go on mending the bridge. She could write I am going to do a thing that will bring men up your road, and the letter would go by Sorley’s cart and Deirdre’s and three other hands before it reached Kilrenny, and anybody who opened it on the way would know what she meant to do, and where, and roughly when, and whom she cared about enough to warn.

She could not write the warning without writing the list. And the list was the thing the men would want.

She sat with the pencil above the paper until her fingers were cold.

Then she wrote: Tell Tavish I will write to him properly soon, and that it is about the road the knight took, and that he should read it himself and not in the hall. She looked at that for a long time. It said too much and not nearly enough. She let it stand.

Eat something. Keep the knife. R.

She did not seal it. She would find a better way before it went, or she would not, and either way she was not going to give it to Davra’s purser tonight.

The bell went at the landing while she was walking down. It was the ship’s bell, two strokes, then two. Davra wanted her people aboard.

The wind had gone round to the north.

Saikou was standing at the foot of the path, where it came down onto the quay, with his hands in his sleeves. He looked at her face as she came down, and then at the empty space at her side where the pot had been, and did not ask anything.

“The wind,” he said.

“I know.”

“Davra says the evening tide.”

“I know.”

He fell into step beside her. They walked along the quay together towards the ship, past the fire on the shingle, past the nets, past the harbour officer, who was sitting on a bollard reading over his letter to the king’s steward with his lips moving.

“Was it worth a mark?” said Saikou.

Riarhin thought about it.

“She told me what it costs,” she said. “Both ways.”

“Ah.” He was quiet for a few steps. “That is generally more than one is sold for a mark.”

He did not ask what else Enid had said. She did not tell him. She went up the plank and stood at the rail and watched the house on the shoulder of the hill, where the smoke had begun at last to lean away south, until the ship came off the quay and turned and the hill went behind the point.

Part Two — The Crossing and the Undertaking

Chapter Six — As Far as the Crossing

Davra’s ship took five days over the Dolmis, and for the first three of them Riarhin behaved as though the thing had been settled.

She did not notice she was doing it. She had come down from Enid’s hill with a price in her head and a letter in her coat, and when she came aboard the company was there, all seven of them, in the waist and the cabin and the space between the water casks where Berent had been installed with his leg on a coil of rope. They were there in the morning when she woke and in the evening when she ate. They were there when she walked the round of the deck at night, which she had begun to do because there were no doors to check and she needed something to check. On the second day she found herself at the chart table with a pencil, working out how many of them could be lodged in one house in Valora and how many would need to be elsewhere, and writing the names in two lists.

She wrote seven names. She did not ask anybody.

The ship was not large. There was nowhere to be alone on it except the bows, and the bows were wet. By the second day everyone had found the place on deck that suited them, and a way of being in it that did not get in anybody else’s.

Berent’s was the coil of rope between the casks. He did not like it. He had wanted to be put by the rail, and Davra had said that a man who could not stand without a stick would not be put by her rail in a sea, and he had not argued with Davra, which Riarhin thought was the first sensible thing he had done since the causeway. Aurine came twice a day to look at the leg. She unwound the bandage and looked and smelled and wound it again, and made him bend the knee as far as it would go, which was not far, and then a little farther, and he swore at her in a steady undertone the whole time. She did not seem to mind.

“It’s knitting,” she said on the second morning. “Badly. You’ll limp.”

“I know I’ll limp.”

“You’ll limp less if you do what I tell you.”

“I’ve done what you told me.”

“You’ve done half.” She wound the bandage. “The other half is the part that hurts. You skip it.”

Berent looked at Riarhin, who was sitting on the next cask mending a strap.

“Tell her.”

“Tell her what?”

“That I’m doing it.”

“You’re not,” said Riarhin. “I watched you yesterday.”

He shut his mouth. Aurine gave Riarhin a look that was very nearly a smile and went away with her satchel.

Tavin’s place was wherever there was work that could be done sitting down. By the third day he had repaired the purser’s abacus, re-whipped the ends of four lines the bosun had been meaning to see to, and won eleven pence from the cook at a game Riarhin did not recognise and suspected he had invented. Penny had found the ship’s biscuit store and been banned from it. She sat on Tavin’s shoulder in disgrace, with her head under her wing, and every so often took it out to look at the biscuit store with enormous longing.

Cadan’s place was the bows, wet as they were. He stood there in the mornings with his sleeves pushed back and did a long slow sequence of movements that looked like nothing at all until the ship pitched and he did not. The crew watched him from the rigging. On the second morning one of them tried it and went over the rail to the waist, and Cadan helped him up and showed him where he had put his weight wrong, and after that there were three of them.

Amari and Saikou had the stern.

On the third evening Riarhin went aft with her bowl and found them there, sitting on the deck with their backs to the rail and their legs stretched out, not talking. Amari had the book open on his knee. Saikou had his hands in his sleeves and his eyes shut.

“Sit,” said Amari. “There’s room.”

She sat. The ship rolled gently under them. The sky ahead was going dark and there was a line of cloud along the south that might have been land.

“He’s asleep,” said Amari.

“I am not,” said Saikou, without opening his eyes.

“He was sick the whole of the first day,” Amari said to Riarhin, as though Saikou were not there. “He always is. The first day. Never after.”

“You’ve crossed with him before.”

“Many times.” Amari turned a page. “The first time he was sick for four days, and it wasn’t the sea.”

Saikou opened his eyes.

“The beach,” he said.

“The beach.”

Riarhin looked from one to the other. Amari kept his eyes on the book. His thumb held the corner of a page without turning it.

“I washed up on a beach in Dhahara,” said Saikou, after a moment, in his level voice. “After the business at Khemesh. I have written about it. It is in the preface to a book of mine that nobody reads. It says that I was alone and that I had a silver mirror I did not have when I went into the water.” He looked at the sky. “It does not say that I was not alone.”

“He wasn’t,” said Amari.

“No.”

“I found him at the tide line,” Amari said to the book. “He had the mirror in his hand and he wouldn’t let go of it. His fingers had gone white. I had to carry him up the beach with it held out in front of him like a lantern.” He turned a page. “I took him to a fisherman’s hut and the fisherman’s wife made him soup. He couldn’t keep it down. He kept asking her for salt. Not in the soup. On the doorstep.”

Saikou was quiet.

“I didn’t know what it was for,” said Amari. “I put it there anyway. She thought we were both mad.”

“You never told me that,” said Saikou.

“You never asked.” Amari looked up at last. “You wrote it down that you were alone. I thought, well, that’s his book. He can put in it what he likes.”

Saikou looked at him for a long time. Then he took one hand out of his sleeve and put it, briefly, on Amari’s shoulder, and took it back.

“I don’t remember the salt,” he said.

“No,” said Amari. “I didn’t think you would.”

Riarhin ate her supper and did not say anything. She thought of the gap in Saikou’s papers, the space the width of a page between the tenth sheet and the eleventh. She put her bowl down. The line of cloud to the south had not moved.

It was Nessa who told her.

It was the fourth morning. Riarhin had come up from the cabin with the sheet from the chart table, the two lists of names, meaning to ask Amari about money, and found Nessa sitting on the hatch cover in the sun with her shawl round her and her hair loose, singing under her breath to nobody.

Nessa looked at the sheet in her hand.

“That’s us,” she said.

“It’s where we’ll lodge. In Valora. I’ve been working out—”

“May I see?”

Riarhin gave it to her. Nessa read it. She read it carefully, both lists, with her lips moving a little over the names, and then she held it out again.

“I’m on it,” she said.

“Yes.”

“I didn’t say I was coming to Valora.”

Riarhin stood very still.

“I said I’d come as far as the crossing.” Nessa said it gently. “On the shingle at Scarrow. You were there. I said it was another leg, not a life, and I’d say when it was enough. You said that’s all I ask.” She looked up. “This is the crossing. We’re on it. When we get to the other side, I’ll decide.”

“I thought—”

Nessa held out the sheet. “You can ask me when we land.”

Riarhin looked at the sheet in her hand. Seven names in two lists. She had put Nessa in the second list, with Berent, because Berent would need somebody and Nessa was good with him.

“You haven’t asked any of them,” said Nessa. “Have you.”

“No.”

“Berent couldn’t walk,” said Nessa. “You might ask him what he’d have done if he could.”

The ship rose and fell. Somewhere forward Cadan’s three pupils were doing their slow movements in the bows, and one of them lost his footing and swore, and the others laughed.

“You’re right,” said Riarhin.

“I know.” Nessa took the sheet from her hand again, folded it in half, and gave it back. “Ask the others there too. Where we can walk away if we want to. Not here, where there’s nowhere to go but the water.”

She began to hum again. It was the door song. It went up and turned and came down, and it went all the way to the end.

That night Riarhin sat at the chart table with the sheet and a pencil for a long time. Then she drew a line through both lists. Underneath she wrote: Lodging at the landing. A week. For everyone. Passage home for anyone who wants it. She added up what that would cost. It was a great deal. It was a great deal of what she had meant to spend in Valora.

She looked at the sum. She did not cross it out.

In the morning there was land.

Chapter Seven — A Week at Fairport

Fairport had been burned in the war, and it had been built again, and the new part of it was the first thing anybody saw from the sea.

It stood along the waterfront in pale stone: a long quay with iron rings, a customs house with a clock, three warehouses with the queen’s mark cut over their doors, and behind them, climbing the hill, streets that had been laid out straight by somebody with a rule. The old town was still there beyond the new, crooked and dark and patched with scorched timber. It looked, from the deck, like a mouth with half its teeth replaced.

Davra put them ashore at the fish quay, which was the old one, and wished them luck in a tone that suggested she did not expect them to need it for long, and was gone on the next tide with her salt fish.

Riarhin found a house.

It belonged to a widow called Mother Harte who let rooms to ship’s officers between voyages, and it was in the old town, on a street that went down to the water in steps, and it had eight beds in five rooms and a kitchen with a range that smoked. Riarhin paid for a week. She paid in silver, in front of Amari, and when Mother Harte had gone to see about sheets she put the rest of the money on the kitchen table and counted it out into two heaps.

“That,” she said, touching the larger, “is for Valora. That—” the smaller— “is for anybody who wants to go home. Passage from here to wherever you came from. Or wherever you want to go instead. I’ve asked Amari to keep it separate and give it to whoever asks without asking them why.”

The company looked at the two heaps.

“Why a week?” said Tavin.

“Because that’s how long it takes me to find out what I don’t know about the road,” said Riarhin. “And because I want you all to have time to decide somewhere that isn’t a ship.” She looked at Nessa. Nessa looked back without expression. “I’ll tell you tonight what I mean to do. All of it. Then you can decide. Not before.”

The week was full of work. The deciding happened between one task and the next.

On Aurine’s orders, Berent walked three hundred paces each morning, to the end of the new quay and back. He returned grey and sweating, put his leg up in the kitchen, and would not speak for an hour. On the third morning Riarhin went with him. He did not want her. He said so. She walked beside him anyway, not touching him, and at the end of the quay where the iron rings were he stopped and put both hands on the stick and stood looking out to sea with his jaw set.

“I was quicker than you,” he said. “Once. At the ford. I was quicker than everybody.”

“I know.”

“I’m not going to be again.”

“No.”

He looked at her. She had not said it kindly. She had not meant to.

“You could have lied,” he said.

“You’d have known.”

He almost laughed. He turned round and began the walk back, and she walked beside him, and when he stumbled at the step by the customs house she did not catch him, and he did not fall.

Tavin had a repair.

It was the cart. They had bought it on the second day from a man in the old town who sold carts that had belonged to other men, and it had a cracked felloe on the near back wheel that the man had painted over with great skill. Tavin found the crack in the time it took the man to count the money, and did not say anything until the money was counted, and then said it, and the man said the price was the price. Tavin said a price for a cart was a price for a cart that could carry things. The man said it could carry things. Tavin said it could carry things about a mile.

It took most of an afternoon. Riarhin sat on the step of Mother Harte’s house and listened to it drift up the street. By the end the man had agreed to take the cart back to his yard and put in a new felloe for the cost of the wood, and Tavin had agreed to help him do it, and they were sitting together in the yard with a jug between them arguing about whether a man from Tavin’s country could tell good ash from bad by the smell. Penny sat on the cart and watched them with deep suspicion.

“You’ll want that cart,” Tavin said when he came back, “all the way to Valora and a bit after. I thought I’d best see to it myself.”

“After?”

He looked at her with his pleasant, empty, open face.

“There’s always an after,” he said. “In my experience. People forget to buy a cart for it.”

Amari wanted the route.

He said so on the first evening, at the kitchen table, before anybody else had said anything. He said he would listen to whatever Riarhin had to say about why, but that he had once run a pay-tent for an army that marched on a route somebody had drawn on a map without walking it, and he had spent a winter feeding eleven hundred men on half rations because of a river that was on the map in the wrong place, and he did not intend to listen to anybody’s why until he had seen their how.

So Riarhin showed him the how, or as much of it as she had.

She had half the road from Fairport to Valora in her head. The coast road south from Fairport met the great north road at a market town three days out, and from there she had ridden it once, in Ewen’s caravan, with Berent at the rear and Na’ar walking beside a mule talking to a frightened boy about wheels. She had all of that half: the posting houses, the fords, the place the road had been washed out and the other road that was being worked, the soldier at the well who had said we protect the men who do the useful part. She drew it for Amari on the back of a sail-maker’s bill, stage by stage, with the distances.

He looked at it.

“And the first three days?”

“I’ve never ridden them.”

“And the rest is a year old,” he said.

“More.”

“Then half of it’s wrong.”

“Yes.”

He looked up. “Which half?”

“I don’t know. That’s what the week’s for.” She put her finger on the map. “There’s a posting house here. There’s a man there who keeps a board, and he writes on it what the road is like, and he’s cross about it. If he’s still there I’ll know by Thursday.”

Amari folded the sail-maker’s bill and put it in his book.

“Thursday,” he said. “Then I’ll listen.”

She told them on the first night anyway, because she had said she would, and because she did not want any of them to decide on less than the whole of it.

They sat round Mother Harte’s kitchen table with the range smoking and the shutters closed. She told them she meant to go to Valora and kill the queen. She told them she meant to kill the Consort too, if he stood between, and that she thought he would. She told them she did not yet know how.

Then she told them about the amulet.

She had told some of them some of it before. She told them all of it now: the dead city, the brass case with the green glass, the road north in the pocket on the right side of her coat, the room in Valora where she had put it in the queen’s hands. She told them it had been on the queen’s throat every day since. She told them what she had seen: the luck, the grain, the bridge, the men who fell down stairs.

“I don’t know that it does anything,” she said. “I want that clear. Saikou doesn’t know either. He has a theory, and he’ll tell you it’s a theory. I have a feeling, and I’ll tell you it’s a feeling. I brought it to her. I think it has helped her. I think it may be helping her now. I can’t prove any of that.” She looked round the table. “You don’t need to believe any of that to see what she’s doing.”

Nobody spoke.

“There’s a man in the old town,” said Aurine, “who came in on a coaster from the Acasian side on Monday. He was in the market this morning selling a cart. His own, I think. He had four children on it.”

Riarhin waited.

“He was from the hills behind Serrat,” said Aurine. “The upper Tarne. He said they came through in the first frosts. Soldiers. With a list. Every village inside a line that hadn’t sent its headman to swear.” She turned her cup. “They burned the stores and the roofs and the standing corn and they drove the people down the roads to the towns. He said it was very orderly. He said there were wagons for the old people.”

Tavin had stopped moving.

“He said,” Aurine went on, in the same even voice, “that the officer read them an order first. He said it was a very well drafted order. It said it was a kindness. That a war which went on for three years would kill more of them than one that ended in one.” She looked up. “He said he didn’t think the officer believed it. He said he thought the queen did.”

The range ticked. Somewhere outside a door banged in the wind.

“That’s what she’s doing,” said Riarhin.

After that there were questions.

Aurine asked hers at once, before the silence had quite finished. She asked whether she was being hired for a particular task, a named one, with a beginning and an end, or whether she was being asked to come along and do whatever killing seemed to be needed each time the plan changed.

“Because I’ve done both,” she said. “And I know which I’ll do well. And I know which I’ll do anyway.”

“A particular task,” said Riarhin.

“Which?”

“I don’t know yet.”

Aurine considered her.

“Then I’ll wait till you do,” she said, “before I say.”

Cadan did not ask anything. He sat at the end of the table with his hands in his sleeves and his head a little on one side, listening to all of it with courteous attention, and when the others had gone up to their beds he was still there.

Riarhin was banking the range. She felt him waiting behind her. She finished the range and turned round.

“Whom,” said Cadan, “do you intend me to kill?”

It was very quiet. He said it the way another man might have asked what time they would leave in the morning.

“I don’t know yet.”

“I understand.” He did not move. “You will know. Before my part begins.”

“Yes.”

“You will tell me. Exactly.”

“Yes.”

“And what I am not to do.”

“Yes.”

He looked at her for a while longer, in the light from the banked range, with his pleasant face and his patient eyes. She did not look away. She kept her hands still at her sides, and he saw her do it, and smiled with great kindness.

“Then I will wait,” he said. “Goodnight.”

He went up. She stood in the kitchen until she heard his door close, and then she went out and walked the round: the street door, the yard door, the cart in Mother Harte’s yard with its painted crack, the stair. She did it twice.

Chapter Eight — Three Questions

The call-up came to Fairport on the Tuesday, and it took Tavin before noon.

Riarhin heard the drum first. She was in the chandler’s on the fish quay buying tallow for the cart’s axle, because Tavin had said the man with the painted felloe would sell them his grease as well if they let him, and it would be the wrong grease. The drum came up the quay from the customs house end, a slow flat beat with a space in it, and the chandler put down the scoop and said a word she did not know and went to shut his door.

“What is it?”

“Muster.” He did not look at her. He was sliding the bolt. “The spring one. They cry it at Midwinter in the country. In the towns they come early, before the men go off on the boats.” He went back behind his counter. “You’ll want to get your lads indoors if they’ve no master. That’s all I’m saying. I’m saying it because you paid for the tallow.”

Riarhin left a penny beside the scales. The chandler pushed it back with one finger, then pointed her towards the yard door. She took the tallow and went out by the back.

The counting field was behind the customs house, where the old town met the new. It had been a rope-yard before the burning. Now it was a long strip of beaten ground with a wall on three sides and the queen’s mark cut over the gate, and there were wagons drawn up along one wall with their shafts on the ground, and a table at the far end under an awning, and at the table a clerk with a book.

Beside the table stood a serjeant.

He was not young. He had a bowman’s shoulders gone thick with age, and a face like a winter apple, and he was drinking cider out of a leather jack while he watched the gate. There was a pole driven into the ground beside him with notches cut in it at the height of a man’s chin, and beside the pole a boy of about fourteen was standing with his back against the wood while the clerk looked at him.

“Under,” said the clerk.

“He’s under,” said the serjeant. “Go home. Tell your mother to feed you.”

The boy went. He did not look glad. He looked, Riarhin thought, like somebody who had been told in front of everybody that he was not tall enough to be sent away.

There were perhaps thirty men in the field. Some of them had come of their own will; she could tell which by the way they stood, a little apart from the rest, with their arms folded. Some had been brought by the two soldiers at the gate, who went out into the streets with the drum and came back with whoever had not got indoors in time. Those stood in a knot by the wagons with their hands empty and their faces careful.

Tavin was among them.

He sat on a wagon shaft with Penny on his shoulder, explaining something to the man next to him with both hands, and the man next to him was laughing in spite of himself. When he saw Riarhin at the gate he lifted one hand and waved, cheerfully, as though she had come to meet him at a fair.

She did not go in at once. She stood by the gate with the tallow under her arm and watched the table.

The clerk had a way of doing it. He called a man. The man came to the pole and stood against it. If he was under the notch he went home. If he was over, the serjeant asked him three questions, and the clerk wrote the answers in the book.

Whose man are you?

What parish?

What trade?

The serjeant sent for the masters. A master’s mine sent a man home. Smiths, wheelwrights and farriers went into the book under their trades; those with neither master nor trade under their parishes. These men were told where to report at the thaw.

A man who would not answer was written in the book under the question he had refused.

Riarhin watched it happen twice. The first was a sailor, off a Linn boat, who said he was the ship’s and the ship was the master’s, and when the soldier went to the quay the boat had gone out on the morning tide, and the sailor stood in the field with his mouth open while the clerk wrote him down. The second was an old man who might have been fifty, who stood against the pole and was over it, and said he was nobody’s and from nowhere and had no trade but carrying, and when the serjeant asked him the third time he said carrying again, louder, and the clerk wrote carrier and the serjeant looked at him for a long while and then said, go home, father, and don’t be so proud next time, it’s a poor trade for a proud man.

The old man went. The clerk drew a line through what he had written.

Riarhin found that she was holding the tallow very hard.

They called Tavin third after that.

He got down off the wagon shaft and went to the pole with Penny on his shoulder, and stood against it, and was over the notch by the width of a hand, and looked sorry about it.

“Whose man are you?” said the serjeant.

“My own,” said Tavin. “Mostly. On good days.”

“That’s not an answer.”

“It’s the true one. I can give you a better one if you’d like, but you’d have to send somebody to check it, and they’d come back and tell you it was a lie, and then we’d both be sorry.” Tavin smiled his pleasant, open smile. “I thought I’d save your man the walk.”

The serjeant drank some of his cider.

“Parish?”

“I was born under a cart,” said Tavin. “Between two parishes. My mother always said it was the left-hand one and my father the right. They argued about it till he died. I’ve never liked to take sides.”

The clerk had stopped writing. The serjeant had not stopped looking at Tavin. He had a bowman’s eyes, pale and patient, that had spent a long time looking at a thing a long way off until it came close enough.

“Trade?”

Tavin opened his mouth.

He shut it again.

Riarhin saw him do it. She had never seen Tavin not have a word ready. She had seen him lie to a ferryman, to a cook, to the man with the painted felloe, to Davra, and to her; she had seen him tell the truth in a way that sounded like a lie so that nobody would believe it. She had never seen him stand in front of a question with nothing to put in front of it.

The magpie on his shoulder put her head on one side and looked at the serjeant.

“Well?” said the serjeant.

“I’m thinking,” said Tavin.

“Think faster.”

He did not. He stood by the pole with the whole field watching him and his hands open at his sides, as though he had put something down and could not remember where.

The clerk dipped his pen. He looked at the serjeant.

“Refused,” he said. “The third.”

“He’s not refused.”

It was not Riarhin who said it.

Berent had come in at the gate behind her. She had not heard him; she had been listening to the field. He was grey from the half of his walk he had done along the new quay and leaning hard on the stick, and he came past her into the counting field at the pace his leg allowed, which was slow, and every man in the field watched him do it.

The serjeant watched him too.

“And who are you?”

Berent did not answer that. He came up to the table and stopped, and put both hands on the stick, and looked at the clerk’s book upside down.

“That’s the Midwinter writ,” he said. “The spring muster. Under the old Dawn rules. I know the hand.” He did not look up from the book. “Clause five. A man in transit, lodged under a householder’s roof, with passage bought, is the householder’s until his passage sails. He’s not the parish’s. He’s not liable to the call.” He looked up then. “You know clause five. You’ve used it. You used it on the sailor off the Linn boat, and his boat had gone, so it didn’t help him. This one’s onward passage is by cart. He’s lodged with Mother Harte on the Steps till it leaves. You can send a man to ask her.”

The serjeant set down his cider.

For a long moment nobody in the field said anything. The drum at the gate had stopped. Riarhin could hear the gulls on the customs house roof and the sound of the wagons’ harness as the horses shifted.

“You were a soldier,” said the serjeant.

“Once.”

“Where?”

Berent said nothing.

The serjeant looked at his stick, and his leg, and the way he stood on it. Then he looked at his face, for a long time, with his pale bowman’s eyes.

Riarhin did not move. The Queen’s men had been in the Mound’s yard the night Mara was taken out, their torches above the cart carrying a knight with a bolt in his leg. She could not place this serjeant among them. That left every other road Berent had travelled.

“Send to Mother Harte,” said the serjeant to the soldier at his elbow.

Mother Harte came herself.

She came down the Steps and along the quay and in at the gate of the counting field in her apron, with her sleeves rolled and flour on her forearms, and a face like a closed door, and she stood in front of the table and looked at the serjeant with an expression Riarhin had last seen on Elspeth in the doorway of the hall at Glenruan.

“You’ve taken my lodger,” she said.

“He’s been called.”

“He’s been called off my step. He’s under my roof till Saturday and after that he can be the Queen’s for all I care, and welcome to him.” She looked at Tavin with no warmth at all. “He’s eaten three of my eggs that weren’t his. He owes me for a pane in the yard door the bird went through. I want him where I can get it out of him.”

“You answer for him?”

Mother Harte hesitated.

Riarhin saw it. It was very small. The clerk’s pen waited over the book.

“Till Saturday,” said Mother Harte. “I answer for him till Saturday. After that I don’t know him.”

The serjeant looked at her. Then he looked at the clerk.

“Strike it,” he said.

The clerk drew a line through refused.

Tavin bowed to the serjeant. He did it very well, a deep, courtly, absurd bow with one hand on his heart and the magpie clinging to his shoulder and flapping to keep her footing, and the men by the wagons laughed, and the serjeant did not.

“Get out of my field,” said the serjeant. “And you.” He looked at Berent. “You know clause five. You know it better than my clerk. That’s a thing I’ll remember.”

“I’d sooner you didn’t,” said Berent.

“I know you would,” said the serjeant, and picked up his cider.

Mother Harte walked back up the Steps ahead of them without speaking. At the house door she stopped and turned round and looked at Riarhin, not at Tavin, and said, “Saturday,” once, in a flat voice, and went in and shut the kitchen door behind her.

Tavin stood on the step with Penny on his shoulder and watched the door.

“I owe her for a pane,” he said. “That part was true. I was going to tell her.”

“Tell her now,” said Riarhin.

He looked at her. Then he went in. Through the door she heard his voice, and Mother Harte’s, sharp, and then his again, lower, going on for some time. When he came out he had flour on his sleeve and the magpie had been shut in the yard.

“She wanted to know why I didn’t have a trade,” he said. “I said I did, only it wasn’t one you could put in a book. She said that was the worst answer she’d ever heard and she’d heard a great many. She said I was to mend the pane myself.” He looked at the flour on his sleeve. “She gave me a bun. I don’t know what that means.”

“It means she answered for you.”

“Till Saturday.”

“She didn’t have to say it at all.”

Tavin was quiet. It was the second time that morning Riarhin had seen him quiet, and she did not like it any better than the first.

She walked Berent back along the new quay in the afternoon, because he had not finished his three hundred paces that morning and would not let the day go without them.

He did not talk for the first hundred. He did the walk the way he always did it, with his jaw set and his eyes on the iron rings at the far end, as though the rings had insulted him. At the end he stopped and put both hands on the stick and stood looking out at the water, where a Linn boat was coming in under a brown sail, low and slow.

“That was the sailor’s,” he said. “Come back for him.”

“Too late.”

“Too late.” He watched it. “He’ll be written down at Midwinter in some parish that isn’t his. They’ll send him inland to dig. He’ll never have seen a field.” He shifted his weight. “Clause five’s a good clause. It was written for men like him. It was written by somebody who’d been a sailor and been taken off a quay.”

“You know it well.”

“Elyas made us learn the writs,” said Berent. “All of us, in his household. He said a knight who didn’t know the law he was sworn to carry out was a man with a sword and an opinion. He said there were enough of those.” He was quiet. “He used to test us at supper. The whole Dawn code. Clause by clause. If you got one wrong you poured for the table.”

Riarhin did not say anything. The Linn boat came on across the harbour.

“He asked me where I’d served,” said Berent. “The serjeant.”

“I heard.”

“I nearly told him.” Berent did not look at her. “Not because I wanted to. Because it was a question and I know the answer, and when a man with a book asks you a question you know the answer to, you give it. That’s what they teach you. It’s all they teach you.” He moved his hand on the stick. “I stood there and I had the answer in my mouth. In the household of the Lord Elyas. And then I thought of the book, and who reads it.”

“You didn’t say it.”

“No.”

“That was the right thing.”

“It didn’t feel like the right thing.” He looked at her then. “It felt like I’d let go of something. Standing there. I’ve said that name in front of every table I’ve ever stood at. I said it in Tavish’s hall with the rain coming down. I said it to you.” He stopped. “I didn’t say it to a serjeant with a jack of cider in a rope-yard in Fairport. And I’m glad I didn’t, and I don’t like that I’m glad.”

They stood for a while at the end of the quay.
