It was cold. The wind had come round to the east again and there was rain in it, a long way off, over the sea. Riarhin had her coat buttoned to the throat and her hands in her sleeves, and she could feel the letter in the right-hand pocket, and under it the empty place where once there had been a small hard shape.

She had been meaning to ask him since the hatch cover of Davra’s ship. She had not found the time. She had found, if she was honest, a great many other things to do instead.

“Nessa said a thing,” she said. “On the ship.”

“Nessa says a great many things.”

“She said you got in the boat because you couldn’t walk.” Riarhin kept her eyes on the Linn boat. “She said I might ask you what you’d have done if you could.”

Berent was silent for a long time.

“Gone to Valora,” he said at last. “The day I heard she was taken. Alone.”

“And?”

“And got in somehow. Some stupid way. Over a wall.” He almost smiled. “I’d have got to the steward’s wing, maybe. Maybe. I’d have got to the foot of the stair and there’d have been two men on it, and I’d have fought them, because that’s the only idea I’d have had, and one of them would have got a shout off.” He looked at his leg. “And I’d be dead by now. With my name on a board at the west gate.”

Riarhin said nothing.

“That’s what I’d have done,” said Berent. “If I could walk. That’s what I’d still do, some days. I lie on that coil of rope, or on Mother Harte’s bed, or wherever you’ve put me, and I think it, and I get as far as the stair every time, and then I stop thinking because I know what comes after.” He lifted the stick an inch off the stones and set it down again. “The leg stopped me. That’s all. It wasn’t sense. I’d like to say it was sense.”

“And now?”

He looked at her.

“Now I’ve a stick,” he said, “and seven people who got in a boat, and one who asked.” He looked out at the water. “I’d rather the seven. I’d rather the seven and the stick than the wall and the stair and the west gate. I didn’t think I would. I thought I’d hate it. Going slow, with people.” He was quiet. “I do hate it. I’d still rather.”

“Then you’ll come.”

“I was always coming. You knew that. Nessa knew it.” He turned from the rail and began the walk back. “You asked, though.”

She walked beside him. At the step by the customs house he did not stumble.

The rain came in the evening, and with it the serjeant.

He came to the door of Mother Harte’s house on the Steps at dusk, alone, without the drum, in a wet cloak with a lantern, and knocked, and asked for the man with the stick. Mother Harte stood in the door with her arms folded and said he could ask for whoever he liked on her step and he would get whatever she decided to give him, and that it was raining. The serjeant said he knew it was raining, and that he had not come about the call.

Riarhin was on the stair. She came down.

“He’s asleep,” she said. “What do you want?”

The serjeant looked at her. His winter-apple face was wet and the lantern made it hollow under the eyes.

“To tell him a thing,” he said. “You’ll do. You were at the gate this morning with a lump of tallow.” He did not wait for her to agree. “There’s a man comes through Fairport at the turn of every season. From Valora. From the household. With a list. He looks at our books, and he asks about anybody the clerk couldn’t place, and he takes away what he wants.” He turned the lantern a little in his hand. “He’ll be here the week after Midwinter. He’ll ask about the ones who wouldn’t say. He always does.”

Riarhin waited.

“Your man with the bird’s been struck,” said the serjeant. “There’s a line through him. A line’s a thing that was written and then wasn’t. He’ll ask about lines too.” He paused. “Your man with the stick wasn’t written at all. I didn’t ask him his name. I noticed I didn’t ask. I thought I’d best tell somebody I noticed, before the man from Valora comes and asks me what I noticed.”

“Why?”

The serjeant was quiet for a moment, with the rain coming down between them.

“I stood a watch on the Belworth,” he said. “Before the war. Under the old Dawn rules. There was a knight from the Lord Elyas’s household who made us learn clause five.” He looked at her steadily. “I don’t know that man in there. I didn’t see his face. It was raining.”

He went back down the Steps with the lantern going small in the wet. Riarhin stood in the doorway until Mother Harte said, from behind her, that she was letting the heat out, and she shut the door.

Tavin was in the yard, mending the pane.

He had a candle in a jar and a square of new glass and a pot of putty that smelled of linseed, and he was working with his back to the kitchen window and his sleeves pushed up, whistling under his breath. Penny sat on the water butt and watched him with her head on one side. The rain had eased to a drizzle and the yard smelled of wet stone and the sea.

Riarhin stood in the doorway and watched him for a while.

“You told the serjeant I was your man,” said Tavin without turning round.

“I didn’t. Berent told him about clause five. Mother Harte told him the rest.”

“You were going to.” He pressed the putty along the edge of the glass with his thumb. “I saw your face at the gate. You were going to walk into that field and say he’s mine, and then he’d have asked you whose you were, and you’d have told him, and it’d have been in the book.” He smoothed the putty. “You weren’t going to think about it first. You were just going to do it.”

She did not answer. It was true.

“I’ve been nobody’s man since I was nine,” said Tavin. “It’s the one thing I’ve managed to keep. Everything else I’ve sold at some point, or lost at cards, or given to a woman who asked nicely. Not that.” He wiped his thumb on his breeches. “And I stood by that pole this morning and a man asked me my trade, and I found I didn’t have one I could say out loud. Not one. I’ve been doing it for twenty years and I couldn’t say it.”

“You don’t have to.”

“I know I don’t have to. That’s the thing.” He turned round. His face in the candlelight was tired. “I got in the boat because it was the next thing. I like the next thing. It’s the only thing I’ve ever liked all the way through, the next thing, because by the time it’s over there’s another one and nobody’s asked me what I was doing in the first one.” He looked at the pane. “And now there’s a queen. And after the queen there’s nothing. You know that. I know that. Nobody goes on to the next thing after a queen.”

Riarhin came out into the yard. The drizzle was cold on her face.

“Then why come?”

Tavin did not answer at once. Penny hopped from the water butt to the sill beside him and looked at the putty with interest, and he moved the pot away from her without looking.

“Because if I leave you to it, I’ll spend the next six months hearing how you should have done it from men who never got near the door.” He trimmed a ridge of putty. “And I’ll know they’re wrong, and I’ll have to buy them another drink to hear the end.”

“Is that why?”

He kept working at the ridge until she thought he would cut it all away.

“I’d like to be there,” he said. “When it’s done.”

“Then stay,” said Riarhin.

He put the knife down on the sill.

He looked at her for a moment longer, and smiled.

“One thing,” he said.

“What?”

“Don’t give me instructions. Not like you’re going to give the monk.” He picked up the putty knife. “Give me a problem. Tell me what has to not happen, and leave me to see to it. I’m no good with instructions. I get halfway through and see a better way and then I’m arguing with myself instead of doing the thing.” He turned back to the pane. “Give me a problem, and I’ll give you a man who’s solved it.”

“All right.”

“And I’ll want the cart after.”

“You’ll have the cart.”

“Good,” said Tavin, and went on smoothing the putty, and began to whistle again, and Riarhin went back inside out of the rain and found Nessa sitting on the stair with her shawl round her, listening, with her eyes shut.

“Two,” said Nessa, without opening them.

“Two what?”

“Two who’ve said.” She opened her eyes. “You asked them. I heard you ask them both. It’s going better than the ship.”

“You were listening.”

“It’s a small house.” Nessa pulled the shawl closer and stood up. “I’ll say mine when I’m ready. Not tonight. I want to hear what the carrier says on Thursday. I want to see whether you can send a letter north without getting anybody killed.” She went up a step and stopped. “If you can’t, I’ll still come. I only want to know whether you can.”

She went up. Riarhin stood at the foot of the stair in Mother Harte’s narrow hall, with the smell of linseed coming in from the yard and Tavin whistling, and the rain on the door, and thought about the serjeant’s pale eyes, and a man who would come the week after Midwinter with a list and ask about lines.

Chapter Nine — What the Carrier Would Take

The coaster to the north went on Saturday, and its master would not take the list.

His name was Hollis. He ran a two-masted boat between Fairport and the Ubral harbours with wool one way and salt the other, and letters both ways in a canvas bag that hung on a nail in his cabin, and he had been recommended to Riarhin by Mother Harte, who said he was honest and slow and had never lost a letter in twenty years except once to a goat. Riarhin found him on Thursday on the fish quay, supervising the loading of salt and eating an apple.

She gave him the letter to Ailsa first. He weighed it in his hand, named a price, and put it in the bag.

Then she gave him the other.

It was two sheets folded together. The first was to Tavish. She had written it three times, in the kitchen at Mother Harte’s, at night, with the range ticking, and the third time she had made it plain enough to be read by a man who did not like reading and dull enough to be read by a stranger who opened it on the way and learned nothing he could use. It said that the court in Valora knew where Berent had gone the year before, and whose door he had come to. It said that she had reason to think somebody in Valora might have cause to ask about that again before the spring, and to ask harder. It said that she could not say why in a letter. It said that he should decide for himself and his house what to do about it, and that she was sorry.

The second sheet was the list.

She had written it after the letter, late, when she could not sleep. It was the names of the people she thought might be asked. Tavish and Elspeth and Iona. Mairi, whose workroom Berent had slept in. Donnel and his brother at the crossing, because the knight had crossed there. Ewen the carrier, because he had taken them south. The wool buyer who had brought the proclamation. And Ailsa, at the room over the chandler’s at Kilrenny, by the harbour, with a window that looked at the boats.

It was a list of everybody who should be warned. She had written it so that Tavish could warn them. She had written it because she could not bear to leave anybody off.

Hollis took the two sheets and began to fold them into the bag, and then stopped, and unfolded the second one, and looked at it.

He did not read it. He looked at the shape of it: names one under another, a place beside each.

He folded it again and held it out to her.

“Not that one,” he said.

“It’s sealed with the other.”

“It’s not sealed. It’s folded.” He did not lower his hand. “I’ll take a letter. I’ll take a letter to anybody at any address you like, sealed, and I won’t look in it, and if somebody asks me what’s in it I’ll say a letter. I won’t take a list of names and houses.” He looked at her steadily. “I’ve a sister at Kilrenny. I’ve a brother-in-law at the Glen Tern crossing. I’ve been up and down that coast twenty years. If somebody stops my boat and opens my bag and finds a list of Ubral houses in a hand from Valora, I don’t know what that list is for and I don’t want to, and I don’t want to be the man who carried it.”

“It’s a warning.”

“Maybe it is.” He pushed it gently towards her. “To the man who opens my bag, it’s a map.”

Riarhin took it back.

She stood on the fish quay with the salt going aboard behind Hollis and the second sheet in her hand. She could see it as he had seen it. Not names: a route. A list of doors along the road the knight had taken, written out neatly by somebody who knew exactly who lived behind each one, and sent north in a sealed bag the season before men came from Valora asking.

“Take the letter,” she said. “Just the letter.”

“Glad to.” He put it in the bag. “To Tavish Rocke. Care of?”

She had not decided. She decided now.

“The posting house on the lower road below Glenruan. The keeper there knows him. He buys wool.”

“I know it.” Hollis wrote it on the outside in a large careful hand. “Saturday. Kilrenny in six days if the wind holds, Glenruan three after that by the carter.” He looked at her. “He’ll have it before the snow.”

She tore the second sheet into four on the walk back up the steps, and in Mother Harte’s kitchen she put the pieces into the range.

They did not burn at once. The range was banked low for the afternoon. The paper curled at the edges and went brown and lay there, and she could see a name on one of the pieces, Mairi, and on another the word harbour, and she stood and watched them until the brown went black and the names were gone.

When she turned round Saikou was in the doorway.

He had come in from the yard. He had Penny on his wrist, which meant Tavin had given her to him to hold, which Tavin did when he wanted to do something with both hands and did not want the magpie to see what. Saikou looked at Riarhin, and then at the range, and then at the black curl of paper on the coals, and then at her again.

He did not ask what it was. He stood in the doorway with the bird on his wrist until she had put the kettle over, and then he said, “Tavin has found somebody who will sell him a lock,” and went back out into the yard.

The carter from the south came in on Thursday evening with a load of barrels for the customs house, and Riarhin was waiting for him.

He had come up the coast road from the market town where it met the great road, and before that he had come up the great road from two stages south of it, and he knew the posting house with the board. The keeper was still there. He was still cross. The road had been finished past the place where it had washed out, and there was a new stone bridge at the ford where Ewen’s wheel had cracked, and the queen’s company that had worked the road was gone south and there was a new company in the posting house yard that nobody liked, because they did not pay for their water.

“They used to pay?”

“The old lot paid. For everything. Water, feed, the lot. Very particular. Their captain had it in the orders.” The carter spat. “This lot say the new orders don’t say. They say the queen’s at war and the posting house is a queen’s posting house and the water’s the queen’s water. The keeper’s written to somebody. He’s always writing to somebody.”

Riarhin wrote it down. She went through the whole of the road with him, stage by stage, while he ate Mother Harte’s bread and drank Mother Harte’s beer and was paid for his time. When he had gone she went up to the room Amari shared with Saikou and knocked.

Amari opened the door with the book in his hand.

She gave him the new map. It was the old map with half of it crossed out and written over. He read it standing in the doorway, by the light of the stair lamp, all of it, twice. He asked her three questions about the company in the posting house yard. He asked her how long she thought it would take, with the cart and Berent’s leg, and she told him, and he said that was a day too short, and she said she had allowed a day for Berent’s leg, and he said then it was a day too short for the cart.

Then he closed the book on the map and stood with it under his arm.

“I’ll come,” he said.

She had not asked him yet. She had meant to ask him tomorrow, properly, with the others.

“You said you’d listen Thursday.”

“I listened.” Amari did not move from the doorway. “I’ll come as far as Valora and I’ll keep the book, and I’ll want three things. I want to be told when the plan changes. Not after. When. I don’t care if I don’t like the change. I want to know it. Second, Saikou’s reserve stays where it is. Nobody touches the After for the killing. Not you, not him, not me.”

“Agreed.”

“Third.” He looked past her down the stair, where Saikou’s voice could be heard in the yard, saying something to the magpie. “Wherever the plan puts him, it doesn’t put him alone.”

Riarhin looked at him.

“He’ll not thank me for asking,” said Amari. “He’ll say he can see to himself. He can. He’s very good at it. He’s been seeing to himself since the beach.” He shifted the book. “It’s not the seeing to himself I worry about. It’s that he forgets to write down who was there.”

“I can’t promise that,” said Riarhin. “I don’t know yet where the plan puts anybody.”

“I know you can’t.” Amari nodded, as though she had said the thing he had wanted her to say. “I’m telling you so you know I asked. When you know, you’ll remember.”

She would. She could not help it.

“Goodnight,” said Amari, and shut the door.

That night Saikou did not cover the mirror.

Riarhin saw it because she walked the round, and the round took her past the door of the room he shared with Amari, and the door was ajar because the room was small and the night was close. Amari was asleep, with his back to the wall and the book under his pillow. Saikou sat on the other bed with the little round mirror in its plain silver frame on his knee, uncovered, and his face bent over it, quite still. The glass had fogged. She could see that from the door. It had gone grey from the rim inwards, as a window does when somebody breathes on it from the other side.

He did not look up. She did not stop.

In the morning he was at the kitchen table with his papers squared, and the mirror was in his satchel with the cloth round it, and when she asked him whether he had slept he said that he had slept very well, thank you, and that the magpie had stolen one of his pens.

Nessa came down last. She stood in the kitchen doorway with her shawl round her and her white streaks bright in the morning light, and looked at the table, and at the two heaps of money that Amari still kept separate on the dresser, and at Riarhin.

“As far as Valora,” she said. “It’s another leg.”

“That’s all I ask.”

“I know.” Nessa smiled. “You asked this time.”

Part Three — The Country That Works

Chapter Ten — The Country That Works

The road south of Fairport had been mended by people who expected to be paid for it, and had been.

Riarhin saw it on the first morning. The coast road ran along the top of a low cliff with the sea on one hand and fields on the other, and every half mile there was a stone at the verge with a number cut in it and a mark beneath, the same mark as over the warehouse doors at Fairport. The ditches had been cleared. Where the road crossed a stream there was a new culvert with the date cut in its lintel. Where it had once gone round a bog it now went across on a causeway of packed stone, and at the near end of the causeway a man sat on a stool beside a hut with a slate, and wrote down the carts.

“Toll?” said Tavin, driving.

“No toll,” said the man. “Count.”

“What for?”

“For the road.” He wrote them down: one cart, one horse, eight persons. “They want to know how much it’s used. So they know what to mend first.” He looked at Berent in the back of the cart with his leg on a sack. “He all right?”

“He’s mending too,” said Tavin.

The man laughed and waved them on.

It was like that all the way to the market town.

It was like that in a way she had not expected and did not know what to do with. The posting houses had boards outside with the stages written on them and the price of a bed. The inns had new roofs. At the second night’s stop there was a recruiting sergeant in the inn yard with a table and a book, and a queue of young men in front of him that went out through the gate into the road, and at the head of the queue a woman was arguing with the sergeant about her son’s age and winning. At the third there was a company of the queen’s foot billeted in the barns, and they had paid for the billet, and the innkeeper’s wife said so to Riarhin while she was serving the soup, unprompted, with a kind of defiant pride, as though Riarhin had accused her of something.

“They pay on the day,” she said. “My sister’s boy is with them. He sends it home.”

“I’m glad,” said Riarhin.

“You don’t look glad.”

“I’m tired.”

The woman looked at her, and at Remorse on the back of her chair, and at Cadan at the end of the table eating his soup with small neat movements, and at Berent’s stick.

“You’re not from the army,” she said.

“No.”

“Then what are you from?”

“The north,” said Riarhin, which was true, and the woman went away satisfied with it.

That night Riarhin lay awake in the inn’s loft with the company breathing round her and thought about the woman’s sister’s boy. She thought about the road, and the man with the slate, and the new culvert with the date cut in it. She thought about the four hundred villages in the upper Tarne and the man in the Fairport market with his four children on his cart. They were the same thing. They were the same queen doing the same work with the same care. The care that mended the culvert had drawn the line from the Tarne to the pass. The wages that came on the day to the woman’s sister’s boy were the wages of the men who had burned the standing corn.

Below the loft, one of the billeted soldiers began coughing. A door opened, and the innkeeper’s wife told him where to find hot water. Riarhin heard him thank her.

Berent read the soldiers.

He did it without being asked. At every stop where there were soldiers he found a place to sit with his leg up where he could see them, and he watched, and in the evening, when the company was together, he said what he had seen. He said it quietly, without any of the heat he brought to his leg, as a man reports the weather.

“The captain at the barns is new,” he said on the third night. “He doesn’t know their names yet. He says you there. They don’t like it but they’ll do what he says, because the sergeant does what he says, and the sergeant’s old.” He moved his leg on the sack. “If it came to anything, they’d follow the sergeant.”

“And the recruiting sergeant?” said Amari.

“Honest. Tired. He’s been sitting at that table since harvest. He turned away two boys because they were too young and he knew their mothers.” Berent looked at the fire. “He’d not have done that, a year ago. They’d have taken anybody a year ago.”

“How do you know what they’d have done a year ago?”

“Because I was there a year ago,” said Berent. “On the road south. With Riarhin and Na’ar.”

Amari was quiet. Then he said, “You were at the rear of a carrier’s train with your mind on a man in a prison. You saw two posting houses and a well.”

Berent went red.

“I saw enough.”

“You saw what you saw. It was a lot, for a man in a fever.” Amari did not raise his voice. “I’m only saying. Tell us what you saw today. Don’t tell us what it means about last year. You’ll be right more often.”

Riarhin waited for Berent to lose his temper. She could see it coming up in him. It came up, and stayed a moment, and went down again.

“The captain at the barns,” said Berent, “is performing. He stands in the yard with his hands behind his back and looks at the road as though he’s expecting somebody important. He’s not. He’s expecting nobody. He does it because he thinks that’s what a captain does.” He looked at Amari. “That’s what I saw today.”

“That’s good,” said Amari.

“I know it’s good.”

“I know you know,” said Amari, and grinned, and after a moment Berent grinned too, and Tavin said that if they were going to be pleased with each other he would go and see to the horse.

The posting house with the board was two days past the market town, on the great road, and the keeper was still there and still cross.

The company in his yard were not paying for their water. They were not paying for anything. They were a new company, raised in the summer, and their captain was a young man with good boots and a fresh commission who had read in his orders that the posting houses on the great road were the queen’s, and had concluded that everything in them was the queen’s too. The keeper had written to Valora twice. He showed Riarhin the letters. He showed her the board, where he had written, under the stages and the price of a bed, in chalk, WATER 1d THE BUCKET. SOLDIERS ALSO, and somebody had rubbed out ALSO and he had written it in again.

Riarhin stood in the yard with the keeper and watched a soldier come to the well with two buckets, fill them, and walk away without looking at the board.

“The old company paid,” said the keeper. “Every bucket. Their captain had it in the orders. Very particular man. Name of Garnier. You’d hear him in the yard: what does the board say, lad, read it to me.” He spat. “This one can’t read his own name. Or won’t.”

“Have you told him?”

“I’ve told him. He says it’s wartime.” The keeper looked at the soldier going back to the barns with the water slopping. “It isn’t wartime here. It’s wartime three hundred miles off. Here it’s a well.”

Berent had been listening from the cart. He looked at the young captain, who was standing at the barn door with his hands behind his back.

“He wants somebody to tell him he’s wrong,” said Berent. “Somebody with a better commission than his. He won’t take it from a posting-house keeper. He’d take it from a colonel.”

“There isn’t a colonel,” said the keeper.

“There will be.” Berent shifted his leg. “There always is. He’s writing to somebody too, I’d bet. Asking whether he’s right. He’s the sort who asks after.”

Riarhin looked at him. It was, she thought, exactly true, and it was a thing Elyas would have seen, and Elyas would have gone across the yard and talked to the young man and made him see it himself, and been thanked for it, and remembered.

She did not go across the yard. She paid the keeper for their water, for eight persons and one horse, at a penny the bucket, and he took it and looked at it in his palm and said it was the first honest coin he had had at the well in a month.

The news about the queen came on the ninth day, from a herald.

He came up the great road from the south at a canter with a pennon on his lance and two riders behind him, and he stopped at the posting house at the next stage long enough to change horses and read a proclamation from the step. The company were eating in the yard. The herald did not look at them. He looked at the paper and read it in a high carrying voice that had been trained for larger places, and it said that Her Majesty the Queen, Protector of Vhasia, would hold her Midwinter council at Valora, and that the lords and officers named in the attached schedule were to attend upon her there by the eve of Midwinter, and that the court would keep the feast in the capital.

“She’s coming home,” said Tavin, when the herald had gone.

“For the winter,” said Saikou. “To plan the spring.”

Riarhin sat on the bench in the posting-house yard with her bowl going cold in her hands.

She had not known where the queen would be. She had come south knowing only that Veralyn had been at Serrat in the autumn, in a granary, with maps on the wall, and that there was a muster in the spring. She had thought she might have to go to Serrat. She had thought she might have to follow an army.

Now the queen was coming to Valora for Midwinter, to the palace Riarhin had walked in under another woman’s face, to the rooms whose stairs and passages she carried in her head the way she carried everything.

It was very convenient. She sat with that for a moment, and did not like it, and could not have said why, except that the road ahead was being cleared for the queen before she had reached it.

“How long?” said Amari.

Riarhin counted. She did not need to; she knew the date. She counted anyway, on her fingers, as Nessa did, and found she wanted the time to be longer.

“Six weeks,” she said.

Chapter Eleven — The Dike

It began to rain the day after the herald, and it did not stop.

It came in from the west over the flat country, not hard, a grey steady rain that got into everything and stayed there. By noon the road was running with it. By evening the ditches Riarhin had admired on the first morning out of Fairport were full to the lip, and the fields on either side had begun to shine. Tavin drove with his hat pulled down and water running off the brim, and Penny rode under his coat with her head out at the collar, looking at the weather with deep personal offence.

They slept that night in a barn that leaked, and the next day it rained harder.

The milestone stood at a crossroads in the middle of the fen, where the great road met a lesser one coming in from the east on a causeway. It was a tall stone, older than the road, with the Queen’s mark cut deep into its face and the number of miles to Valora beneath. Over the Queen’s mark somebody had pasted a notice. Over that notice somebody else had pasted another. Over both, a third, newer, had been nailed with a horseshoe nail driven into a crack in the stone.

Riarhin got down to read them because the cart had stopped.

The first was from the sheriff of the eastern hundred and said the causeway was his to close. The second was from the sheriff of the western hundred and said the causeway was his to keep open, the eastern sheriff’s writ being void on account of a boundary settled in the old king’s time. The third was from somebody who signed himself Reeve of the Dike of Harrowmere and said, in a large square hand, that the dike had broken at the Long Sluice, and that the road beyond the stone was under water to a horse’s belly, and that neither sheriff was here.

Beneath it, in the same hand, smaller: All travellers to the sluice. The dike feeds the road.

“What does that mean?” said Tavin, from the box.

Riarhin looked down the great road. It ran on south across the fen, straight and raised, for perhaps half a mile, and then it went into water. The water lay across it grey and flat under the rain with the tops of the hedges standing up out of it on either side, and on the far side of the water, a long way off, the road came out again and went on.

Between, off to the right where the fen dropped towards the river, there were lights. There were a great many of them, lanterns and torches in the grey afternoon, strung out along a line she could not see, and figures moving among them, and the sound, very faint, of somebody shouting.

“It means we work,” said Amari.

The reeve met them at the edge of the water.

He was an old man, short and wide, in a leather coat that had been oiled so many times it shone like a beetle, and he carried a rod of black wood as long as his arm, with notches cut into it from end to end. He stood in the road up to his shins in flood and looked at the cart, and the eight of them, and the horse, and counted them with his eyes the way Davra had counted them on the landing at Scarrow.

“You can wait,” he said. “Or you can come. If you wait, it’s three days, maybe four, before the road’s clear. If you come, it’s one night, maybe, if the rain eases and the sluice holds. Nobody passes this stone going south till the dike’s shut. That’s not the sheriffs. That’s the water.” He pointed with the rod. “There’s bread at the sluice, and a fire, and the brotherhood feeds anybody who puts his hands to the bags. Them as can’t lift can carry the lanterns.” He looked at Berent in the back of the cart. “Or sit and count.”

“Count what?” said Berent.

“Bags.” The reeve lifted the rod a little. “I can’t be in two places. The count wants a man who can call it and be listened to.”

Riarhin looked at the lights along the dike. She looked at the water across the road, and the hedges standing in it, and a gate in a field to the left that was underwater to its top bar, with a drowned sheep caught against it.

“We’ll come,” she said.

It was the hardest night’s work she had done since the causeway at Rossan, and it had nothing to do with her.

The dike ran along the edge of the fen where the land fell away to the river, a long bank of turf and clay as high as a man and as wide as a cart on top, and at the Long Sluice, where a stone channel let the fen water out into the river when the river was low, the river had come the other way. It had pushed in through the sluice gate and torn it off its hinges and eaten away the bank on either side, and now there was a gap in the dike the width of a house, with the river pouring through it into the fen in a brown roaring slope, and the whole of Harrowmere on the bank above it trying to fill it with bags of earth.

There were perhaps a hundred people. Riarhin could not count them; they would not stay still. Men and women and older children, in coats and sacking, in a line from the spoil pits behind the bank to the lip of the breach, passing the bags hand to hand. At the lip there were men in the water to the waist, lashed to posts with ropes, taking the bags and setting them. Every so often the river took a bag out of their hands and it went, turning, down the brown slope into the fen and was gone.

There were soldiers too.

She saw them as soon as she came up onto the bank. Twenty or so of the Queen’s foot, in their coats, with their pikes stacked under a tarpaulin and their sleeves rolled, working in the line with the fen people. Their serjeant was up to his waist at the lip of the breach. Their officer, a very young man with a wet fair moustache he did not yet need, stood on the top of the bank with a paper in his hand and looked miserable.

“Escort,” said the reeve, beside her. “For a salt convoy going north. The eastern sheriff’s writ pulled them off the road this morning and put them on the dike. The lieutenant says his orders don’t say dikes.” The reeve spat into the rain. “I said the river doesn’t read. He’s been standing there since noon deciding whether to believe me.”

Riarhin looked at the young man on the bank with his paper.

“He’s let his men work.”

“He has. He’s not stopped them.” The reeve considered this. “That’s not nothing. I’ve known worse.”

They went into the line.

Cadan went to the lip of the breach without being told and put a rope round his waist and went down into the water beside the soldiers’ serjeant, and did not come out again until the breach was shut. Riarhin saw him every time the torches flared: a grey shape in the brown water, taking the bags and setting them, not fast, never losing one. The men on either side of him began, after a while, to watch where he put his feet.

Amari found the spoil pits. There were four of them, and the people filling bags at them were getting in each other’s way, and the bags were going up the line in a clump and then not at all, and Amari stood at the edge of the pits for the time it took to eat a piece of bread and then began, quietly, to move people. He moved two women with spades to the third pit, and a boy with a barrow to the fourth, and a man who was filling bags too full to the far end where there was nobody to carry them, and after half an hour the bags were going up the line one after another like beads on a string.

Tavin rigged a block.

Riarhin did not see where he found it. He came up out of the dark behind the bank with a coil of rope over his shoulder and a pulley block from somebody’s barn and a pole, and talked to the reeve for a moment, and the reeve looked at him with profound suspicion and then nodded, and Tavin went down to the lip of the breach and sank the pole and hung the block from it, and after that the heaviest bags went out over the water on the rope instead of in men’s arms, and the men in the water could set them where the river was fastest without being taken off their feet.

Aurine went to the fire behind the bank where they were bringing the ones who had been in too long. She did not go into the line at all. Riarhin saw her once, kneeling beside a man whose lips had gone blue, rubbing his hands between hers, and saying something to the woman who had brought him that made the woman go and fetch more blankets.

Saikou carried lanterns. He carried them up and down the line all night, wherever one had gone out, with his long coat soaked black and his face very calm, and relit them from his own, and hung them on the posts, and went on. Nobody thanked him. Nobody noticed him. Riarhin thought that he preferred it.

Nessa sang.

She did not begin it. A woman in the line began it, a work song, a slow heavy one with a beat in it for the passing of a bag, and it went up and down the line and faltered where the people did not know it. Nessa knew it. She took it up from the middle of the line in her clear light voice, and carried it through the place where it had faltered, and the people on either side of her caught it from her, and it went on. After that it did not stop. It went on through the night under the rain, with the river roaring through the breach below it, and whenever the line began to slow the song came round again, and the bags came with it.

Berent counted.

The reeve had put him on a cart on the top of the bank, above the breach, with a lantern and a slate and the notched rod laid across his knees for a sign of whose voice it was. He had been there since dusk. At first he had called the count the way the reeve had told him to, by the bag, a number every twenty, so the people in the line could hear it and know how far they had come.

Then, a little after midnight, the river took the block.

Riarhin was in the line when it happened. She heard the crack of the pole, and a shout, and saw the rope go slack and then taut and then go, whipping out over the water with the block on the end of it, and the bag that had been swinging out on it drop into the breach and vanish. The men at the lip staggered. One of them went down. The soldiers’ serjeant had him by the collar and hauled him up again, and he was coughing and shouting, and the line stopped, all the way back to the pits, and the song stopped with it.

In the stillness Riarhin heard the river. It was louder. The gap had widened. She could see it in the torchlight: a slab of the bank on the near side, turf and clay together, sagging slowly outward into the flood.

Nobody moved. Everybody was looking at the lip.

“Line!”

It came from the top of the bank. It was not loud. It did not need to be. It was a voice she had heard shout across a ford and down a hall and once, on the causeway at Rossan, over the sound of men dying, and it went down the dike through the rain like a thrown rope.

“Line. Bags to the near side. Every second bag to the near side, the rest as you were. Serjeant, your men off the lip, two paces back, let the bags come to you. You in the water, hold. Hold. The block’s gone; we don’t need it. Near side. Go.”

The line went.

Riarhin felt it through her hands: the bag coming to her, and her turning, and the bag going on, and the next one coming. The soldiers stepped back from the lip. The bags came to them faster. The sagging slab of the near bank took one bag against its foot, and another, and another, and stopped sagging.

“Count,” said Berent from the cart, in the same voice. “Twenty. Forty. Sixty. Near side holding. Good. Keep it coming. Singer, where’s your song.”

Nessa began it again. The line took it up.

Riarhin, passing a bag, looked up once at the top of the bank. Berent was standing. He had got off the cart somehow and was standing on the top of the bank in the rain with the lantern at his feet and the notched rod in his hand and his weight on the good leg, and the young lieutenant with the fair moustache was standing beside him, a little behind, watching the breach where Berent was watching it.

She looked away. She went on passing bags.

They shut the breach a little before dawn.

It came slowly, a narrowing of the brown slope of water through the gap until it was a rush, and then a stream, and then a sheet of water pouring over the top of a bank of bags that the river could no longer shift, and then, as the fen people brought turf and beat it down on top of the bags with the backs of their spades, a trickle. Somebody cheered. It was a thin sound in the rain and nobody took it up. They were too tired.

The rain had eased. Riarhin stood at the top of the bank with her hands raw and her back one long ache and looked out over the fen. It lay grey and shining to the horizon, full of water, with the tops of the hedges standing in it and the drowned sheep still caught against its gate. It would be a week draining, the reeve said. Two, if the rain came back. But the river was on its own side of the dike again, and the road would come up out of the water in a day, and the fen would go down, slowly, into its ditches, the way it always had.

The line was breaking up. People sat down where they stood. The women with the bread came round.

Cadan came up out of the water on the rope, blue to the lips, and sat down on the bank without a word and let Aurine put a blanket round him. The soldiers’ serjeant came up after him and stood looking at him for a moment, and then sat down next to him and offered him a pull from a flask, and Cadan declined it with great courtesy, and the serjeant drank it himself.

The lieutenant came to find Berent while the bread was going round.

Riarhin was sitting on the cart’s tail beside him, eating. She saw the young man coming along the top of the bank and saw him hesitate, and straighten his coat, which was soaked through and did not straighten, and come on.

“Sir,” he said.

Berent looked at him.

“I wanted to thank you.” The lieutenant had gone pink under the wet moustache. “For the line. When the block went. I didn’t—” He stopped. “I’d not been in anything like that. I didn’t know what to tell them. You did.”

“You let them work,” said Berent. “All day. When your orders didn’t say dikes. That was the hard part. The rest was just shouting.”

The lieutenant did not seem to know what to do with that. He stood in the rain looking at Berent, and at his stick, and at the long bundle in the back of the cart under the oilcloth, and something went across his face that Riarhin did not like.

“You’ve commanded,” he said.

“Carts,” said Berent. “I’ve commanded carts.”

“Sir, with respect, you called that line like a Dawn knight. I’ve heard them drill in the Hall. I know the voice.” The lieutenant took out a small damp book from inside his coat and a stub of pencil. “I’m bound to report the day to the sheriff’s justiciar. Why we were off the road, and how long, and what was done. It’s the form. I’d like to put your name in it. For the credit.” He looked up with the pencil ready. “It’d be a good thing to have in a justiciar’s report, sir. For a man who’d served. They read those at the Hall.”

Berent did not answer.

He sat on the cart’s tail with the bread in his hand and looked at the young man, and Riarhin watched his face do the thing it had done in the counting field at Fairport, when the serjeant asked him where he had served. She watched him have the answer in his mouth.

“Don’t,” he said.

The lieutenant blinked.

“Put the brotherhood in it,” said Berent. “Put the reeve. Put your serjeant; he held that man at the lip when he went under, you saw it. Put the singer. Put the monk.” He looked away down the dike, where the fen people were sitting in the wet with their bread. “Don’t put me. I don’t want the credit. I’ve had credit. It’s not worth the ink.”

“Sir, it’s the form—”

“I know it’s the form.” Berent’s voice did not rise. “Write a lame man from the north road called the line. That’s true. That’s all that’s true. The rest is somebody else’s.”

The young man looked at him a moment longer. Then he wrote something in the book, and closed it, and put it away, and saluted, very properly, in the rain, and went back along the top of the bank to his men.

Riarhin did not ask what he had written. Berent did not say.

“A lame man from the north road,” he said after a while, to his bread. “Going south before a council.” He turned the bread over. “If they’re thorough at the Hall.”

“Are they?”

“Some of them.” He took a bite. “I trained some of them.”

The reeve came last, with the rod.

He came along the bank from the sluice, where he had been looking at the new work with his lantern, and stood in front of the cart and looked at the eight of them, the way he had looked at them at the edge of the water. Then he held out his hand to Berent for the rod, and Berent gave it back to him.

The reeve took a knife from his belt. He looked along the rod, from end to end, at all the notches in it: old ones worn smooth and dark with handling, and newer ones sharp and pale. Near the end there was a space. He set the knife in the space and cut a notch, one, clean, across the grain.

“Harrowmere keeps a night for you,” he said. “That’s what that is. Any of you come to this dike again in your lives, in any weather, you show me or my son or whoever has the rod after, and you’ll be fed, and housed, and nobody in the parish will ask you anything you don’t want to answer. That’s the brotherhood. That’s old. Older than either sheriff.”

He put the knife away.

“Now,” he said. “Names. For the roll.”

Riarhin had known it was coming. She had known it since the milestone.

“The dike keeps a roll,” said the reeve. “Every flood. Who came. Who worked. It goes in the book at the church, and it’s read out at the dike feast every spring, so the parish knows who stood on the bank.” He had taken a book out of his oiled coat, a small thick one wrapped in leather against the wet. “Say them and I’ll write them.”

Nobody said anything.

The reeve looked at them. He looked from face to face, slowly, in the grey light: at Riarhin, and Berent, and Amari, and Cadan in his blanket, and Tavin, and Nessa, and Aurine with her satchel, and Saikou, standing a little apart with a lantern he had not yet put out. His old face did not change.

“I’ve had the rod forty years,” he said. “I’ve seen people not want to be in a book before.”

“I’m sorry,” said Riarhin. “It’s not the dike.”

“I know it’s not the dike.” He looked at the book in his hand. He looked at the rod. Then he put the book back inside his coat without opening it. “The priest can ask me why there are no names.” He tapped the new notch with his thumb. “Eight from the north road. That’s what that says. I’ll know you. My son will know the notch. Nobody else needs to.”

He looked at Nessa.

“You,” he said. “The singer. You took the dike song up in the middle when Mag lost it. Where’d you learn it?”

“My grandmother,” said Nessa. “In the north. It’s not the same words.”

“No. It wouldn’t be.” The reeve looked at her a moment longer. “It’s the same song, though.”

He went away along the bank with his rod over his shoulder.

The road came up out of the water that afternoon, as the reeve had said it would, and they went on south with the fen shining on either side and the drowned hedges standing up out of it, and nobody talked much. They were too tired. Tavin drove with his eyes half shut and the horse knew the way. Cadan slept in the back of the cart against the sacks with his hands folded on his chest like a carving on a tomb.

On the second day after the dike they came to the ford.

Riarhin knew it before they reached it. She knew the shape of the land, the way the road dipped down between two low hills to a stream, the alders on the far bank, the stone with a ring in it where carters tethered horses to wait their turn. She had crossed here a year ago, going south, in Ewen’s train. Ewen’s wheel had cracked on a stone in the middle of the ford and they had lost half a day, and Na’ar had walked back along the line of wagons in the evening to where she was standing by the rear cart and said, in his slow deep voice, that he had never in his life seen a man so angry with a stone.

The ford was gone. There was a bridge.

It was new, and stone, three low arches over the stream, with a parapet on either side and a date cut into the keystone of the middle arch. The Queen’s mark was cut beside the date. The road went over it level and smooth, and the stream went under it, and the stone with the ring in it on the near bank was still there, beside the approach, with nothing tethered to it.

Tavin slowed the cart without being asked.

Riarhin got down. She walked onto the bridge and stood at the parapet in the middle, over the place where the wheel had cracked, and looked down at the water. It was clear and quick and brown over the stones. She could see the stone the wheel had struck. It was a big flat stone near the far bank, with a notch in its edge. It was still there. The bridge had been built over it.

Behind her she heard the stick on the paving.

Berent came and stood beside her at the parapet. He had got down from the cart without help, which he could do now, and walked the length of the bridge, which he could also do. He did not say anything. He looked down at the water and the flat stone with the notch in it.

Then he reached behind him and unhooked the long bundle in its oilcloth that he had carried from the cart, and laid it along the parapet in front of them both, and put his hand flat on it.

They stood there for a while.

“He hated fords,” said Berent. “He said so, that night. He said he’d crossed a hundred and hated every one. He said a ford was a place where a road stopped being honest about what it was.” His hand did not move on the oilcloth. “He’d have liked the bridge.”

“He’d have said it was too good for the stream.”

Berent almost laughed. It came out as a breath.

“He would. He would have.” He was quiet a moment. “He’d have said the stream didn’t ask for it.”

Riarhin looked at the keystone, with its date and the Queen’s mark cut beside it.

Berent picked up the sword and hooked it on his back again.

“Come on,” he said. “Tavin’s giving us the look.”

They walked back off the bridge together. At the end of it, by the stone with the ring, Berent stopped and put his hand on the ring, briefly, and then went on to the cart.

It did not rain again all the way to Valora.

Riarhin noticed that. She did not say anything about it. The sky cleared behind them over the fen and stayed clear, cold and pale, and the road dried under the wheels, and the country on either side of it went on working: the ditches full, the new culverts running, the posting houses with their boards, the recruiting tables in the inn yards. At every stop there was somebody who had heard about the breach at Harrowmere, and somebody who had heard it had been shut in a night, and nobody who knew who had shut it.

Chapter Twelve — One Guest

Ewen kept his yard outside the north gate of Valora, where the carriers’ yards had always been, between a ropewalk and a field of turnips, and he recognised Riarhin before she had got down from the cart.

He came out with a halter in his hand and his one good eye narrowed against the low sun. He lifted the halter in greeting, then saw the passengers behind her. His hand came down. He stayed in the gateway.

“You’re back,” he said.

“Yes.”

“He’s alive.”

“Mostly,” said Berent.

Ewen looked at Berent’s leg. Then he looked at the other six: at Amari, broad and quiet with his book; at Saikou, long and grave in his dark coat; at Tavin with a magpie on his shoulder; at Nessa with her white streaks and her shawl; at Aurine with her satchel; at Cadan, who smiled at him. He looked at Remorse on Riarhin’s back and at the long bundle in the cart that was Silence and Na’ar’s sword wrapped together in oilcloth.

“Come in,” he said. “You. Not the cart. We’ll talk in the stable.”

The stable was warm and smelled of horse and hay and linseed. Ewen’s drivers were at their supper in the yard beyond. Riarhin could hear them through the wall: a woman laughing, a child being told to sit still, a man complaining about the price of axle grease.

Ewen leaned against a stall with the halter over his shoulder.

“You want a place,” he said.

“For a few weeks. Until Midwinter. We’d pay.”

“I know you’d pay. You paid me last time. You worked the rear and you fetched the farmer’s daughter and you never once asked me to take a wheel off for your hurry.” He looked at the stable door, and through it, at the cart in the lane. “I’d have you. You, and him with the leg, and I’d find a corner for the tall one with the book, maybe. Three.” He shook his head. “Not eight.”

“They’re quiet. They’re—”

“They’re armed.” Ewen said it flatly. “I counted. Your sword and your bow and his sword, and the big man’s got a cudgel under the seat he thinks I didn’t see, and the woman with the satchel’s got something in it that isn’t herbs, and the one who smiles.” He stopped. “I don’t know what the one who smiles has. I don’t want to.”

Riarhin said nothing.

“Listen,” said Ewen. “I’ve eleven drivers in this yard. Seven of them have wives and there’s nine children. They sleep over the stables and in the loft and in the house. If you’re here, and the queen’s men come looking for a woman with a sword and her friends, which they will, because they always come looking for somebody in the weeks before a council, they don’t find you in a room in my house that I can say I let to a traveller. They find eight of you in my yard. And they don’t ask me what I knew. They ask Mag, who does the harness, and Col, who’s fourteen, and Col’s mother.” He took the halter off his shoulder and hung it on the peg. “I can answer for a guest. I can say, she was a carrier’s guard, she rode with me, she asked for a bed. I can’t answer for a company.”

“I understand.”

“I don’t think you do. I think you think I’m afraid.” Ewen looked at her with his one eye. “I am afraid. That’s not why. I’m saying no because it’s their yard too. And they didn’t get asked.”

Riarhin stood in the warm stable with the smell of linseed and the voices through the wall, and she thought of Nessa on the hatch cover of Davra’s ship. Where we can walk away if we want to.

“No,” she said. “They didn’t.”

She could have argued. She could see the argument: the money, the few weeks, the promise that they would be careful, the fact that he owed her something for the farmer’s daughter. She could have found the thing in Ewen that would make him say yes, and made him say it, and he would have been sorry every night until Midwinter, and so would Col’s mother.

“Thank you for the three,” she said. “I’ll not take them. It’d be the same yard.”

Ewen let out a breath.

“There’s a woman in the ropewalk has a room,” he said. “One. She’s deaf and she likes a quiet lodger. And there’s a fellow in the old tanneries by the river who lets anything to anybody and asks nothing, because nobody asks him anything either. It stinks.” He paused. “I didn’t tell you either of those.”

“No.”

“Come and eat, though,” he said. “You and him with the leg. Tonight. That I can answer for. Mag’ll want to see him.”

They ate in Ewen’s yard that night, the two of them, while the other six waited in the lane with the cart and ate bread and cheese out of a basket Mag sent out to them without being asked. Mag was the woman who did the harness. She had been on Ewen’s train south a year before and had watched Berent ride the rear with his jaw set for three hundred miles, and she looked at him now across the table, and at the stick, with an expression of disgust.

“Somebody’s told you to walk on that,” she said. “You’ve not been doing it.”

“I have.”

“You’ve been doing half.”

Berent opened his mouth and shut it again. Riarhin, beside him, found that she could not stop smiling, and put her face in her cup.

Afterwards, in the lane, the company stood round the cart in the dark while she told them.

“Separate,” she said. “Three places. Maybe four. Not all together. Nobody should be able to find all of us by finding one of us.”

“That’ll cost,” said Amari.

“I know.”

“More than one house would have.”

“I know that too.”

Tavin had been leaning on the cart wheel with Penny asleep on his shoulder. He straightened.

“Leave it with me,” he said.

Tavin found the tannery.

He was gone all the next day and came back at dusk to the ropewalk, where Riarhin had taken the deaf woman’s room for herself and Berent and nobody else, and sat on the end of the bed and told them about it with enormous satisfaction.

It was in the old tanning quarter by the river, below the east wall, in a street that had been abandoned by the tanners when the new yards were built downstream and not yet taken by anybody else because of the smell. It had a loft over a drying floor, with a hoist door that opened onto the river and a stair that came down into a yard that had three ways out: into the street, into the next yard, and through a gap in a wall that Tavin said nobody but a cat would think was a way. The man who let it was a former tanner with no teeth who let anything to anybody and asked nothing and wanted paying weekly in advance.

“How much?”

Tavin told her.

Amari, who had come in behind him, made a sound.

“That’s three times what it’s worth,” said Amari.

“It’s three times what it’s worth to a tanner,” said Tavin. “To us it’s worth a great deal more. Three ways out. A hoist door on the river. No neighbours who can see in. And a landlord who won’t remember our faces because he’s never once in his life looked at a face.” He turned to Riarhin. “And after.”

“After?”

“There’ll be an after. There always is.” Tavin had his most innocent face on. “People forget to take a place for it. I thought, if things go—” he waved a hand— “however they go, there’ll be a night when some of us need to be somewhere with three ways out and a door onto the water. I thought I’d rather it was somewhere we’d already been.”

Amari looked at him.

“You mean somewhere you’d already been.”

“That too.”

“You’re taking it for yourself.”

“I’m taking it for all of us,” said Tavin with dignity, “and I expect to use it myself. Those aren’t different. A man can be generous and prudent at once. It’s very rare. I’m proud of it.”

Riarhin laughed. It surprised her, and it surprised Berent, who had been lying on the deaf woman’s bed with his eyes shut.

“Take it,” she said.

“It’ll come out of the Valora heap,” said Amari. “Not the other.”

“Take it out of the Valora heap.”

Amari wrote it in the book. He wrote it slowly, in his neat hand, and when he had finished he looked at it and said, without looking up, that it was the first entry in three weeks he had not wanted to argue with, and that he was going to go and look at the smell himself before he believed it.

Part Four — The Changed Household

Chapter Thirteen — The Screened Stair

The laundry carts went into the palace by the west service gate at the first bell, and Riarhin went in with them.

She had worked it out from the ropewalk window over three mornings. The carts came up from the washing yards by the river, where the palace linen was done, two or three of them, pushed by women, piled high with baskets under oilcloth. At the west gate the guard lifted the oilcloth on the first cart and looked under it and let it through, and lifted the oilcloth on the second cart and did not look under it, and did not lift the oilcloth on the third at all. The women pushing the carts said good morning to him and he said good morning back. On the second morning one of them gave him a bun.

On the fourth morning Riarhin was at the back of the third cart with a basket on her hip and a scarf over her hair, and she said good morning to the guard in the flat Valoran voice she had heard in the street, and he said good morning back and did not look at her.

She was inside.

It was not the same.

She had walked these yards under another woman’s face, a year ago, in a brown gown with a narrow cuff, with Orrel beside her carrying papers. She had every stone of them. She had the laundry stair and the kitchen door and the passage to the back rooms where the household officers kept their desks. She had the screened service stair at the far end of that passage, with the carpenter kneeling at the bottom shaving a strip from the lowest tread, and the maid putting her foot exactly where he did not want it, and the guard who changed at supper and asked whether the dishes had come down.

The yard was the same. The kitchen door was the same. The passage was not.

A wall had been put across it. Not a thick one: lath and plaster, freshly limed, with a door in it, and the door was shut, and beside the door there was a table, and at the table sat a woman with a book.

Riarhin stood in the yard with her basket on her hip and looked at the new wall for as long as a washerwoman’s help might look at anything, which was not long.

The laundry women were going through the kitchen door with their baskets. Riarhin went with them. Inside there was a long stone passage, warm from the ovens, and at the end of it a stair going up, and at the foot of the stair another table with another woman at it, and a book. The laundry women stopped at the table one by one. The woman at the table looked at each basket, wrote in the book, and said a number. The laundry woman went up the stair with the basket and came down again without it.

Riarhin had not known about the book.

She stood in the line with her basket. There were two women ahead of her. She could see the stair beyond the table: the screened stair, the one from the far end of the passage, reached now from this end instead, the carpenter long gone and the rail fixed. Somebody had moved the linen stores. Somebody had made the service stair the linen stair and put a woman with a book at the foot of it.

The woman with the book looked up.

She was perhaps forty. She had a broad plain face and forearms like a baker’s and a pen behind her ear, and she looked at Riarhin, and at the basket, and at Riarhin again.

“You’re not Aude’s,” she said.

“I’m helping.”

“Helping who?”

“Aude.”

“Aude’s at home with her chest.” The woman did not raise her voice. “I sent her home yesterday myself. She’s coughing like a rook. Whose are you?”

Riarhin looked at her. Behind her in the line the laundry women had stopped talking. One of them, the one who had given the guard a bun, was looking at the floor.

“Nobody’s,” said Riarhin. “I heard there was work.”

“There isn’t.” The woman put her pen down. “Not on this stair. This stair goes to the Queen’s own linen, and the Consort’s, and the little one’s, and every sheet that goes up it goes in my book with my name on the line, and every woman that carries it goes in my book too. If a sheet goes missing it’s my name. If a woman goes up that shouldn’t, it’s my name. It’s my place.” She looked at Riarhin steadily. “I’ve had this place two months. The woman before me had it nine years and they turned her off in a day because a cup went up this stair on a tray that wasn’t in anybody’s book, and nobody could say whose.”

“I’ll go,” said Riarhin.

“You will.” The woman stood up. She was taller than Riarhin had thought. “I’ll walk you to the gate. Put the basket down. That’s Aude’s.”

Riarhin put the basket down.

The woman walked her back through the kitchen and across the yard to the west gate, not touching her, not hurrying. The guard at the gate looked up from his bun.

“Maheut?”

“She’s going.”

“Who is she?”

“Somebody who heard there was work.” Maheut stopped at the gate. She looked at Riarhin once more, a long plain look from her boots to her scarf. “There isn’t,” she said. “Not for somebody who isn’t in the book. I don’t know who you are and I don’t want to. If you come in with the carts again I’ll have you taken up, and I’ll be sorry, because you look like you need the work. But I’ll do it.”

“I understand.”

“I don’t think you do.” Maheut folded her baker’s arms. “I’ve a boy of eleven and a mother who can’t get down the stairs. This place is the first place I’ve had in six years where I get paid on the day. It’s mine because somebody else lost it over a cup. I’m not losing it over you.”

She went back across the yard. She did not look round.

Riarhin went out through the gate and down the lane and did not stop until she was three streets away, in a doorway, with her back against the wood and her heart going hard.

She had remembered every turn and arrived at a woman who wanted to know who had hired her. The name she needed had never been on her map.

Saikou got in.

She had not wanted him to try. He had asked, on the second evening at the tannery, whether he might go with her in the morning as far as the outer yard, and she had said no, and he had said that he would not go in with her, only near, and that he wished to see whether a thing he sometimes did would work in a palace. She had asked what thing. He had said that he would rather show her than describe it, because described it sounded like a conjuring trick and it was not.

He was waiting in the lane outside the west gate when she came out. She walked past him without stopping, as they had agreed. Ten minutes later he found her in the doorway three streets off.

“You were stopped,” he said.

“By a woman with a book.”

“Maheut.” He said the name as though he were filing it. “I saw her. She is very good.”

“You saw her? From the lane?”

“From the kitchen passage.”

Riarhin looked at him.

He was standing in the street in his long dark coat with his hands in his sleeves, exactly as he always stood. Over his head and shoulders he had put a length of grey cloth, the kind worn in the south by a man walking behind a coffin. It did not hide his face. It hung down on either side of it, and he had pushed it back a little now, but it had been forward, she thought, when he went in.

“The custom in a house in mourning,” he said. “When a man walks behind the dead he does not look to either side. He does not speak. He walks at the pace of the bier, and people let him go where he is going, because to stop him would be to stop the dead.” He took the cloth off and began to fold it. “I say a thing under my breath, when I put it on. It is a very old thing and I did not make it. I am not sure it is doing anything at all. I am sure that people do not look at me while I wear it. Their eyes go past. As they would go past a mourner. They do not want to see what he is carrying.”

“You walked through the west gate like that.”

“Behind the third laundry cart. Yes.” He put the folded cloth into his coat. “And into the kitchen passage. And to the end of it, where the woman was. I could not go farther. She has a book, and a book is a kind of looking that does not go past anything. And I had to stop when you were stopped, because I could not walk at a bier’s pace and also see what happened to you.” He paused. “And if I had spoken—if anybody had spoken to me and I had answered—it would have been over. I know that much. It does not survive a conversation.”

Riarhin stood in the doorway.

“You didn’t tell me you could do that.”

“I did not know I could do it in a palace. I have done it in villages. In a shrine once. Never with so many people who are paid to look.” He considered. “It is not much. It will not open a door. It will not get past a woman with a book, or a guard who has been told to stop everybody, or a dog. It will get me across a yard, perhaps, or along a passage, if nobody has a reason to stop a mourner. I thought you should know what it is before you think of a use for it.”

“You think I’ll think of a use for it.”

Saikou tucked the cloth farther into his coat. “Before you put it in a plan, tell me.”

She did not answer. She was already thinking. He saw that, and did not seem surprised, and after a while they walked back together to the tannery through the smell.

Chapter Fourteen — The Wool Store

Deirdre’s wool store was beyond the east gate, where it had been a year ago, and Riarhin went to it on foot, in daylight, with her own face.

She had argued it with herself for two days. She had argued it with Berent for one, and lost, because Berent did not argue; he sat on the deaf woman’s bed with his leg out and said Lyra at intervals, the way he had said it every day on Davra’s ship, and waited for her to finish explaining why not. She was still explaining. Deirdre had asked, through three hands, before the company left Theona, that nobody send through her channel any more; her food carrier was accompanied now, and there had been questions about the books. Deirdre had a sister and carts and a lease on a store. Deirdre had been summoned once already on account of Lyra and had answered for every fold of a freight claim.

After this visit, Deirdre would have one more question to answer if they summoned her again.

“Then don’t go,” said Berent, when she said this.

“I have to know where she is.”

“Then go.”

“You’re not helping.”

“I’m not trying to.” He looked at her. “I’m trying to make you say which. You keep saying both.”

The store had a new door. The old one had been broken, Deirdre said afterwards, the week after Lyra was taken, by men looking for the freight claim, and she had had it replaced with oak and a lock she had paid Tavin’s sort of price for. She opened the new door a hand’s width when Riarhin knocked, and looked out, and did not open it any farther for a long moment.

Then she opened it enough to let a person through and shut it behind her.

“You,” she said.

“Yes.”

“You were a redhead.”

“That was somebody else’s face.”

“I know it was.” Deirdre stood with her back against the oak. She was a lean brown woman with rope-burns on both palms and hair cut short like a man’s, and she was angry, and under the anger she was something else. “I know what it was. Maelis told me after. Before she ran. I carried your letters for a year with that face on them and I never knew.” She stopped. “Does anybody know you’re here?”

“You do.”

Deirdre closed her eyes.

“Thank you,” she said. “That’s very helpful.”

She told Riarhin anyway. She told her in the back of the store, among the bales, sitting on a sack of fleece with the smell of lanolin thick in the air and the door barred.

Lyra had been moved. In the autumn, after Riarhin had gone, out of the rooms at the west office where the examinations were held and into the old east range of the palace, the part that had been a house before it was a palace. The household had it now. The Queen’s own household, not the chancellor’s: the young men in dark coats who answered to nobody but the Queen. Lyra was on the second floor, in a room at the end of a passage that had once been the steward’s wing. Deirdre knew because the woman who carried Lyra’s food lived in the same street as Deirdre’s sister and said so, once, in the summer, before the woman was told to stop saying anything and began to be followed to work.

“Is she well?”

“She was in the summer.” Deirdre looked at her hands. “She asked for a book. You know that. It went through me. I sent it to the old man at the University and he never answered. Not a line. I sent to him twice more. Then they started asking the food woman what books, and who for, and I stopped.”

“The comparison. The orchard direction. Orrel—”

“Orrel pressed it. It was heard. The dates didn’t match; the direction was made after the gate. Everybody in the room knew it.” Deirdre’s voice was flat. “They didn’t release her. They said the charge was being reconsidered in the light of the comparison, and that pending the reconsideration she would remain where she was, for her own safety, since there were matters touching the Queen’s household in which she might be a witness.” She laughed without any sound. “For her own safety. In the steward’s wing. With two men on the stair.”

Riarhin sat on the bale.

“Two men.”

“Two men on the stair. One at the foot of the passage. That’s what the food woman said. In the summer.” Deirdre looked at her. “It’ll be more now. Everything’s more now. There’s the council coming and they’ve turned the whole palace inside out for it. New walls. New women with books. Every carrier who goes in the east gate gets his cart searched to the axle.” She stood up. “That’s all I know. That’s all I’m going to know. Don’t come here again.”

“I won’t.”

“And don’t—” Deirdre stopped. She stood among her bales with her rope-burned hands at her sides. “Don’t get her killed getting her out. If that’s what you’re here for. She’s alive. She’s eating. It’s not much. It’s more than a lot of people.”

“I know.”

“Do you?” Deirdre looked at Riarhin, and at the sword on her back, and at her face, which Deirdre had never seen before this morning. “You got the other one out. The widow. I heard. In a cart with a bolt in your knight’s leg and your big friend dead in the yard.” She said it without cruelty. “I carried word of Lyra to you after, over the sea, till they stopped me. You took her papers with you. And you left her.”

Riarhin said nothing.

“I’m not saying you were wrong,” said Deirdre. “I don’t know if you were wrong. I’m saying she’s still there. And you’re here with your own face on. And I’d like to know, before you go out that door, whether you came to Valora for her.”

The store was very quiet. Somewhere outside a cart went past on the road with its wheels grinding.

“No,” said Riarhin. “Not only for her.”

Deirdre looked at her. Then she went and unbarred the door.

“That’s honest,” she said. “Go on. Out the side, not the front. There’s a woman across the road who sees everything.”

She told Berent that night, in the deaf woman’s room at the ropewalk, with the candle between them on the chest.

She told him all of it: the east range, the steward’s wing, the two men on the stair and the one at the foot of the passage, the comparison heard and the charge reconsidered and Lyra kept for her own safety. She told him what Deirdre had said at the end. She did not soften it.

Berent listened with his hands round his stick.

“She’s alive,” he said, when she had finished.

“In the summer. Yes.”

“She asked for a book.”

“Yes.”

“And nobody answered.” He looked at the candle. “The old man. Ridow. Nobody’s told him, maybe. Maybe he never had it.” He was quiet a while. “Or maybe he did and he was frightened.”

“Maybe.”

“You said not only for her.”

“I did.”

“To Deirdre.”

“Yes.”

Berent nodded slowly. He did not seem angry. He seemed, she thought, relieved.

“Then say it to me,” he said. “So I know what I’m in.”

Riarhin looked at him across the candle.

“I came to Valora to kill the queen,” she said. “And the Consort, if he stands between, and he will. That’s first. That’s the thing I can’t put down. Lyra is in the plan. I want her in it. I don’t yet know whether there’s a way to get her out on the same night that doesn’t get her killed, or you, or the thing that stops the queen. If there is, I’ll find it. If there isn’t—” She stopped.

“If there isn’t, she stays,” said Berent.

“If there isn’t, I don’t know.”

He looked at her.

“I’ll find it,” he said. “If you don’t. I’ll find the way. You find the queen.” He put his hand flat on the chest between them, beside the candle. “But she’s in it. Not after. Not when there’s time. In it.”

“She’s in it.”

“Say her name.”

Riarhin had not said it all evening. She had said she and her and Lyra’s room and the steward’s wing. She had been careful with it without knowing she was being careful.

“Lyra,” she said.

It went out into the small room and did not come back. Berent nodded, once, and lay back on the bed with his arm over his eyes, and after a while she blew out the candle.

Chapter Fifteen — Who Could Stop Us

It took them three weeks to learn what would stop them, and most of what they learned they learned by being wrong.

At the table in the tannery loft, Riarhin crossed out the line she had drawn through the linen stair. She could still use the stair. She could no longer put a time against it until Maheut trusted her to carry a basket upstairs. She wrote Maheut’s name beside the landing, leaving room beneath it. The hoist door stood open for air, and the corner of the sheet lifted in the draught.

Everybody brought her something.

Tavin brought the bell. He had spent four days in the old town drinking with a man who had been a bell-ringer at the palace chapel in the old king’s time and was now too deaf for it, and had come back with the whole of it: the bell in the tower of the old range, the rope that came down into a little room at the foot of the tower stair, the boy who slept in the little room, the three strokes and a pause that meant fire, the long ringing without a pause that meant arms. When the long ringing went, said the old ringer, every door in the palace that had a bar was barred, and every man in the household came out with a sword, and in the queen’s new wing a man with a key on a chain went round and locked the iron doors between the new wing and the old.

“Who?” said Riarhin.

“Captain of the inner doors.” Tavin had written it on his cuff. “Name of Denisot. Old soldier. Has the key on a chain round his waist and sleeps with it. Has the gout. Very bad temper in the mornings.” He looked at the cuff. “The ringer didn’t like him. He said so for about an hour.”

Aurine went very still at the word gout.

Amari brought the barracks. He had not got inside; he had sat for five mornings on the bridge over the palace ditch below the old range with a basket of apples to sell, and sold almost none, and counted. The household guard slept in the old range, on the ground floor, in what had been the stables of the house before it was a palace. There were forty of them, give or take. They came out at the second bell and drilled in the court and went back in, and at night the door of their range was shut and one man stood outside it. When they came out they came across a covered bridge from the old range to the new wing, because the old range had its own court and the new wing had the queen.

“One bridge,” said Amari. “Wooden. Roofed. Wide enough for three. They’d all come over it.”

“And their captain?”

“Lieutenant, at night. A young one.” Amari looked at his book. “The men call him by his name. Not sir. By his name. They like him.”

“What name?”

Amari told her.

Riarhin sat back. She knew the name. She had heard it in a list of the Mound’s young men that Berent had given her in Glenruan, in the hall, with the rain outside, the evening he told her about the queen’s new guard: younger sons, heirs of small houses, sworn to the queen’s person and to something else. Guichard. A younger son of a western lord. Berent had said he was the best of them. Berent had said that Elyas had once sat up half a night to get the boy a hearing when his father wanted him hanged.

“Berent knows him,” she said.

“Berent said,” said Amari. “When I told him. He went a bad colour. He said Guichard was the one who’d come if the bell went. First over the bridge. He said he’d be very good at it.”

Nessa and Berent brought the steward’s wing, and Saikou brought Fayd.

Nessa had got into the old range by the simple means of being asked to sing there. There was a woman who kept the household’s kitchens in the old range, and her mother was dying, and her mother had been born in the north and wanted a northern song at the end. Nessa had heard about it in the ropewalk from the deaf woman, who had heard about it from her sister, and had gone. She had sung at the dying woman’s bed for three evenings. On the third evening, going out, she had been sent down the back stair instead of the front because the front was being washed, and the back stair had taken her past the foot of a passage on the second floor, and at the foot of the passage there had been a man on a stool, and at the end of the passage a door with a bar across it on the outside.
