“Two on the stair above,” said Nessa. “One on the stool. They change at the fourth bell. The one on the stool plays dice with himself, left hand against right. He cheats.”

“Which hand wins?” said Tavin.

“The left.”

Tavin nodded as though this told him a great deal.

Saikou brought Fayd because he had gone looking for somebody else and found Fayd instead. He said so with some annoyance. He had gone to the chancery, in his character as a Kahfagian scholar with letters of introduction from his mother’s house, to ask about records of a southern object, and had been passed from one clerk to another until he found himself in a room with a new wall the colour of old bread and a neat grey man behind a desk who asked him very courteously what he was really looking for.

“What did you tell him?”

“The truth. That I am looking for the origins of a thing that the queen wears.” Saikou folded his hands. “He said that a great many people were. He said that he had a file on it, which he would not show me, and that the file was mostly people who had been looking and had not found. He asked whether I was one of those. I said I thought I probably was.”

“Did he know who you are?”

“He knew who my mother is. That was enough.” Saikou looked at the river through the hoist door. “He is not going to be in the palace at Midwinter. He said so. He keeps the feast with his own household, in his own house, across the river. He said it as a man says where he will be, so that one knows where not to send for him.” He paused. “I thought that was interesting.”

Riarhin thought it was very interesting. She wrote Fayd — across the river — says so on her sheet, and drew a line under it, and did not know what the line meant.

When she had it all, she laid it out on the table in the loft for the company to see.

It was not a map. It was three names.

Guichard. He could bring armed help. Forty men across a covered bridge, in the time it took to wake them. If he came, nobody in the new wing would be alone with anyone for long.

Denisot. He could close the passage. One key on one chain, round one gouty waist, and the iron doors between the old range and the new would be shut, and whoever was on the wrong side of them would stay there.

Tylan. He could send word beyond the household. He held the Marshal’s seal. If he knew in time, he could send a rider with that seal to the garrison outside the walls, and the garrison would come, and it would not matter who else was dead.

She looked at the three names for a long time.

“Why kill them?” said Nessa.

She had been quiet all evening. She was sitting on the floor with her back against a bale of old hides and her shawl round her knees, and she asked it without any challenge in it at all.

“Why not lock them in? Why not give them something to make them sleep? Why not pay them?”

Riarhin had asked herself the same thing every night for a week.

“Guichard sleeps in the barracks with forty men,” she said. “His round is the only time we’ve found him alone. Cadan can reach him there. He can’t carry him across the court alive, and we have nowhere on the round to hold him. Someone would hear.”

“Bind him. Leave him in a stall.”

“For one of his men to find when he misses the vigil.” Riarhin kept her eyes on Nessa. “Amari needs longer than that. I mean to buy him the time.”

“And Denisot?”

Aurine answered that. She had been sitting at the end of the table with her satchel and had said nothing at all since Tavin said gout.

“He takes a draught every night at the ninth bell,” she said. “For the gout. I’ve been making his powders in the apothecary’s shop for four days. The old man’s hands shake. He’s grateful for mine.” She opened her satchel and took out an empty folded paper. “Denisot already takes poppy. Sometimes it puts him to sleep. Sometimes he calls for more. The apothecary has been trying to find the measure for months.”

“And you can’t?”

“Not through a servant who carries up a paper. I could increase it and hope. If you want him certain to stay in that chair until morning, I’d have to give enough to kill him.”

“So you’ll kill him because you can’t be sure how long he’ll sleep,” said Nessa.

Aurine folded the empty paper along its old creases.

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

Nessa waited. Riarhin put Denisot’s name beside the doors and left it there.

“And Tylan?”

Nobody answered that one. Riarhin looked at the third name. She did not need anybody to answer it.

“Tylan I’ll see to,” she said.

Afterwards, when the others had gone down, Saikou stayed.

He sat at the table across from her with the three names between them. The river went by below the hoist door, black and slow. Somewhere in the next yard a dog was barking at nothing.

“You haven’t put her on it,” he said.

“She’s not an obstacle. She’s the—” Riarhin stopped. “She’s the reason.”

“She is also wearing the thing,” said Saikou. “You have not put that on it either.”

Riarhin looked at him.

“You have been thinking about it,” he said. “You have been thinking about it since Carrickane. You have been thinking: what if every piece of this goes right, every name on this sheet, and then something arrives. A messenger in the wrong passage. A door that should have been open and was not. A man who was supposed to be across the river who comes back early for his hat.” He did not smile. “You have been thinking: what if it helps her.”

“Does it?”

“I don’t know.” He said it as he said the time. “I have a theory. I have told you it is a theory. The theory says that things come towards the one who wears it the way water comes down a slope. Not what she wants. What is nearest to what she wants, and downhill.” He put a long finger on the table. “If that is right, then on the night, things will come towards her. Help will come towards her. It will not be magic. It will be a guard who could not sleep and went for a walk. A servant who forgot a cup and went back. Coincidence, arriving when it is wanted.”

“Then we can’t plan against it.”

“You can’t plan against luck, no. You can only make it have further to go.” He looked at her. “If it matters that it is on her throat—if it matters that it is near her, touching her—then taking it off her would matter. Not destroying it. I don’t know how to destroy it and I do not think you do. Only taking it off her. Putting a room between them. A yard.”

“You think that would work.”

“I think it is consistent with.” He almost smiled then. “I think it is the only thing I can think of that is consistent with anything. I also think—I want you to hear this—that I do not know what happens to the slope when you take it away from the one it is running towards. It may run towards somebody else. It may run towards whoever is nearest. It may run towards whoever picks it up.”

Riarhin was quiet.

“The ribbon,” she said at last.

“The ribbon.”

“It’s on a black ribbon. Round her throat. It’s been on a black ribbon since the week I gave it to her.” She could see it, the way she could see everything: the narrow black silk, the little brass case against it, the queen’s finger catching it when Matylde reached. “A ribbon can be cut.”

“In a fight,” said Saikou. “With a sword. Against a woman with a longer reach who has been expecting you.”

“Yes.”

“It would cost you something. To make that cut first. Instead of a cut that kills.”

“Yes.”

He looked at her for a long time across the three names.

“Then you had better know before you go in,” he said, “how much you are willing to pay for it. Because you will have to pay it at once, and you will not have time to think.” He stood up. “And if you cut it, and it falls, do not let anybody else pick it up. Not me. Especially not me.”

He went down the stair. Riarhin sat in the loft with the river going by and the three names on the table and the fourth, which she had not written, in her head, where it had been since the Fairport market and the man with four children on his cart.

She wrote it under the others. Veralyn. Then she folded the sheet and put it in the fire, because it was not a thing that should exist on paper, and watched it burn, and found that it did not matter. She had every letter of it.

Chapter Sixteen — Placements

Cadan asked for Guichard himself.

He did it at the table in the loft on the evening after the three names, when the others had gone and Riarhin was sitting with the fire. He came up the stair quietly, as he did everything, and sat down across from her, and folded his hands on the table.

“The lieutenant,” he said. “The one the men call by his name.”

“Yes.”

“Is he mine?”

She looked at him. She had known this conversation was coming since Fairport. She had not known it would come before she had decided.

“I haven’t told you yet.”

“No.” Cadan was patient. “I am asking whether you are going to. So that I can begin. It will take time. He is not a man one reaches in a day.”

“How would you reach him?”

Cadan considered this with his head a little on one side.

“He drills his men in the court every morning,” he said. “I have watched from the bridge with Amari. He drills them well. Better than most. But he drills them in the new way, the Valora way, lines and forms, and his men are young and have never been in a fight where the line broke.” He turned one hand over. “He knows that. It troubles him. I saw him, on the fourth morning, stop a boy who had done the movement perfectly and stand in front of him for a long time as though he wanted to ask him something and did not know what.”

“And you would tell him what.”

“I would offer to show him what happens after the line breaks.” Cadan’s voice did not change. “I am a monk of Ikasha. There are not many of us who have left the house. The ones who have are known to teach. A young officer who is troubled about his men would be glad of such a teacher, if the teacher were courteous, and asked nothing, and came in the mornings when the court was open, and went away before the bell.”

Riarhin looked at him.

“You’d be teaching him,” she said. “For weeks. You’d know his men’s names. You’d eat with them.”

“Yes.”

“And then.”

“And then you would tell me exactly what to do,” said Cadan. “And I would do it.”

She sat with that. The fire ticked. She thought of the boat shed on the Theonan coast in the spring, and the thing she had watched him do there on a narrow instruction, and her own satisfaction afterwards, which she had noticed and not liked.

“Yes,” she said. “He’s yours. Begin.”

Cadan inclined his head.

“I will need the instruction before Midwinter,” he said. “Not on the night. Before. I would like to have it in my hands for some days. To be sure I have understood it.”

“You’ll have it.”

“Thank you.” He rose. At the head of the stair he paused. “He has a dog,” he said. “A brindled bitch that sleeps across his door. I mention it because you will want to decide about the dog. I would rather not decide about the dog.”

He went down.

By the end of the week Cadan was in the court every morning.

Riarhin did not see it. She had Amari’s account, from the bridge, where he sold apples very badly. Amari said that on the first morning Guichard had looked at the monk in the grey sleeves standing at the edge of his court and sent a sergeant to ask what he wanted, and the sergeant had come back and said something, and Guichard had come across himself, and they had talked for a long time. On the second morning Cadan had stood in the middle of the court while four of the household’s young men came at him with practice staves, and none of them had touched him, and he had not touched any of them either, until the end, when he had put one of them on the ground so gently that the boy had sat there laughing. On the third morning Guichard had tried it himself.

“He’s good,” said Amari. “Guichard. He went at him properly. The monk let him get closer than the boys. Then he showed him why he shouldn’t have.” Amari turned an apple in his broad fingers. “They talked for an hour after. Sitting on the mounting block, like two men at a fair. The lieutenant was laughing.”

Riarhin said nothing.

“I don’t like it,” said Amari.

“Neither do I.”

He put the apple back in the basket, turning its bruised side down. “I’ll keep watching the bridge.”

Tavin found the boy.

The boy who slept in the little room at the foot of the bell tower was called Jory, and he was twelve, and he was the grandson of the old ringer Tavin had drunk with, and he was very lonely. He was not allowed to leave the little room at night. In the day he had to sweep the chapel and polish the brass. He had nobody to talk to except the rope.

Tavin talked to him. Tavin went in through the chapel on the pretext of a message for the ringer’s grandson from the old man, which was true, and stayed to help polish the brass, which Tavin did extremely well, and came back the next day with a sugared bun from the old man, which was not true, and the day after that with Penny.

“He loves her,” said Tavin, on the fourth evening. He was sitting on the end of the table in the loft with Penny on his wrist, feeding her crumbs. “He’s never had anything that loved him back. She sits on the rope and he tells her about the bell. Every stroke. What it means. How long you ring for arms. What the rope feels like when the bell’s swinging and what it feels like when it isn’t.” He fed Penny another crumb. “He’s very proud of it.”

“And on the night?”

Tavin was quiet for a while. That was unusual.

“On the night there’ll be a boy in a small room with a rope,” he said. “And if anything goes, anything at all, somebody will come running down the tower stair and shout at him, and he’ll pull it. Because he’s twelve and it’s his rope and it’s the one thing he knows how to do.” He looked at Penny. “So on the night, I’ll be in the small room. Before anybody comes running. With the bird. And the door locked from the inside. And the rope—” He stopped. “I’ll see to the rope. I’ll see to it so it can be put right after. I’m not having that boy turned off because his bell didn’t ring.”

“And the boy?”

“The boy’ll be with me,” said Tavin, “telling the bird about the bell.”

Riarhin looked at him.

“That’s not what I asked you to do.”

“You didn’t ask me to do anything yet.” Tavin smiled at her. “I’m telling you what I’m going to do. So when you ask, you’ll know what I’ll say.”

Amari had the bridge.

He said so without being asked, the same evening, after Tavin had gone down.

“The covered bridge,” he said. “From the old range to the new. If the bell doesn’t go, Guichard’s men don’t come out. That’s Tavin’s. If Guichard doesn’t wake them, they don’t come out either. That’s the monk’s.” He did not say Cadan. He never did. “But forty men sleep light before a council. Somebody will hear something. Somebody will get up for a piss and see a light where there shouldn’t be one. And he’ll wake the man next to him. And they’ll come over the bridge without a bell and without a lieutenant, because they’re young and they’ll think they’ll be heroes.”

“And you’ll be on the bridge.”

“On the far side of the bridge. Where it comes into the new wing. There’s a door there. Oak. It opens outward, towards the old range. If somebody holds it shut from the new-wing side, they’ve to break it down to come over, and it’s a good door.” Amari opened his book and looked at nothing in it. “I’ve held a door before. At a pay-tent, once. Six hours. It was a mob and they wanted the chest.”

“Did they get it?”

“No.” He shut the book. “I’ll want Tavin after. When he’s done with the rope. The two of us on the door. That’s better than one.”

“And Saikou?”

Amari looked at her for a long moment.

“You remember what I asked,” he said.

“I remember.”

“Then tell me when you know where he is,” said Amari, “and I’ll tell you whether I can bear it.”

Nessa did not need a placement. She had one.

She went to the old range every evening now, to sing for the kitchen woman’s dying mother, and every evening they sent her down the back stair because the front was being washed, and every evening she passed the foot of the passage on the second floor and said good evening to the man on the stool, and he said good evening back. On the ninth evening he asked her what she had been singing. She sang him a verse. He said his own mother used to sing that one. She said everybody’s mother used to sing that one, and he laughed.

“I know his name now,” she told Riarhin. “And his wife’s. And that he hates the stool.”

“And Berent?”

Nessa was quiet.

Berent could not climb the back stair of the old range fast. He could climb it slowly. He had been practising on the stair of the tannery, up and down, twenty times each morning, with his teeth set and the stick in his left hand and his right hand on the wall, while Aurine watched and said slower and he went faster. He was better. He was not better enough to go up a stair past two men in the dark and bring somebody down it again.

“He’s going,” said Nessa. “Whatever you tell him. You know that.”

“I know.”

“Then put him somewhere he can be,” said Nessa, “and put me with him. I can get a person past a man on a stool. It’s what I’m for. Aveh gets people free.” She pulled her shawl closer. “I can’t get a person up a stair who can’t climb it. That’s the other thing about Aveh. It gets people out. It doesn’t carry them.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“If it goes wrong,” she said. “On the stair. If he’s caught.”

“Then I’ll do what I can.” Nessa said it plainly. “I don’t know yet what that is. I’ll know on the stair.” She stood. “Don’t ask me to promise more. I can’t.”

Chapter Seventeen — The Girl Who Could Not Sleep

The girl was fourteen and she screamed at night, and the women she worked with said there was something in the loom-room.

Aurine heard about it in the apothecary’s shop in the street of the spicers. A woman came in on a Tuesday for a sleeping powder for a child. The apothecary’s hands shook too much to weigh it, so Aurine weighed it, and while she weighed it the woman talked. The child was an apprentice in a weaving household on the river road. She had come from the country in the autumn. Every night since the first frosts she had woken at the same hour screaming that there was a woman at the foot of her bed with her face turned away. The other girls would not sleep in the room with her. The mistress of the household had moved her into a room by herself, and she screamed there too.

“Whose household?” Aurine had asked, while she folded the paper.

“Mistress Margery’s. On the river road.” The woman had taken the powder. “She’s been very good. She sits up with her. Some nights. She doesn’t like it, you can see she doesn’t. She’s frightened of anything like that. Always has been. She sits up anyway.”

Aurine told Riarhin that evening in the loft.

Riarhin sat very still.

She had known Margery for three weeks, a year ago, under another woman’s face.

She had every hour of it. The lamp that had been placed where it fell in a guest’s eyes, and Margery quarrelling with the servant until it was moved. Hester arriving with a collar that would not lie flat, and Margery watching her fuss with it and saying nothing that would make it worse. The evening at the window, with the music in the other room, and Margery’s hand at her wrist asking nothing but leave to stay there. The garden the next afternoon: the late pears wrapped against the birds, Margery changing her shoes on the low wall because a patch of ground under the fruit trees had swallowed one the day before. My sister says I notice everything that is going wrong.

And the apprentices. Riarhin had asked her about them, afterwards, alone, in the week before everything came apart. It had been a sharper question than a guest should ask, and Riarhin had waited for resentment. Margery had given her none. She had answered it, all of it, as though the question were fair and the apprentices were hers to answer for, which they were.

Margery was the queen’s. She had been the queen’s for years, in the way that the fair company were the queen’s: for pleasure and friendship and something more than both. Riarhin had watched Veralyn catch her hand across a room.

She was also a woman who sat up at night with a screaming girl in a loom-room, frightened, because the girl was hers to see to.

“I want Saikou to go,” said Riarhin.

Saikou looked up from his papers.

“To the weaving house,” she said. “As what you are. A scholar from the south who knows about such things. Aurine can tell the apothecary, and the apothecary can tell the woman, and the woman can tell Margery that there’s a man at the tannery who deals in night terrors and women with their faces turned away.”

“It is very likely nothing,” said Saikou. “A girl from the country in a strange house who is homesick and has bad dreams. Most of them are.”

“I know.”

“Then why?”

Riarhin looked at the fire.

“Because on the night,” she said, “when it’s done—if it’s done—there’ll be a child in the new wing. Matylde. She’ll be three. She’ll wake up and her mother and father will be dead or dying and there’ll be blood in the passage and men running, and whoever finds her first will decide what happens to her.” She did not look away from the fire. “I want it to be somebody who sits up with frightened girls when she’s frightened herself. I want it to be somebody who puts the child in front of her own grief. I think Margery is. I want to know.”

Saikou was silent for a long time.

“You are going to send for her,” he said. “On the night.”

“I’m going to send you for her.”

He put down his pen.

“Amari will not like that.”

“I know. I’ll tell him.” Riarhin turned round. “You can get across a yard with a cloth on your head and nobody looks. You told me. Margery lives on the river road, outside the palace. You’d have to go out and come back in, with her, through a household that’s just lost its lieutenant and its captain of the doors. Nobody else can do that. Nobody else would be looked past.”

“With her, I would not be looked past. She would be looked at. She is the queen’s.”

“Then you’ll have to think of something.”

“I shall,” said Saikou, “have to think of something.” He picked up the pen again and looked at it. “Very well. I will go to the weaving house. I will look at the girl. If there is nothing there, I will say so, and I will say it kindly, and I will see what Mistress Margery does when she is told that the thing she has been sitting up afraid of is only a child who misses her mother.” He looked at Riarhin. “And I will not tell her anything else. Not yet.”

“Not yet.”

“She will have to choose to come,” said Saikou. “On the night. I cannot carry her.”

“I know.”

He went three times.

The first time he went with the apothecary, in the afternoon, and looked at the girl, who was a thin frightened creature called Rosine with chilblains on both hands, and asked her a great many questions in his level dry voice about the woman at the foot of the bed. Where she stood. What she wore. Which way her face was turned. Whether she was there when the candle was lit. Rosine answered all of them, crying at first and then not. Margery sat in the corner of the room with her hands in her lap the whole time and did not interrupt.

“She was very frightened,” Saikou said to Riarhin that night. “Margery. Not the girl. The girl was frightened too, but she was used to it. Margery sat in the corner as though the corner might open.”

“Is there anything?”

“I don’t know yet.” He had a sheet of notes. “The woman at the foot of the bed stands where the girl’s mother stood, at home, when she came in to wake her for the milking. Every morning. Rosine told me that without knowing she was telling me. The face is turned away because the mother always turned to the window to see the weather.” He folded the notes. “It is very probably a dream that a homesick girl has at the hour she used to be woken. I will go again. I want to be sure.”

The second time he went at night, and sat up with Rosine and Margery until the hour, with a candle and a bowl of water and a little heap of salt on the sill that he did not explain. At the hour Rosine woke and screamed. Saikou did not move. Margery did. She crossed the room in two strides and got onto the bed and took the girl into her arms and held her, rocking, with her own face white and her eyes on the foot of the bed, and said hush, hush, it’s the milking, your mam’s come for the milking, hush, over and over, until the girl stopped.

The third time he went in the day and told Margery that it was a dream, and why, and what to do about it: let the girl sleep facing the window; wake her yourself, before the hour, and tell her the weather. Margery listened with her hands folded.

“That’s all?” she said.

“That is all I can find. I have been wrong before. I do not think I am wrong about this.”

Margery looked at him for a long time. Saikou said she had a way of looking that went over a man as though he were a lamp that had been put in the wrong place.

“You’re not only a scholar,” she said.

“No.”

“You came here for something else.”

“Yes.”

“Are you going to tell me what?”

“Not today,” said Saikou. “Perhaps not at all. Perhaps on a day when you will not have time to decide whether you like me.” He stood. “If that day comes, I will ask you to come somewhere with me for the sake of a child. I will not ask anything else. You may say no.”

Margery did not answer for a while.

“Whose child?” she said at last.

Saikou did not answer.

He told Riarhin that she had gone quite still, and then looked towards the window, towards the river road and the city and the palace roofs above it, and her face had done something he could not read. Then she had said, very quietly, that he should go now, and that she did not want to know any more, and that if he came to her door on that day she would open it and listen, and she was not promising anything beyond listening.

“She knows,” said Riarhin.

“She knows something is coming. She does not know what.” Saikou sat down. “She knows it is for a child. She did not ask which. I think she did not want to hear herself ask.” He looked at his hands. “You were right about her. I wish you had not been. It would be easier to send for somebody who was only frightened.”

“Would it?”

“For me,” said Saikou. “Not for the child.”

Chapter Eighteen — Apples

Riarhin went out to the bridge on the Feast of Lamps because Amari asked her to.

At the table in the loft the evening before, Amari said that he had sold eleven apples in nine days and that his standing as an apple-seller was the only cover he had and it was wearing thin. He said that a woman sitting beside him on the bridge would make him look like a man with a wife and a stall, instead of a man with a basket and nothing to do, and that the Feast of Lamps would bring half the city across the bridge in procession and he might sell a dozen.

“Two processions,” said Tavin, from the end of the table. “The ringer told me. The Light go round the palace ditch at noon with their lamps, from the chapel of the washerwomen to the old gate and back. The Everflame go round it the other way at noon with their fire in a brazier, from the cathedral close to the old gate and back.” He fed Penny a crumb. “They meet on the bridge. Every year. The ringer says it’s the best thing in Valora after the hangings.”

“Why do they go at the same time?” said Nessa.

“Because the Light say their feast is at noon,” said Tavin, “and the Everflame say it’s theirs, and neither will be the one to move it.”

The bridge over the palace ditch was stone, old, with a hump in the middle and a parapet on either side worn smooth by elbows. It crossed from the lanes of the old town to the gate of the old range, the part of the palace that had been a house before it was a palace, and from the top of the hump you could see down over the ditch wall into the court where the household guard drilled.

Amari had his place at the town end, against the parapet, with the basket at his feet and a folded sack to sit on. He had brought a second sack. He put it down beside his own and Riarhin sat on it, with her scarf over her hair and her hands in her lap, and looked like a woman with a husband and a stall.

It was cold. The sky was the high clear pale it had been all the way from the fen. The ditch below the bridge had a skin of ice at its edges and a swan in the middle of it, turning slowly, as though it could not decide which way to go.

“There,” said Amari, very quietly.

Riarhin looked down into the court.

The household guard were at their drill. There were perhaps thirty of them, young men in dark coats with staves instead of swords, in three lines, stepping forward and back to a voice she could not hear from here. At the edge of the court, by the mounting block, in grey sleeves, with his hands folded, stood Cadan. And walking along the front of the lines, stopping at one man and then another, with a brindled dog at his heel, was a young man with his coat off and his shirtsleeves rolled, who put his hand on a boy’s shoulder and turned him a little and stepped back and said something that made the whole line laugh.

“That’s him,” said Amari.

Riarhin watched him for a long time. She did not need to. She had him the first moment: the height of him, the way he walked, the set of his head, the fair hair cut short. She watched anyway.

He was younger than she had thought. Berent had said the best of them and she had pictured somebody Berent’s age, with Berent’s weight. Guichard was perhaps twenty-three. He walked the lines like somebody who had been told he was good at it and had not yet decided whether to believe it, and every so often he stopped and looked across the court at Cadan, the way a man looks at the clock.

The dog never left his heel.

The Light came first, a little before noon.

Riarhin heard them before she saw them, coming up the lane from the chapel of the washerwomen: a thin high singing, women’s voices mostly, and a bell. Then they came round the corner into the approach to the bridge, and the people in the lane stood back against the walls to let them pass.

There were perhaps sixty of them. They walked two by two, in white over their ordinary clothes, and every one of them carried a lamp: small clay lamps, the kind that cost a farthing in the market, lit and carried in both hands in front of the chest, so that the flames shook with every step. At their head walked a thin man in a grey gown with a book open in his hands, reading aloud from it as he walked, and beside him a boy with the bell.

Riarhin knew the words. She had heard them in Valora the year before, through a chapel door, under another woman’s face. Let the light go before, and we will follow where it shows.

Amari took off his hat.

The Light came up onto the bridge. They were halfway to the hump when the singing faltered, and the thin man with the book stopped reading, and looked up.

From the palace end of the bridge, the Everflame were coming.

They were fewer and louder. They came out through the old gate from the palace side, where the cathedral close backed onto the ditch, perhaps forty men and a few women, in red, with a great iron brazier carried on poles between four of them, heaped with burning coals that roared in the cold air and threw sparks up into the sky. At their head walked a heavy man in a black gown with a silver chain of office round his neck, and a staff, and an expression of a man who has walked this way every year for thirty years and expects the bridge to be empty when he gets to it.

The bridge was not empty.

The two processions stopped, facing each other, a dozen paces apart, on either side of the hump. The singing stopped. The bell stopped. The brazier went on roaring. Along both parapets the people of the lanes, who had come for exactly this, leaned forward.

Nobody moved.

“There’s room,” Riarhin said quietly. “If they went single file.”

“There’s room,” said Amari. “There’s always room. That’s not the question.”

The thin man with the book spoke first. He spoke in a clear, reasonable, carrying voice, the voice of a man who has been making the same argument in the same place for a long time and has got it very polished. He said that the Feast of Lamps was the Light’s by right of the calendar of Saint Aubrey, which set it at the noon of the ninth day before Midwinter, and that the procession of the Light had kept that hour on that bridge since before the palace had a ditch, and that the Everflame were welcome to follow it, after, as they had been welcome every year, and had never once accepted.

The heavy man in the black gown said that the calendar of Saint Aubrey had been superseded by the Council of Tarlington in the old king’s time, and that the Council had set the feast of the Kindling at noon on the same day, and that a superseded calendar conferred no right of precedence on any bridge in the realm, and that he would cite the canon if the Light required it.

“Cite it,” said the thin man.

The heavy man cited it.

The thin man cited another.

They went on. It was, Riarhin realised after a while, courteous on both sides. Neither of them raised his voice. Neither of them moved. They stood on either side of the hump of the bridge with their processions behind them and cited canons at each other in turn, the thin man from his book and the heavy man from memory, and the people on the parapets counted them under their breath. Beside her an old woman with a basket of eggs was holding up her fingers, one after another.

At seven the old woman stopped holding up fingers and put her hand over her mouth.

At eight the thin man with the book stopped.

He had the next canon ready. Riarhin could see it: the book open at the page, his finger on the line, his mouth open to speak. He looked at the page. He looked at the heavy man. He shut his mouth, and closed the book on his finger, and stood there.

The heavy man had stopped too.

Along the parapet the people let out their breath. The old woman with the eggs took her hand off her mouth and touched her brow twice, once with an open hand in the fashion of the Light and once with a closed one in the fashion of the Everflame, and looked round as though daring anybody to say anything about it.

Nobody on the bridge said anything at all. The brazier roared. The lamps shook.

It was a boy who broke it.

He was in the Everflame procession, near the front, one of the four carrying the brazier: nineteen perhaps, big, red in the face from the heat of the coals. He set down his end of the pole so suddenly that the brazier lurched and the men at the other poles staggered, and sparks went up in a shower, and a woman in the front of the Light cried out and put her hand to her lamp.

“It’s ours,” said the boy. His voice cracked. “It’s our hour. We’ve stood here every year since my grandfather and every year we let them go first because they got here first. They get here first because they leave early. Everybody knows it.” He came forward a step, past the heavy man, who put out a hand to stop him and did not. “I’ll stand for it. If they’ll put somebody up. On the bridge. Now.” He looked along the white-clad line of the Light, at the women with their farthing lamps. “Or are there only women.”

There was a young man in the Light’s procession. He was near the back, thin, pale, in white over a clerk’s black coat. He set down his lamp on the parapet very carefully, so it would not go out.

“I’ll stand,” he said.

The people on the parapets began to talk all at once.

“Oh, no,” said Amari, very quietly.

The gate of the old range opened.

Riarhin did not see who opened it. She saw the dog first: the brindled bitch, coming out through the gate at a trot with her head low, and stopping at the palace end of the bridge, and standing there with her legs braced looking at the brazier. Then Guichard came out behind her.

He had put his coat back on. He had not had time to do it up. He had four of his young men behind him with their staves, and he walked out onto the bridge between the two processions at an ordinary pace, as though he had come out to see what the noise was, and stopped on the hump of the bridge exactly in the middle, and looked at the boy from the Everflame and the clerk from the Light.

“Good morning,” he said.

Nobody answered.

“It’s the Queen’s bridge,” said Guichard. He said it pleasantly. “I don’t know if anybody’s mentioned. The ditch is the Queen’s and the bridge goes over it and it’s mine to keep while she’s away, and she’ll be home within the week. I’d like to be able to tell her it’s still standing.” He looked at the boy. “You want to stand for your hour.”

“Yes.”

“That’s your right. There’s a court for it. Behind the Hall. It opens after the feast. You put your name in the book and they put his in and a justiciar sets the day.” He looked at the clerk. “You’ll want to put your name in too.”

“Yes,” said the clerk. He had gone paler.

“Good. That’s done, then.” Guichard turned round, slowly, and looked at the two processions, the white and the red, with the hump of the bridge between them. “Now the other thing. It’s past noon. You’ve both lost your hour standing here citing canons at each other, so neither of you has it. That’s a pity. It’s cold.” He looked up at the sky, which was pale and clear. “There’s room on this bridge for two men abreast. There always has been. I’ve measured it. The Light go over on the ditch side, because they got here first and they’ve got lamps that go out in a wind and the ditch side’s out of the wind. The Everflame go over on the town side, at the same time, because they’ve got a fire that doesn’t go out in anything and they don’t need the shelter.” He looked at the heavy man in the black gown. “You’d be crossing together, Canon. On the Queen’s bridge. In the Queen’s peace. I don’t think either calendar has anything to say about that. I looked.”

The heavy man looked at him for a long moment.

“You looked,” he said.

“Last year. After last year.” Guichard smiled. He had a boy’s smile, sudden and wide. “My men were fishing lamps out of the ditch till dark.”

Somebody on the parapet laughed. It was the old woman with the eggs.

The thin man with the book opened it again, and found a place, and read, in a clear voice, let the light go before, and we will follow where it shows, and began to walk. His procession came after him, two by two, on the ditch side of the bridge, with their lamps held in both hands. The heavy man lifted his staff, and the four men took up the brazier again, the big red-faced boy among them, not looking at anybody, and the Everflame came on over the hump on the town side of the bridge at the same pace, with their fire roaring and their sparks going up.

They passed each other on the hump. They did not look at each other. A spark from the brazier fell on the sleeve of a woman in white and she brushed it off without breaking step. The dog stood at the palace end and watched them all go by.

It took perhaps a minute. Then the bridge was clear, and the Light were going away down the lane towards the old gate, singing, and the Everflame were going away along the ditch towards the cathedral close, with their fire, and the people on the parapets were going home, talking.

Guichard stood on the hump for a moment after they had gone, with his hands on his hips and his coat undone, and let out a long breath that smoked in the cold.

Then he turned round and saw Amari’s basket.

He came down the town side of the bridge towards them. The dog came with him. His four young men stayed on the hump, leaning on their staves, grinning at each other. Riarhin kept her hands in her lap and her eyes on the basket.

“How much?” said Guichard.

Amari told him.

“For one?”

“For one.”

“That’s robbery.” Guichard crouched down by the basket and began to pick through it. He picked the way a man picks who grew up in a house with an orchard, turning each apple over, pressing it with his thumb near the stalk, smelling it. “Late apples. Somebody stored these well. Straw and a cold loft.” He chose four. “Four. For my lads. They stood on that bridge for me without knowing why and they’ll want paying.” He chose a fifth, small and hard and yellow, and held it out to the dog, who took it very delicately in her front teeth and lay down with it on the cold stones between her paws. “And one for Bel.”

“Five,” said Amari.

Guichard paid him. He paid the whole price, without arguing, and then added a coin, and stood up.

He looked at Riarhin.

It was the look a young man gives a woman sitting beside an apple-seller on a feast day, and it went over her scarf and her face and her hands in her lap and came back up again, and did not stop anywhere.

“You were counting,” he said.

Riarhin looked up.

“On the bridge. When they were citing.” He smiled. “Everybody was. Half the lane was. I could see their fingers from the gate.” He shifted the apples in his arms. “Did you get to nine?”

“They stopped at eight.”

“They always stop at eight.” He said it lightly. “Every year. The Canon and the Reader. Thirty years, the ringer says. Somebody told me once that the ninth canon voids the eight before it and nobody’s ever brave enough to cite it and see which eight.” He shrugged. “I don’t know. I’m not a lawyer. I measured the bridge.”

He gave Amari a nod, and whistled, and the dog got up with the apple still in her mouth and trotted after him, back up over the hump and across to the gate of the old range, where his four young men were waiting. He gave them an apple each. Riarhin heard one of them say something, and Guichard laugh, and then the gate closed behind them.

Amari sat on his sack and did not say anything for some time.

The swan in the ditch had made up its mind and was drifting slowly towards the palace end, where the ice was thinnest. Down in the court the lines had broken up; the guard were standing about in groups, eating apples, and Cadan was still at the mounting block with his hands folded.

“Bel,” said Amari at last.

“I heard.”

“He named the dog Bel.” Amari picked up an apple and turned it over. “He bought five apples at robbery and he paid a coin over and he gave one to the dog.” He put the apple back. “I ran a pay-tent for an army for nine years. I paid out to perhaps six thousand men. I’d say one in fifty was like that. One in fifty paid a coin over.”

Riarhin said nothing.

“The ones like that,” said Amari, “you remember. Not their names. I never knew their names; I knew their marks. You remember that one of them paid over, and what his mark was, and when you get the roll after a battle you look for the mark.” He was quiet a moment. “I got good at not looking for it. You have to. Or you can’t do the pay.”

The court below had begun to empty. The young men were going in through the door of the old range, in twos and threes, still eating. Guichard was standing by the mounting block talking to Cadan. As Riarhin watched, Cadan said something, and Guichard put his head back and laughed, and clapped Cadan on the shoulder, and Cadan inclined his head.

“I don’t want to know his mark,” said Amari. “I don’t want to know his dog’s name. I told you I didn’t like it. I’ve told you every day.” He looked at her. “Why did you come?”

“To see him.”

“You’d seen him. You saw him from up here before he’d crossed the court.” Amari was not angry. He said it the way he said everything, flat and steady, with his broad hands on his knees. “You wanted to see him close. You wanted to see him buy an apple.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

Riarhin looked down into the court. Guichard had gone in. Cadan was still standing by the mounting block, alone now, with his hands folded, looking at nothing.

“Because Cadan’s going to do it,” she said. “And I told him to. And I wanted to have seen what I told him to do.”

Amari did not say anything for a long while.

“That’s fair,” he said at last. “I’ll give you that. That’s fair.” He began to pack the apples back into the basket, the good ones on top. “It won’t help.”

“I know.”

“I know you know. I’m saying it so it’s said.” He lifted the basket and stood up. “You can’t say after that nobody said.”

They walked back towards the tannery through the lanes of the old town, where the Light’s lamps had left a trail of spilt oil on the cobbles and the Everflame’s brazier had left a trail of ash, crossing and recrossing each other, all the way to the old gate. Children were following the ash with sticks, drawing in it.

At the corner by the chapel of the washerwomen there was a board on the wall, fresh-painted, with a notice nailed to it. Riarhin stopped to read it because a knot of people was standing in front of it and she wanted to know why.

It was from the Hall. It was very formal. It said that the court of honour behind the Hall of Dawning would open on the second day after the feast of Midwinter, and would hear such challenges as had been entered in its book, and that any man who wished to enter a challenge should do so before the Hall’s clerk at the Law Quarter before the eve of Midwinter, and not after. Beneath it somebody had written in chalk, already, two names: one with a flame drawn beside it and one with a little lamp.

“They’ve entered already,” said a woman in the crowd. “The Canon’s boy and the Reader’s clerk. My sister saw them go up.”

“Somebody ought to stop them.”

“Who? The lieutenant told them to.”

“He told them to go to the court instead of fighting on the bridge,” said another woman. “That’s not the same.”

“It’s the same by the time it gets to the court,” said the first woman, and they all laughed, a little, and began to argue about who would win.

Riarhin stood looking at the two chalked names. The second day after Midwinter. She thought of the court behind the Hall, where she had never been, and a justiciar with a book, and a boy of nineteen red in the face from the heat of a brazier, and a pale clerk who had set his lamp down on the parapet so carefully that it did not go out.

“Come on,” said Amari, beside her.

She came on.

Cadan came back to the tannery at dusk. He came up the stair quietly, as he did everything, and sat down at the table, and folded his hands, and Riarhin, who was sitting at the other end with a candle and nothing to do with it, looked at him.

“You saw the bridge,” he said.

“Yes.”

“He was pleased with himself,” said Cadan. “Afterwards. In the court. He said he had been waiting a year to do that. He said last year he had not known what to do and had stood on the hump shouting at them and they had ignored him, and somebody had pushed a woman into the ditch, and he had gone in after her in his boots.” Cadan turned one hand over on the table. “He asked me whether I thought it had been well done. The bridge. This year.”

“What did you tell him?”

“That it had been very well done.” Cadan looked at the candle. “That was true. I did not have to find another answer.”

Riarhin said nothing.

“He has asked me to eat with the men on the eve of Midwinter,” said Cadan. “After the evening office, before the vigil. It is their custom. The lieutenant sits at the head of the table in the old range and carves, and then he walks his round, and then they all go to the chapel together. He says a teacher ought to eat with the men he has taught at least once.” He looked up. “I said I would think about it.”

“Will you go?”

“No,” said Cadan. “You have told me where to be. I will be there.” He was quiet a moment. “I wanted you to know that he asked. So that it is not a thing I carry alone.”

He stood up, and inclined his head to her, and went down the stair.

Riarhin sat at the table with the candle until it guttered. Then she got up and went to the hoist door and opened it, and stood in the cold looking out over the black river towards the palace, where the old range stood up dark against the stars on the far side of the ditch, with one window lit on the ground floor, low down, where forty young men slept in what had once been the stables of a house.

Chapter Nineteen — The Second Volume

The University had given its great court to the council, and there were tents in it.

Riarhin had not expected that. She had been to the University once, a year before, under another woman’s face, with Lyra, to sit in Master Ridow’s garden while he disputed the name of a plant, and she had it as it had been then: a square of old grey buildings round a lawn, with a well in the middle and a clock over the gate and students crossing the grass in black gowns with their arms full of books. The buildings were the same. The clock was the same. The lawn had gone under canvas.

There were perhaps thirty tents, pitched in rows, with banners on poles at their doors: the retinues of the lords who were coming to the Midwinter council and had more men than the city’s inns would hold. The banners hung stiff in the cold. Between the rows grooms were leading horses to the well, and a cook had set up a fire where the students had once crossed the lawn, and somebody’s steward was arguing with the University’s porter about who would pay for the turf.

“The council sits in eight days,” said Saikou, beside her. “Everybody who is anybody has sent his harbingers ahead to take the best ground. The ones who come first get the colleges. The ones who come last get the lawn.” He looked at the banners. “I count four dukes and two bishops. The Fenwood banners are all at the east end, together. The western lords are at the west end, together. Nobody from either end has pitched in the middle.”

“What’s in the middle?”

“The well,” said Saikou. “And the porter.”

Master Ridow had rooms in the old tower at the corner of the court, up a stair so narrow that Saikou had to go sideways. Riarhin went behind him with her scarf over her hair and her hands in her sleeves, as a woman goes behind a scholar she is serving.

It had been Saikou’s idea. He had said that a Kahfagian scholar with letters from his mother’s house could ask for a master of tongues at the University about a Kahfagian grammar, and be taken to him, and nobody would think it strange; he had done something like it already at the chancery. He had said that a woman with a sword on her back who had once been a redhead could not. He had said, with some care, that he did not think she should come at all.

She had come. It was her letter Ridow had not answered.

The door at the top of the stair was open. There were voices behind it, two of them. One was old and thin and very tired. The other was deep and warm and amused, and was saying, as Saikou knocked on the open door, “—and if the seal was put on under the household’s authority, Master, and not the chancery’s, then it was put on without the Chancellor’s writ, and a seal without a writ is a piece of wax, and I will tell the household so to its face, with pleasure, in any court it likes.”

“Come in,” said the thin voice.

It was a round room, lined with books from floor to ceiling, with a window that looked out over the tents. There was a fire, not much of one, and a desk under the window heaped with papers, and in a chair by the fire an old man in a scholar’s gown with a rug over his knees.

He was very small. He had white hair that stood up round his head like thistledown and a face creased all over, like paper that has been folded and unfolded many times, and he was holding a cup in both hands without drinking from it.

The other man was standing by the bookcases. He was large, handsome, perhaps fifty, in a black advocate’s gown with a fur collar, and he had one hand resting on the front of a tall press against the wall. The press had doors. Across the doors, where they met, somebody had laid a strip of red tape and sealed it at both ends with a great blob of wax, and pressed a seal into the wax. Riarhin knew the seal. She had seen it on the doors of the rooms in the palace where the young men in dark coats kept their papers.

“Master Ridow,” said Saikou, and bowed. “Forgive me. I am told you are the master of the southern tongues. I am Saikou Ira, of Kahfagia, and I have come about a grammar.”

The old man looked at him. Then he looked at the press with the seal on it. Then he looked at the advocate.

The advocate laughed.

“About a grammar,” he said. “Of course you have. Everybody has come about a grammar this autumn. I’ve been here since noon about a grammar.” He came forward and held out his hand. “Halric. I argue for the University when it has something to argue about. It has, at present, a great deal.” He shook Saikou’s hand and looked at him with bright, interested eyes. “Ira. The House Ira? In Kahfagia?”

“My mother’s house.”

“I argued against your mother’s factor once. In the matter of a salt cargo at Fairport. I lost.” Halric seemed delighted by the memory. “She had a precedent from a Kahfagian court I had never heard of and could not find, and I still think she made it up, and I could never prove it.” He looked at Riarhin, briefly, standing behind Saikou with her eyes down. His look went over her and did not stop. “Well. I’ll leave you to your grammar. Master, I’ll come on the second day after the feast and we’ll see about this wax.” He tapped the seal on the press with one finger. “It’s the principle that matters. A master’s books are the master’s. The charter says so. It said so before the Queen’s grandfather was breeched.”

He went out. They heard him going down the narrow stair, sideways, humming.

Ridow did not say anything until the humming had gone.

Then he said, without looking at either of them, “It’s in there.”

He lifted one thin hand and pointed at the sealed press.

“The second volume,” he said. “Brown cloth. The spine torn. She tore it herself, on the corner of a desk in the chancery, three years ago, getting it out of her satchel in a hurry. She was very cross.” His hand came down onto the rug. “It’s in there with the others. Every Kahfagian book I have. They came at the end of the summer. Four young men in dark coats and a clerk. They asked what books I had lent to the woman Lyra, and what books she had asked for since, and I told them, because they asked, and they took a list of my southern shelf and put everything on it into that press and sealed it.” He looked at the seal. “For the reconsideration of her charge, they said. In case the books were evidence.”

“Evidence of what?” said Saikou.

“They didn’t say. I don’t think they knew.” Ridow turned the cup in his hands. “I think somebody had been told to find out what she was reading, and they found out, and they sealed it, and somebody wrote that down somewhere, and now it is a thing that has been done, and nobody will undo it because nobody remembers why it was done. Halric thinks he can undo it. Halric thinks everything can be undone by arguing. He is usually right. It takes him years.”

Riarhin stood behind Saikou and looked at the press. It was oak, and old, and the seal across its doors was the size of a child’s palm. The book was a hand’s width from her on the other side of the wood.

She could open it. She could see how. The hinges were on the outside. The pins were brass, and old, and would come out with a knife point and a little patience, and the doors would come off with the seal still whole across them, and go back on after.

She thought about the four young men in dark coats, and the list, and the man who would come after the feast to see about the wax and find the seal whole and the book gone.

She did not move.

“You wrote to me,” said Ridow.

He was looking at her. Not at Saikou. He had been looking at her, she realised, since he pointed at the press.

“At the end of the summer,” he said. “From Theona. In a small slanting hand. The left hand, I thought, from the slope of it. You said she had asked for the second volume of the grammar. You said she was in the middle of a sentence. You said you would be grateful if I could see it went to her by the woman who carried her food.” His voice was very thin. “You didn’t sign it. You didn’t need to. There aren’t many people in Theona who know Lyra, and write left-handed, and would think to tell me about the sentence.”

Riarhin lowered her scarf.

Saikou did not move. He had gone still in the way he went still when a thing had happened that he had not planned for and was deciding whether to mind.

“Yes,” said Riarhin. “I wrote to you.”

“I didn’t answer.”

“No.”

“I want you to know why.” Ridow set the cup down on the hearth beside his chair. His hands, without it, did not know what to do; he folded them on the rug and unfolded them again. “Your letter came in the week they sealed the press. It came from Theona. The week before, the Theonan envoy had walked out of the chancery with a hawthorn branch in his hand and said the islands would send no levy to the Queen’s spring muster, not one man, not one ship, and the whole city was talking of Theonan treachery. And here was a letter from Theona, in a hand I did not know, unsigned, about a prisoner of the household and her Kahfagian books.”

He stopped.

“I burned it,” he said. “I read it three times and I burned it in that grate. And I wrote you an answer and burned that. And another. I was frightened. I am an old man and I have never been anything but a teacher of grammar and I was very frightened, and I told myself that she would understand, because she is a sensible woman and would not want an old man hanged for a book.” He looked at the press. “I told myself that every day. I told myself that she would not want me to. I don’t know if it is true. I have never asked her.”

Riarhin did not say anything.

“So there,” said Ridow. “That is why. You came all the way up my stair to hear it and now you have heard it.” His face did something that was not quite a smile. “You may tell her, if you see her. I would like her to know it was fear and not forgetting. I don’t know why that matters. It does.”

It was Saikou who spoke first.

“Master Ridow,” he said, “when the young men came, and asked what books you had lent her. Did you tell them about the second volume?”

Ridow looked at him.

“It was on the list,” he said. “It was the first thing on the list.”

“And when they put the books in the press.” Saikou’s voice was mild, patient, a scholar asking about a text. “Did you watch them? Did you see the second volume go in?”

Ridow did not answer.

He sat in his chair by the fire with the rug over his knees and his thin hands on the rug, and he looked at Saikou for a long time. Then he looked at the press with the seal across its doors. Then he looked at the window, where the banners of four dukes and two bishops hung stiff over the tents on the lawn.

“There is a man in Kahfagia,” said Saikou gently, “who has written a commentary on the second volume. He disagrees with it on every page. He is a very tedious man. I have been trying to find a copy of the second volume for some years in order to decide which of them I dislike more.” He paused. “If a master of the University were to lend a copy to a visiting scholar, for his studies, for a season, that would be a courtesy between the University and House Ira. It would not be a thing any seal was put on to prevent. The seal is on that press. It is not on your courtesy.”

Ridow was quiet.

Then he put his hand down under the rug on his knees, and brought it up again, and there was a book in it.

It was bound in brown cloth gone dark at the edges with handling, and the spine had been torn, near the head, and mended with a strip of linen tape that had been glued down badly and was lifting at one end. Ridow held it in both hands, the way he had held the cup.

“I took it out of the pile,” he said. “When they were writing the list. I put it on my chair and sat on it. It was the only brave thing I did all year and it was sitting down.” He did not look up. “I didn’t know why. I thought, she’s in the middle of a sentence. I thought, if they put it in the press, and she comes out, and asks for it, it’ll be in the press, and somebody will have to argue for three years to get it out, and she’ll have forgotten the sentence.” He turned the book over. “I’ve kept it under this rug since. They’ve been back twice. They never looked at the chair. Nobody looks at an old man’s knees.”

He held it out. Not to Saikou.

Riarhin came forward and took it.

It was lighter than she had thought. She held it in both hands. The cloth was soft and a little greasy with handling, and the torn place on the spine caught at her thumb.

There was a slip of paper in it, a third of the way through. She could see its end sticking out from between the pages: a narrow strip, cut from the edge of a larger sheet, with writing on it.

She did not open the book. She stood in the round room with it in her hands and looked at the end of the paper, and she knew the writing on it without having to see more than three letters of it. It was the hand of the woman who had sat at the table in the room above the seamstress’s shop with the spilled water on it, turning her cup. It was a clerk’s hand, small and upright and clear, the hand of somebody who had spent years writing so that nobody afterwards could misread a figure.

“Her place,” said Ridow. “It’s marked. I haven’t moved it. I haven’t read it. I didn’t think it was mine to read.”

“Thank you.”

“Don’t.” He picked up his cup again. “Don’t thank me. I sat on it. Take it to her.” He looked at her over the cup. “You will take it to her. That’s why you’ve come. Not to hear why I didn’t answer.”

“Yes.”

“How?”

Riarhin did not answer.

Ridow looked at her for a long moment, and at the sword-hilt over her shoulder, which her scarf did not hide, and at Saikou, standing by the door with his hands in his sleeves.

“No,” he said. “Don’t tell me. I’d only have to not say it to somebody.” He drank from the cup at last. It must have been cold. He made a face. “Go down the back stair. There’s a door into the lane behind the tower. The front stair goes past the porter, and the porter will want to know whose woman carried a book out of the tower on the week of the council, and he’ll ask Halric, and Halric will think it the most interesting thing that’s happened all year.” He set the cup down. “And he’ll be right.”

They went down the back stair. It was narrower than the front and smelled of mice and candle-ends. At the bottom there was a door with a bar, and beyond it a lane between the tower and the wall of the next college, with frost still on the cobbles where the sun had not reached.

Saikou walked a little ahead of her, not speaking. At the end of the lane, where it came out into the street beyond the University wall, he stopped.

“You took your scarf down,” he said.

“He knew me.”

“He knew your letter. He did not know your face.” Saikou did not turn round. “Now he knows both. And he knows mine, and my mother’s name, which Halric also knows, and has argued against in a salt case, and lost, and minds.” He was quiet a moment. “I am not saying it was wrong. I am saying what it was.”

“I know what it was.”

“Yes.” He turned then, and looked at her for a moment with his grave face, and did not say whatever it was he had been going to say.

He began to walk again. After a moment she walked beside him.

Berent was at the ropewalk, on the deaf woman’s bed, with his leg up and his eyes shut, when she came in. He opened them when he heard the door.

She did not say anything. She sat down on the end of the bed and held out the book.

He looked at it for a moment without understanding. Then he sat up, too fast, and swore at his leg, and took it from her in both hands.

He turned it over. He found the torn spine with his thumb, the linen tape lifting at one end, and ran his thumb along it, and was still.

“Ridow,” he said.

“He sat on it,” said Riarhin. “Since the summer. They sealed the rest.”

Berent did not ask what she meant. He was looking at the slip of paper sticking out from the pages. He did not touch it. He looked at the three letters of Lyra’s hand that showed at its edge, and his face did a thing that Riarhin had seen it do only once before, across a candle in this room, when she said Lyra’s name to him.

“Can I carry it?” he said. “On the night. I’ll have the sword and the stick, I know. I’ll put it inside my coat. It’s not heavy.”

“You’ll need both hands.”

“I know I’ll need both hands.” He did not look up from the book. “I want her to have it on the stair. Not after. Not when we’re back here and there’s time. On the stair, when she’s coming down, I want to be able to say, I’ve got your book.”

Riarhin sat on the end of the bed.

“No,” she said. “It stays here. At the tannery. On the table, by the fire. When you bring her in through the gap in the wall, it’ll be here. The first thing she sees.”

Berent looked up at her.

“That’s not the same,” he said.

“No.”

He looked at her a while longer. Then he looked back down at the book in his hands, and turned it over once more, and held it out to her.

She did not take it.

“Keep it till the last day,” she said. “Here. Then give it to me and I’ll take it to the tannery.”

He drew it back. He put it on the chest beside the bed, beside the candle, squarely, with its torn spine towards him. Then he lay back on the bed with his arm over his eyes.

Riarhin went out and shut the door. On the stair she stopped, and stood for a moment in the cold, and heard through the door behind her a sound she could not at first place: a page, turning. Then another. Then nothing, for a long time, as though somebody had found the place where a slip of paper was and was sitting with it open on his knee, not reading it.

Chapter Twenty — In the Book

Riarhin went to find Maheut because she owed her an apology, and she knew while she was going that it was not the only reason.

A woman who kept the Queen’s linen book lived where the palace laundresses lived, in the streets below the west wall where the washing yards were, and there were only so many women with forearms like a baker’s and a boy of eleven and a mother who could not get down the stairs. Riarhin asked at the second yard. A girl pointed. The house was narrow and tall, three storeys over a chandler’s, with the stair on the outside, and on the stair, halfway up, sat an old woman in a shawl with her hands on her knees, not moving.

Riarhin stopped at the foot of the stair.

The old woman looked down at her.

“You can’t get past,” she said. “I’m resting.”

“How long have you been resting?”

The old woman considered this.

“Since the bell,” she said. “The noon bell. My daughter’s at the palace and the boy’s at his lessons and I wanted to see the street. I got down this far.” She looked at the eight steps below her. “Then I thought about the rest.”

Riarhin went up the eight steps and sat down beside her.

She took her down in the end. It took a long time. The old woman’s knees did not bend and her feet did not trust the treads, and Riarhin went one step below her all the way with her back to the street and the old woman’s hands on her shoulders, taking the weight, one step and a rest, one step and a rest, while the old woman told her about the street as it had been fifty years ago, when there were no palace laundries and the washing was done in the river and the women stood in it to the knee and sang.

At the bottom there was a bench by the chandler’s door. Riarhin put her on it. The old woman sat in the thin winter sun with her eyes shut and her face turned up.

“That’s the street,” she said. “Thank you. I can see it from here.”

Maheut found them there an hour later, coming home at the end of her day with a basket of mending on her hip, and stopped dead in the street with the basket slipping.

She looked at her mother. Then she looked at Riarhin.

“You,” she said.

“She was on the stair,” said Riarhin. “She wanted to see the street.”

“She was on the stair since noon,” said the old woman, without opening her eyes. “This one took me down. She’s very slow. I liked her.”

Maheut put the basket down.

They took her back up together. Maheut went in front and Riarhin went behind, and between them it went faster, and at the top Maheut got her mother onto a chair by the window, where she could see the street after all, and put a shawl round her and a cup in her hands, and then came out onto the stair head where Riarhin was waiting and shut the door behind her.

“Why are you here?”

“To say I was sorry. For the stair. Your stair. At the palace.”

Maheut folded her arms.

“You heard there was work.”

“I lied,” said Riarhin. “I wanted to see inside. I’m sorry I did it with Aude’s basket. I’m sorry I did it on your stair. I didn’t know about the cup. I didn’t know about the woman before you.”

Maheut looked at her.

“What did you want to see inside for?”

“I used to know somebody there.”

“Who?”

Riarhin did not answer. Maheut’s face changed, a little, the way faces change when somebody has decided not to press.

“You’re not a thief,” she said.

“No.”

“You’re not a laundress either. Look at your hands.” Maheut took one of them and turned it over. The palm had a callus across the base of the fingers where a sword-grip sat, and another on the side of the thumb. Maheut looked at it, and let it go. “Soldier’s hands. Or something.”

“Something.”

“Well.” Maheut unfolded her arms. “You took my mother down the stair. That’s more than the boy’s father ever did in eleven years.” She looked at the door behind her, where her mother was sitting at the window with her cup. “She says you were slow.”

“I was.”

“She liked that. Everybody else is quick with her. She hates it.” Maheut rubbed her forearm. “Come in. There’s soup. It’s not much.”

Riarhin went back four times before Midwinter.

She told herself it was to see the old woman, who was called Grand Isolde by the whole street and had opinions about everyone in it. It was partly that. She took her down the stair twice more, on fine days, and sat with her on the bench by the chandler’s while Grand Isolde told her who had married whom in the washing yards in the year of the great flood, and who should not have. She met the boy, Maheut’s son, who was eleven and serious and learning his letters at a charity school behind the chapel of the washerwomen, and who showed her his slate with the alphabet on it and asked her whether she could read. She said she could. He asked her to prove it. She read him the alphabet backwards, and he looked at her with deep suspicion, and asked her to do it again, and she did.

And she learned the linen.

She did not ask. She did not need to. Maheut talked about her work the way everybody in the street talked about their work, at the end of the day, over soup, because it was the thing that had filled the day and was still in her. The Queen’s linen went up the stair every morning and came down every evening. The Consort’s went up with it, and the little one’s. On feast days there was more; on the eve of Midwinter there was the most of all, because the Queen kept the vigil in her own rooms that night with her household, and every bed in the new wing was stripped at the ninth bell while the household was at the chapel for the evening office, and made up fresh for the feast, and it had to be done in the hour between the office and the vigil, and it took twelve women.

“I have nine,” said Maheut, on the third evening, with her head in her hands. “Aude’s still coughing. Jehanne’s girl has the measles. Mariot’s gone home to her sister’s lying-in and won’t be back till the new year. Nine women and twelve women’s work in an hour, and if one bed isn’t made when the Queen comes up from the chapel it’s my name.”

Riarhin sat across the table with her soup.

She did not say anything. She did not need to. She could feel it coming, the way you feel a step in the dark before your foot finds it. She could have stood up and gone. She could have said she was leaving the city before Midwinter. She sat with the spoon in her hand and let it come.

Maheut looked up.

“You took my mother down the stair,” she said slowly. “You’re slow and careful and you do what you’re told and you don’t talk.” She looked at Riarhin’s hands on the table. “Can you make a bed?”

“Yes.”

“A proper bed. Corners. The way they like it.”

“I learned in a palace,” said Riarhin, which was true.

Maheut was quiet for a long time.

“It’d be one night,” she said. “Midwinter eve. The ninth bell to the tenth. I’d put you in the book. Under my name. As hired for the night. I’d have to write your name.” She looked at Riarhin steadily. “What is your name?”

Riarhin had known this was coming too. She had known it on the stair with Grand Isolde’s hands on her shoulders.

She thought of a well in Theona, and a woman at the edge of it saying she says Aine, and a slate at the Hall with a name on it that had not been hers and had been. She thought of a young man in a tarred cap on the shingle at Scarrow asking who was answerable, and her own voice saying I am.

“Aine,” she said.

Maheut wrote it on the back of her hand with the pen from behind her ear, so as not to forget it.

“Aine,” she said. “Midwinter eve. The ninth bell. Come to the west gate with the carts and say your name to the guard, and I’ll have told him.” She looked at the name on her hand. “Don’t make me sorry.”

Riarhin walked back to the tannery by the river in the dark.

She walked slowly. She did not want to get there. She wanted to walk along the river for the rest of the night and not arrive anywhere where there was a table and a fire and seven people waiting to hear what she had found.

She had found the way in.

It was the best way in there could be. Midwinter eve, the ninth bell, the household at the chapel and the new wing empty of everybody but the women making the beds. Up the linen stair with a basket, past a woman with a book who would look at her and see Aine and look away. Into the Queen’s own rooms, with clean sheets over her arm, in the hour before the Queen came up from the chapel.

And Maheut’s name on the line.

Riarhin looked at it for a long time. Then she went up the stair into the loft, where the others were waiting, and told them she had the way in, and how, and whose name was on the line. She told them all of it. She did not let herself leave out the soup.

Chapter Twenty-One — The Door Song

The Valora heap ran out six days before Midwinter, and Nessa said she would sing for it.

Amari told them at the table in the loft, with the book open. He did not make anything of it. He read out what had gone: the tannery, three weeks in advance and a fourth; the deaf woman’s room; the ropewalk; Aurine’s place in the pepper cart, which the carter had put up twice since the first price because he had heard the gates might be watched after the feast and wanted paying for his nerves; bread; lamp oil; an apron and a scarf for Riarhin such as a laundress’s help would wear; a great deal of cider bought in the old town for a deaf bell-ringer. He read it all in his flat, steady voice, and then he turned the book round so they could see the last line, and there was very little under it.

“And the other heap?” said Tavin.

“Is the other heap,” said Amari. “For whoever wants to go home. Nobody touches it for this. We said.”

“We did say,” said Tavin, sadly.

“Six days,” said Riarhin. “It’s enough for six days.”

“It’s enough for six days if nothing goes wrong,” said Amari. “Something always goes wrong. Something has to be paid for when it does. I’d like to have something to pay it with.” He closed the book. “I’m not asking you to find money. I’m telling you there isn’t any.”

Nobody said anything for a while. The river went by under the hoist door.

“I’ll sing,” said Nessa.

They all looked at her.

“In the Queen’s square,” she said. “Tomorrow. It’s market day, and they’re building the stands for the entry, and there’ll be half the city there to watch the carpenters. I’ve been singing in a kitchen to a dying woman for a month. I’d like to sing in a square before—” She stopped. “Before. Tavin can pass the hat. He’d like that.”

“I would like that,” said Tavin.

“No,” said Riarhin.

Nessa looked at her across the table.

“You’re in the old range every evening,” said Riarhin. “The man on the stool knows your face. If you stand in the Queen’s square and sing for money in front of half the city, somebody will know it who shouldn’t.”

“Somebody always knows it,” said Nessa. “That’s the thing about my face. Somebody always knows it for a little while.” She smiled. “Then they don’t.”

They went in the morning, all three of them, because Riarhin would not let Nessa go without her and Tavin would not let either of them go without him.

The Queen’s square lay at the top of the new town, below the palace, where the great road came in through the south gate and ran up between the tall new houses to the palace forecourt. It was where the Queen would ride in. The carpenters had been at it for a week: there were stands going up along both sides of the road, raw timber and canvas, with poles for banners, and at the palace end a great arch of lath and painted cloth with the Queen’s mark on it, half finished, with men on ladders nailing gilt to its top. Canvas screens had been run up round the whole of the forecourt, on posts, stiff and white in the cold, so that nobody could see in. Behind the screens there was hammering.

The market went on round all of it. Stalls of cabbages and cloth and hot chestnuts, pushed back against the house fronts to make room for the stands, and the people of the town going between them with baskets, stopping to watch the men on the ladders.

At the far end of the square, under the half-built stands on the east side, a serjeant had set up a table with a drum beside it.

Riarhin saw him at once. He was not the serjeant from Fairport; he was younger and thinner and did not drink cider. But he had the same table, and the same clerk with the same book, and the same pole with the notch cut in it at the height of a man’s chin, and in front of the table there was a line of young men, eight or nine of them, waiting to stand against the pole.

“The muster,” said Tavin, beside her. “They cry it here at the entry. So the lads can see the Queen ride in and sign after, while they’re still excited.” He looked at the line. “It works, the ringer says. It works every year.”

Nessa took a place on the steps of a house on the west side of the square, across from the serjeant’s table, where the stand had not yet been built and there was a clear space on the cobbles in front of her. She took off her shawl and folded it and put it on the step beside her. She stood with her hands at her sides and her white streaks bright in the thin sunlight, and looked out at the square, and waited.

Nobody noticed her. She was a tall woman standing on a step in a square full of people.

Tavin took off his hat and turned it over in his hands.

A man with a staff and a badge on his sleeve came across the cobbles towards them before Nessa had sung a note.

“Licence,” he said.

Tavin looked at him with enormous friendliness.

“For singing?”

“For taking coin in the Queen’s square on a market day. From the Guild of the Square. Singers, jugglers, ballad-sellers, the man with the bear.” The warden looked at Tavin’s hat. “You’ve a hat out. That’s taking coin. Licence.”

“I have one,” said Tavin, and felt in his coat, and brought out a square of parchment, folded small and soft with handling, and gave it to the warden.

The warden unfolded it and read it. He read it twice.

“This is a ballad-seller’s licence,” he said.

“It is.”

“Made out to a man called Hob Ferrier.”

“It is. I won it off him at cards. In the Bell, in the old town, a week ago Tuesday. He was very drunk and very sorry about it in the morning.” Tavin smiled. “It’s good till the new year. It says so at the bottom. It doesn’t say anything about who has to be holding it.”
