The warden looked at the licence. He looked at Tavin. He looked at Nessa, standing on the step with her hands at her sides, and at Riarhin, a little behind her, with a scarf over her hair and a basket on her arm.

“It says ballad-seller,” he said. “She’s not selling ballads.”

“She’s singing them,” said Tavin. “Nobody’ll buy one after, I grant you. That’s the ballad-seller’s risk.”

The warden stood for a moment with his mouth slightly open. Then he folded the licence and gave it back and said that if there was any trouble it would be Hob Ferrier’s name, and went away across the square shaking his head.

“There’s always a Hob Ferrier,” said Tavin to Riarhin, putting the licence away. “In every town. You only have to find the Bell.”

Nessa began to sing.

Riarhin had been braced for the door song. It was a northern song, a slow one, about a woman waiting at a ford for somebody who was coming back from the wars, and it had a great many verses, and in each verse somebody different came over the ford and it was not him. Riarhin had heard it at Glenruan. Elspeth had sung it once, badly, in the kitchen, with her hands in the bread.

Nessa did not sing it badly.

She sang it quite quietly at first, so that only the people near the step heard, and they stopped. Then a little louder. Riarhin watched it go out across the square the way a ripple goes out across a pond: a woman with a basket of cabbages stopping and turning her head, and then the man beside her, and then the chestnut-seller, with his scoop held above the pan. The carpenters on the ladders at the arch did not stop; they could not hear. But the people on the cobbles in front of the stands stopped, and turned, and after a while there were perhaps forty of them standing in a loose half-circle round the step, and more coming, and nobody was saying anything.

Tavin went round with the hat. He did not say anything either. He held it out and people put things in it without looking at him.

Riarhin stood at the edge of the half-circle with her basket and watched the square.

Across the cobbles, at the serjeant’s table, the clerk had stopped writing. He was sitting with his pen lifted, looking across at the step. The serjeant was standing with his arms folded, looking the same way. The drum was silent. The line of young men in front of the table had turned round, all of them, to listen.

At the back of the line there was a boy.

He was perhaps sixteen. He was thin and fair and had on a coat too big for him, a man’s coat, and he stood at the end of the line with his hands in its pockets, and he was not looking at Nessa. He was looking past her, at the crowd, at a woman standing near the back of the half-circle with a shawl over her head and a baby on her hip, who was looking at him.

The woman did not beckon. She did not do anything. She only looked at him, the way a woman looks at somebody across a room when she cannot say what she wants to say because there are people in the way.

Nessa came to the verse where the last man came over the ford.

Riarhin knew it was the last verse because Elspeth had stopped there, with her hands in the bread, and said that was the one that always made her cry and she did not know why, since it was not even him. Nessa’s voice went down, very low, and very clear, and the whole square leaned in to hear it.

The boy at the end of the line took his hands out of his pockets. He stepped out of the line, sideways, one step, and nobody at the table looked at him. He took another. The clerk was still looking at the step with his pen lifted. The serjeant was still looking at the step with his arms folded. The boy walked away from the end of the line, across the cobbles, round the edge of the half-circle, not fast, not looking back, to the woman with the shawl and the baby, and she put her free hand on his arm and they went away together into the market between the cabbage stalls and were gone.

The verse ended. Nessa stopped singing.

There was a long moment of quiet in the square. Then somebody began to clap, and then everybody did, and the carpenters on the ladders at the arch turned round to see what the noise was, and the drum at the serjeant’s table began again, slow and flat with a space in it, and the clerk looked down at his book and dipped his pen and called the next man to the pole.

He did not look at the end of the line. There was no one there to look at.

Tavin had three shillings and some pence in the hat, and a button, and a small carved horse that a child had put in and then burst into tears about and been given back. He counted it on the step while Nessa put her shawl on.

“Three and eleven,” he said. “And a button. That’s more than Amari sold in apples in a month.”

“Don’t tell him that,” said Nessa.

“I’m going to tell him that the minute we’re through the door.”

Riarhin was looking at the place where the boy had been.

Nessa saw her looking. She finished settling her shawl and came down off the step.

“Walk me home,” she said. “The long way. By the river.”

They walked back along the river path below the city wall, past the washing yards and the empty tanneries, with the water going by on their left, grey and quick under a thin skin of ice at the edges. Tavin had gone ahead with the hat to tell Amari. The path was empty. A heron stood in the shallows on the far bank and did not move as they passed.

“You saw the boy,” said Nessa.

“Yes.”

“So did I.” She walked with her hands in her shawl. “I didn’t do it for him. I want you to know that. I sang because I wanted to sing in a square, and I sang that one because my grandmother sang it at the churn and it’s the one I have all the verses of. He was at the end of the line. His mother was at the back of the crowd. I saw her looking at him.” She was quiet for a few steps. “I didn’t do anything. I only sang the last verse the way it goes.”

“He walked out of the line and nobody saw.”

“Nobody was looking at the line.” Nessa did not look at Riarhin. She walked on for a while.

“The clerk won’t remember him,” she said. “He’s written in the book, the boy. His name and his parish. And the clerk will look at the book tonight and see a name and not know whose it is. He’ll think he wrote it down wrong. He’ll draw a line through it.” She drew her shawl closer. “I didn’t mean that. I never mean it. It happens anyway. Sometimes more than I meant. Sometimes a great deal more.”

They walked for a while without speaking. The heron on the far bank lifted, all at once, and went away low over the water.

“The woman whose kitchen you slept in,” said Riarhin. “In the north. You told me. You went back and she asked who’d sent you.”

“Yes.”

“That was more than you meant.”

“That was a great deal more than I meant.” Nessa’s voice did not change. “I’d slept by her fire three weeks. She’d fed me. She’d mended my coat. I sang a song to get a man past her door who was being hunted, and I went back in the spring to thank her, and she looked at me the way you look at a stranger who’s knocked on the wrong house.” She was quiet. “It’s a door song. It opens a door.”

“And the ending you found. At Scarrow.”

“Doesn’t fix it.” Nessa smiled, a little. “I thought it might. I thought, if I sang it the old way, with the old ending, the one my grandmother had, it’d be—cleaner. It isn’t. It closes a different way, that’s all. It leaves the door ajar behind you instead of shut.” She looked at the river. “I don’t know yet what comes through a door that’s ajar. I’ll find out.”

Riarhin stopped walking.

They had come to the end of the washing yards, where the path went round the corner of a boathouse and the tanneries began. The smell came to them on the wind. Nessa stopped too, and stood beside her, and waited.

“On the stair,” said Riarhin. “On the night. You said you’d know what to do.”

“I said I’d know on the stair.”

“If you sing it for Berent—”

“I don’t know that I will.”

“If you do.” Riarhin looked at her. “Who forgets?”

Nessa looked back at her for a long time, with the wind moving the white streaks at her temples.

“I don’t know,” she said. “Whoever’s looking. Whoever’s on the other side of the door. I don’t choose. I never have.” She pulled the shawl up round her throat. “I’ll tell you the one thing I do know, and then I’d like to go in, because it’s cold.”

“Tell me.”

“I won’t sing him out of his own name without asking,” said Nessa. “I can promise you that. I can’t promise what will be left of it.” She began to walk again. “I’ll tell him before we go. He mustn’t first hear what I mean with a sword at his back.”

Riarhin walked beside her round the corner of the boathouse.

“You’ve never had to ask my name twice,” said Nessa, after a while. She looked sideways at Riarhin. “Ten years. You’re the first.”

Riarhin wanted to promise her. At the corner Nessa caught her sleeve and drew her out of the way of a cart, and the moment passed.

Tavin was waiting in the loft with the hat on the table and Amari’s book open beside it, and Amari had written three and eleven, and a button in the book under the last line in his neat hand, and was looking at it as though it had been written by somebody else.

“He didn’t argue,” said Tavin, with deep satisfaction. “Two things in one month he hasn’t argued with. I’m going to have it cut on my stone.”

“Don’t say that,” said Nessa, and went past him to the fire.

That evening, after the others had gone down, Riarhin sat at the table with Tavin while he mended a strap. He had asked for a problem. She had been thinking about it since Fairport and she had it now.

“The bell,” she said.

Tavin did not look up from the strap.

“It mustn’t ring arms,” she said. “Not till I’m out of the new wing. Not till the others are out. And the boy mustn’t be blamed for it. Those are the two things. How you do it is yours.”

Tavin drew the awl through the leather.

“That’s two problems,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Good. I like two. One’s dull.” He tied off the thread and bit it. “I’ve got the first. I’ve had the first since the ringer. The second’s harder. The second’s the one that needs the knot.” He held up the strap and looked at it against the candle. “I’ve seen to the after, too. While I was at it. Don’t ask. Amari knows. He wrote it down and didn’t argue, which makes three.”

“What after?”

“I said don’t ask.” Tavin smiled at her, and there was nothing behind it at all this time that she could see. “It’s a thing people forget. That’s all. I thought I wouldn’t.”

He put the strap down. Penny hopped along the table from the candlestick and picked at the loose end of thread, and he let her have it.

“You gave me a problem,” he said. “Thank you. That’s the first time anybody has.” He stroked the magpie’s head with one finger. “Everybody else gives me a lock.”

Much later, when the fire had burned down and Tavin and Amari were asleep, and Nessa had gone across the river to the old range as she did every evening and not yet come back, Riarhin walked the round.

She did the stair and the yard and the three ways out, the street and the next yard and the gap in the wall that only a cat would think was a way. She did the hoist door. At the hoist door she stopped and stood in the cold, looking out over the river at the palace.

Across the water, in the old range, a window was lit on the second floor, low and small, near the end of the building. It was the kitchen woman’s mother’s room. Riarhin knew because Nessa had told her which one it was. As she watched, the light in it went down, as though somebody had turned a lamp low beside a bed, and stayed down, and did not go out.

After a while, very faint across the water, so faint that she could not have said whether she heard it or only knew it was there, somebody began to sing. It went up and turned and came down.

Part Five — The Separation

Chapter Twenty-Two — Exact Instructions

The Queen came into Valora four days before Midwinter, a day earlier than the heralds had said, because the roads from the south had been dry.

They had been dry all the way from Serrat. Riarhin heard it in the street, from a woman selling chestnuts at the corner by the ropewalk, who had it from her brother, who drove for the commissariat. Not a drop of rain on the whole of the great road for eleven days, in the month when it always rained. The wagons had come up it like a carriage on a floor. The army’s horses had arrived in Valora fat.

“Lucky,” said the chestnut woman.

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

She went to see the entry. She had told herself she would not.

The Queen’s square was full by noon. The stands on either side of the road had been finished in the night and hung with cloth, and they were packed to the top rail with people, and the people who could not get into the stands stood below them on the cobbles, ten deep, with children on their shoulders. The arch at the palace end had its gilt on. The canvas screens round the forecourt had come down, and behind them the forecourt had been swept and sanded and there was nothing in it at all, only sand, raked in long even lines, waiting.

Riarhin stood on the cobbles at the back of the crowd below the east stand, where the serjeant’s table had been. The table was gone. The pole with the notch in it was still there, driven into the ground, with a child sitting on top of it.

She heard the entry before she saw it. It came up the great road from the south gate as a sound in the crowd, a long rising noise that moved towards her like weather, and then the trumpets over it, and then the horses.

The household came first, twenty young men in dark coats on matched greys, riding two by two. She looked for Guichard and did not find him. Then the banners. Then a space, very deliberate, with nothing in it but the sanded road.

Then the Queen.

She rode alone, a little ahead of everybody else, on a tall bay horse, in black. She did not wave. She rode with the reins in one hand and the other on her thigh, looking straight ahead up the road to the arch, and the noise of the crowd went up round her like a wave going up a harbour wall and did not reach her. Her face was thinner than Riarhin remembered, and browner from the summer in the south, and quite still.

At her throat, against the black, on its black ribbon, there was a small brass case the size of a coin.

Riarhin saw it from forty yards, through the crowd, in the thin winter light. She did not need to see it. She saw it anyway: the ribbon, the case, the little flash of green as the Queen’s horse turned its head and the light moved.

Behind the Queen, half a length back, rode Tylan.

He had the child on his saddle-bow. She was sitting in front of him with his arm round her, in a red hood, bolt upright, with her fists in the horse’s mane, looking at the crowd with an expression of enormous suspicion. As they passed under the east stand she pointed at something, a banner or a dog or a man with a drum, and said something, and Tylan bent his head to listen and laughed and said something back, and she nodded as though that settled it.

The crowd roared.

They went on up the road under the arch and into the sanded forecourt, the Queen alone in front and the Marshal behind with his daughter, and the gates of the forecourt closed behind them, and the crowd began, slowly, to break up and go home. The child on top of the pole had to be lifted down by her father. She did not want to come.

Riarhin stood where she was until the square was half empty.

Then she went back to the ropewalk the long way, by the river, and did not go to the tannery until dark.

She stood at the window of the ropewalk room that night and watched the lights of the palace, which were many more than they had been. Roads were dry some years. Nothing in the plan hung on a single day. She stood at the window anyway until the lights began to go out, one at a time, and the last one that stayed lit was high in the new wing, on the side that faced the river, where she knew the Queen’s rooms were.

She gave Cadan his instruction the next morning, in the loft, with nobody else there.

She had written it down. She had written it seven times over three nights, in the deaf woman’s room, by the candle, and burned six. She did not give him the paper. She read it to him, and then she put it in the fire in front of him, and then she said it again from memory, because she wanted him to see that it was the same.

“Guichard,” she said. “On the night of Midwinter eve. After the evening office, when he walks the round of the old range before he sets the night watch. He walks it alone; you’ve seen him. He goes out by the chapel door and round by the stables and the harness room and back along the court to the barracks door. Somewhere on that round, you kill him.”

Cadan listened with his head a little on one side.

“Quietly,” said Riarhin. “So that nobody hears. So that nobody finds him before the eleventh bell if you can help it. Put him somewhere a man walking the court won’t see him.”

“Yes.”

“The dog. The brindled bitch. She’s not to be killed. Before the office, take her into the harness room with a bone and bar the door. If she won’t go, leave her. Don’t hurt her to make her go.”

“Yes.”

“Nobody else.” Riarhin kept her voice level. “Guichard only. If one of his men comes on you while you’re doing it, you get away from him. You don’t kill him. If one of his men draws on you, you may do what you must to stop him drawing again, and no more. If you’re seen and not drawn on, you walk away. You don’t silence a witness. Do you understand me?”

“I understand you.”

“When it’s done you go out by the river gate below the old range, which will be open because the laundry carts use it after the ninth bell, and you come here. To the tannery. You wait here. You don’t go back into the palace for any reason. Not for any of us. Not if you hear the bell. Not if you see fire.” She stopped. “That’s all of it.”

Cadan was quiet for a long moment.

Then he said it back to her.

He said it all, in order, in his mild voice, without adding anything and without leaving anything out: the round, the harness room, the dog, the bone, nobody else, if one of his men draws on you, what you must to stop him drawing again, and no more, you don’t silence a witness, the river gate, the tannery, you wait here. When he got to the end he paused.

“After it is done,” he said, “I come here, and I wait, and I do not go back for any reason. Not for any of you.”

“Yes.”

“Not for you.”

“Not for me.”

He looked at her with his pleasant face and his patient eyes.

“That is the part,” he said, “that you will want to have said to me clearly. Afterwards. If I am waiting here and you are not.” He folded his hands. “I will do it exactly. I want you to know that I will do it exactly. It will not be my judgment that keeps me here. It will be yours.”

“I know.”

“Good.” He stood. “Then I have it. Thank you. It is very clear.”

He went down the stair. She sat at the table for some time after he had gone.

Aurine had hers already. She had had it for a week.

The gout powder went up to the palace every afternoon at the fourth bell in a folded paper with Denisot’s name on it in the apothecary’s shaking hand. On the afternoon of Midwinter eve it would go up with Aurine’s hand in it instead. Denisot would take it at the ninth bell, with his wine, as he always did, in the little room by the iron doors where he sat through the evening office because his feet would not stand the chapel. By the tenth bell he would be dead in his chair, and the key would be on its chain round his waist, and anyone sent to rouse him would first have to get through his locked door. The delay was what they had bought. Once someone reached the body, the key would work for them as well as it had for him.

“And then,” said Riarhin.

They were in the spicers’ street, in the back of the apothecary’s shop, among the jars. The apothecary was asleep upstairs. Aurine was grinding something in a mortar that smelled of cloves.

“And then I go,” said Aurine.

“Where?”

“Wherever I like.” Aurine did not look up from the mortar. “You said so. You said, a particular task, with a beginning and an end. That’s the end.” She ground. “You’ve paid for a cart. I know you have. Amari told me. It leaves the spicers’ street at the tenth bell going south, with a load of peppercorns, and there’s a place in it behind the sacks. You didn’t ask me whether I wanted it.”

“I’m asking now.”

Aurine put down the pestle.

“I want it,” she said. “I’ll do the powder. It’ll work. He’ll die at his table with his feet up and nobody will know till they try to wake him, and by then I’ll be three miles down the south road in a cart that smells of pepper.” She looked at Riarhin. “And I won’t come back. Not to the tannery. Not to any of you. Not to him.”

She did not say Saikou.

“I know,” said Riarhin.

“Do you mind?”

Riarhin thought about it. She owed Aurine the thought.

“No,” she said. “You said you’d do one thing well. You’ll do it. After that you’re yours.”

Aurine looked at her for a long moment. Then she picked up the pestle again.

“That’s the first time anybody’s paid me for a thing and then let me go,” she said. “The lady always wanted the next thing. I thought you would too.” She ground. “I’ll write to him. After. From somewhere. He can read it and decide whether he wants to know where I went.”

Saikou’s she gave him in front of Amari, because she had promised.

She told them both, in the loft, at the table: the tenth bell, the west gate, the mourning cloth, the river road, Margery’s door. Margery to be brought back by whatever way Saikou could find, to the new wing, to the nursery, before anybody else reached the child. Saikou to do nothing else. Saikou not to come near the Queen’s rooms. Saikou not to come near Riarhin.

Amari listened with his fists on his knees.

“Alone,” he said.

“Alone. It won’t work with two. You know it won’t. Nobody looks past two mourners.”

“I know.” Amari’s voice was very even. “I asked you to remember.”

“I remember. That’s why I’m telling you now and not after.” Riarhin looked at him. “I can’t put him with anybody. I tried. For a week. There’s no way to do it where he’s with somebody and it still works. So I’m telling you, and you can tell me what you want.”

Amari looked at Saikou. Saikou was sitting with his hands in his sleeves, looking at the table.

“What I want,” said Amari, “is for him to write down who was there.”

Saikou looked up.

“Afterwards,” said Amari. “Whatever happens. When you write it up in your book, the way you do, with the dates and the particulars. You write down who was there. Not I went alone to fetch a woman. You write down that there was a woman who came, and a monk who did a thing, and a thief with a bird, and a man at a door.” He did not look away. “You write us in.”

Saikou did not answer at once.

“I will,” he said.

“Say it again.”

“I will write you in,” said Saikou. “All of you.”

Amari nodded. He stood up. At the head of the stair he stopped, with his broad back to them, and said without turning round that he was going to find Tavin, because he and Tavin had a door to hold, and a door wanted two men who had argued about it in advance.

That left Nessa and Berent, and they did not need her.

They had their own plan. They had made it without her, on the tannery stair, with Berent going up and down it twenty times each morning and Nessa sitting on the bottom step counting. They told it to her on the last evening, the two of them, sitting side by side on a bale of hides, like two people telling a third about a wedding.

Nessa would be at the old range for the dying woman, as she had been every evening. At the eleventh bell she would go down the back stair, as she always did, and stop at the foot of the passage on the second floor, as she always did, and sing the man on the stool the verse his mother used to sing. And while she sang, he would not look up the passage. Nessa said so with complete certainty. Aveh did that. It did not make a man blind or stupid. It made him want to hear the end of the verse more than he wanted to look anywhere else.

“And the two on the stair above?”

“They’ll be at the chapel,” said Berent. “For the vigil. Everybody in the old range goes to the Midwinter vigil at the eleventh bell and stays till the year turns. Except the man on the stool, because somebody has to stay. He’s told Nessa that. He minds.” He shifted his leg. “So there’s one man. And one door. And a bar on the outside.”

“And the stair down.”

“I go up it slow,” said Berent. “While she’s singing. I lift the bar. I bring Lyra down. I go down slow.” He looked at Riarhin. “I’ve been practising. You’ve seen.”

She had seen. He could do the tannery stair in a count of forty now. It had been sixty at the beginning of the month.

“And if the man on the stool looks up?”

Nessa and Berent looked at each other.

“Then I’ll do what I can,” said Nessa. “Berent knows what else I might have to sing.”

“About my name,” he said. He turned Na’ar’s sword a little on his knees. “We talked.”

“And?”

“I want to bring her out. Ask me again if we get that far.”

Riarhin looked at them sitting side by side on the bale of hides: Berent with Na’ar’s sword across his knees, Nessa with her shawl round her and her white streaks bright in the firelight. Nessa moved the end of her shawl before Berent set down the sword. Neither looked at the other.

“Go at the eleventh bell,” she said. “Not before. Come here after. By the gap in the wall.”

“We know,” said Berent.

“Say her name,” said Riarhin.

He looked at her. Then he smiled, for the first time in days.

“Lyra,” he said.

Chapter Twenty-Three — The Last Day

On the morning of Midwinter eve Berent did the tannery stair in thirty-eight.

Riarhin counted it. She sat on the bottom step with her back against the wall, where Nessa usually sat, because Nessa had gone out early to the market for bread and Berent had said somebody had to count or it did not count. He went up with the stick in his left hand and his right on the wall, and turned at the top without resting, and came down. His face was grey at the end of it and there was sweat at his hairline, though the loft was cold.

“Thirty-eight,” said Riarhin.

“You’re sure.”

“I’m sure.”

He stood at the foot of the stair with both hands on the stick and got his breath. Then he reached inside his coat and took out the book in its brown cloth, with the torn spine and the slip of paper sticking out a third of the way through, and held it out to her.

“The last day,” he said. “You said.”

She took it.

She carried it up the stair and across the loft to the table by the fire, and set it down there, squarely, with the torn spine towards the stair head, where a person coming up out of the dark would see it first. Berent watched her from the foot of the stair. When she had put it down he came up, slowly, and stood by the table and looked at it, and put out one finger and moved it a little, a quarter of an inch, so that it was straight with the edge of the table.

“There,” he said.

They ate together at noon. It was the only time all day that all eight of them were in the loft at once, and none of them said so.

Nessa brought bread and a round of cheese and a string of onions, and Tavin produced from somewhere a small cold goose that he said had been given to him by the ringer’s daughter-in-law for the feast, which nobody believed, and Amari had bought a jug of the dark Valoran ale with the last of the three and eleven. They ate round the table with the hoist door shut against the cold and the fire built up. Cadan carved the goose. He did it very neatly, with a small knife, and gave everybody the same amount, and took the neck for himself.

Nobody talked about the night. They talked about the goose. They talked about the ringer’s daughter-in-law, whom Tavin described at length and with great affection and who, it emerged, did not exist. They talked about the swan in the palace ditch, which Amari said had frozen in on the east side overnight and been cut out by the household guard at dawn with axes, and had hissed at them the whole time, and bitten Guichard. Tavin laughed so much at that he had to put down his cup.

Penny walked up and down the table among the plates, stealing.

When the goose was finished Tavin sat back and looked round the table with a cup in his hand and his pleasant face flushed from the fire and the ale, and began, cheerfully, to count them.

“One,” he said, pointing at Riarhin with the cup. “Two.” Berent. “Three, four.” Saikou and Amari, side by side. “Five.” Nessa. “Six.” Aurine. “Seven.” Cadan. “Eight.” He tapped his own chest. Then he looked at the end of the table, where the book lay squared to the edge by the fire, with the slip sticking out of it, and his cup moved towards it.

“Eight,” said Nessa.

She said it quite quietly. Tavin’s cup stopped.

“Eight and a book,” said Nessa, and reached across and took the cup out of his hand, and drank from it, and gave it back.

Tavin looked at her. Then he laughed, a little, and said that he had never been able to count past eight without his boots off, and the talk went on. But nobody at the table looked at the end of it again, and when the plates were cleared Riarhin saw Saikou move the candlestick so that it stood between the book and the rest of the table, and leave it there.

Aurine went first.

She had to be at the apothecary’s shop in the spicers’ street by the third bell. She stood up from the table and put on her coat and picked up her satchel, which she had packed the night before, and stood for a moment by the stair head as though there were something she had forgotten.

Then she said, to the room, “I’ll go, then,” and went down.

Riarhin went after her. She had not meant to. She followed her down the stair and across the yard and caught her at the street door, and Aurine turned round with her hand on the latch and looked at her.

“The carter,” said Riarhin.

Aurine’s mouth went thin.

“He came this morning,” she said. “To the shop. He says the south gate’s to be shut tonight at the eleventh bell for the vigil, and the customs men will look in every cart that goes through before that, and a cart of pepper with a woman in it behind the sacks is a cart that gets looked in. He says he wants more.” She did not look away. “He says he wants twice. I told him I’d see.”

“How much is twice?”

Aurine told her.

Riarhin stood in the doorway. It was more than was in the Valora heap. It was more than had been in it for a week.

Behind her on the stair she heard somebody coming down. It was Amari. He had the book under his arm and a leather purse in his hand, and he came across the yard to the door and held the purse out to Aurine without saying anything.

Aurine looked at it.

“That’s the other heap,” she said.

“It is.”

“That’s for going home. You said. Nobody touches it.”

“Nobody touches it for this,” said Amari. “For the killing. We said.” He did not lower his hand. “You’re not killing anybody with it. You’re going home. That’s what it’s for. It’s the only thing it’s for.” He put the purse into her hand and closed her fingers over it. “Wherever home is. I’ll not ask. I said I’d give it to whoever asked without asking them why.”

“I didn’t ask.”

“No,” said Amari. “I noticed.”

Aurine stood in the doorway with the purse in her hand. Her face did not change very much. It went still, and a little pale round the mouth, and stayed like that.

“I’ll write it in,” said Amari. “Under the rest. Going home. No name.” He opened the book against his arm and took the pencil from behind his ear and wrote, there in the doorway, and showed her. She looked at it. “That’s all,” he said, and closed the book, and went back across the yard and up the stair.

Aurine put the purse inside her coat.

“He’s a very tiresome man,” she said.

“Yes.”

“He’ll have written it to the farthing.” She opened the street door. The cold came in. “Tell Saikou—” She stopped.

Riarhin waited.

“No,” said Aurine. “I’ll tell him myself. I’ll write.” She went out into the tanners’ street and turned towards the river and did not look back, walking quickly, with her satchel over her shoulder and her hands in her sleeves.

At the fourth bell Riarhin was standing at the corner of the spicers’ street with a basket of onions she did not want.

She had not meant to be there either. She had told herself that she would stay at the tannery until the eighth bell, and rest, and eat, and go through the new wing once more in her head, and she had gone through it four times by the third bell and could not sit still any longer.

The apothecary’s shop was halfway down the street, with a green lamp over the door and a window full of jars. At the fourth bell a boy came out of the palace end of the street at a trot, in the household’s livery, and went into the shop. He was in there for the time it took to count thirty. He came out again with a small folded paper in his hand and put it inside his coat, and went back up the street at a trot the way he had come, and passed within two yards of Riarhin at the corner, and did not look at her.

She watched him go until he turned the corner at the top of the street.

Then she looked at the shop. Through the window, between the jars, she could see Aurine behind the counter, grinding something in a mortar. Aurine did not look up. She went on grinding, steadily, with her head bent.

A man in a long dark coat came down the street from the river end and went into the shop.

It was Saikou. Riarhin had not known he would come. She stood at the corner with her onions and watched him through the window. He went to the counter. He said something. Aurine stopped grinding and looked at him, and then went to a drawer behind her and took out a paper and folded it, and gave it to him, and he paid her. He put the paper inside his coat. He said something else. Aurine answered, briefly, and went back to the mortar.

He stood at the counter a moment longer. Then he came out, and walked up the street towards Riarhin, and passed her at the corner, and stopped.

“A sleeping powder,” he said, without looking at her. “I do not sleep well before a thing. She knows. She has made it up for me before.” He looked at the onions in her basket. “That is a great many onions.”

“I know.”

“She said the price was the same as always.” Saikou put his hands in his sleeves. “I said I would pay it next time. She said there would not be a next time, so I had better pay it now. So I did.” He was quiet. “That is all we said. I would like you to know it was enough.”

He walked on, towards the river. Riarhin stood at the corner and watched the shop. At the fifth bell the apothecary came down from upstairs in his nightcap, complaining, and Aurine took off her apron and hung it on its peg and picked up her satchel, and came out of the shop and went away down the street towards the carters’ yard at the bottom of it, and did not come back.

Amari went at the sixth bell, with the vintner.

Tavin had arranged it. He had met the vintner’s carter at the Bell a fortnight ago and lost to him at cards, very carefully, three nights running, and on the fourth night the carter had said that it was a shame about the feast wine, since his lad had broken his arm and there was nobody to roll the casks down the palace cellar stair on Midwinter eve, and Tavin had said that he knew a big man who wanted a day’s work. The big man was Amari. The cellar stair came up at the back of the new wing, a dozen paces from the foot of the stair that went up to the covered bridge. When the casks were in, the vintner’s cart would go and the big man would not.

Riarhin went to the yard gate to see him off. She had not been to see anybody else off. She found that she could not not go.

He had put on a leather apron over his coat and a vintner’s cap and he looked like a man who rolled casks for a living. The cudgel was under the apron. The book was not; he had left it on the table in the loft, beside Lyra’s grammar.

“You’ll want it after,” he said. “For the money. Somebody’ll have to know what’s been spent. Saikou can read my hand.” He looked at the lane, where the vintner’s cart was waiting with its load of casks and the carter sitting on the box eating a pie. “I’ve written it up to this morning. Aurine’s is the last line.”

“I saw.”

“Did you see what I wrote?”

“Going home.”

“Going home,” said Amari. He adjusted the cap. “It’s the best line in the book. I’ve been keeping it nine years and it’s the best one.” He looked at her. “You remember what I asked. At Fairport.”

“He’s not alone. He’ll have Margery.”

“Coming back. Not going.” Amari did not smile. “Going, he’ll be alone. I know. I know you tried.” He looked past her, up at the hoist door of the loft, which was shut. “He came down to the yard this morning before it was light and stood by the water butt for an hour. He didn’t know I saw. I didn’t go down.” He was quiet. “You tell him, after. If I’m—” He stopped. “Tell him I didn’t go down because I knew he’d want to stand there by himself. Not because I didn’t see.”

“You’ll tell him yourself.”

“I’ll tell him myself,” said Amari. “I’m telling you as well. In case.”

The carter on the vintner’s box called something, with his mouth full.

Amari went out through the gate and climbed up beside him, heavily, and the cart went away down the lane towards the river road and the palace, with the casks knocking together in the back, and Amari sitting very straight on the box with his broad back to her and his cap on, and he did not look round.

Tavin went at the sixth bell and a half, with Penny.

He had a sugared bun in a paper and a coil of thin cord over his shoulder under his coat, and the magpie inside his coat with her head out at the collar, and he stood at the top of the tannery stair in his hat and looked round the loft as though he were fixing it in his memory, and then laughed at himself.

“I’ll be back for supper,” he said. “Keep the fire in.”

Riarhin was at the table. She got up.

“Tavin—”

“You gave me a problem,” he said. “Two. I’ve got them. Don’t give me any more now; I’ll only start solving them on the way and be late.” He looked at the table, where Amari’s book lay beside Lyra’s grammar. “Tell Amari he can put the goose in the book. The ringer’s daughter-in-law won’t mind. She doesn’t exist.”

“I know.”

“I know you know.” He came across the loft and did a thing he had never done before: he put his hand on her shoulder, briefly, the way Saikou had once put his on Amari’s on the deck of Davra’s ship, and took it away again. “You asked me what I wanted. At Fairport. To see how it comes out.” He grinned. “I’m going to have a very good view. Right up the top of the tower. I’ll tell you all about it after, and I’ll get most of it wrong, and you’ll correct me, and I’ll say you weren’t there.”

He went down the stair whistling. Penny, at his collar, looked back once at the fire.

Cadan went at the seventh bell, before the evening office.

He did not say anything to anybody. He had spent the afternoon sitting at the end of the table with his hands folded and his eyes half closed, not asleep, while the others went, one after another, and Riarhin had watched him not move for three hours. At the seventh bell he stood up. He had a bone wrapped in a cloth, a big one, from the butcher at the end of the tanners’ street, and he put it inside his grey sleeve.

He stood at the head of the stair and looked at Riarhin.

“I have it,” he said. “Exactly. I will not come back for any of you. Not for you.”

“I know.”

“I wanted to say it once more,” said Cadan. “Here. Where you said it to me. So that it has been said in both directions.”

He inclined his head, and went down.

That left Nessa and Berent, and Saikou, and her.

Nessa and Berent went at the seventh bell and a half, together, to the old range. The kitchen woman had said that Nessa’s lame brother might come and sit with her mother on Midwinter eve while everyone else went to the vigil, since he had nobody in the city and it was the feast. Berent had put on a hood. He stood at the foot of the tannery stair in it with Na’ar’s sword in its oilcloth on his back and a length of sacking over it, like a bundle of firewood, and his stick, and looked up at Riarhin on the stair.

“Thirty-eight,” he said.

“Thirty-eight.”

“Say her name.”

“Lyra.”

He nodded. Nessa was at the street door, with her shawl round her and her white streaks hidden under a kerchief for once, and she looked back up the stair at Riarhin and did not say anything at all.

They went out together into the dark.

Saikou went last but one.

He sat with her at the table after the others had gone, with the fire between them and the two books on the table, Amari’s and Lyra’s, side by side, and the candlestick where he had moved it. He had the mourning cloth folded on his knee. He had been sitting with it there for some time without unfolding it.

“The tenth bell,” he said. “The west gate. The river road. Her door.”

“Yes.”

“Nothing else. Not the Queen’s rooms. Not you.”

“Not me.”

He looked at the fire.

“Amari has written everything in his book up to this morning,” he said. “I looked, while he was at the cart. He has written what everybody was paid and what everybody was given and what everybody spent, in his hand, to the farthing. He has written the tannery twice, because Tavin argued about it twice. He has written three and eleven, and a button.” He was quiet. “He has not written what anybody is doing tonight. There is nothing in it after this morning. It stops.”

Riarhin did not say anything.

“I was going to write it in,” said Saikou. “Tonight. Before I went. What everybody is doing, where, and when. So that it would be written somewhere. I sat down to do it an hour ago.” He turned the folded cloth over on his knee. “I found I could not. It is not a thing that should exist on paper before it has happened. You burned the names.” He looked at her. “So I will write it after. All of them. Who was there. I said I would.”

He stood up. He shook out the grey cloth, and put it over his head and shoulders, and it hung down on either side of his face, and his face inside it was very calm.

“Walk slowly,” he said. “When you come out. Whatever has happened. Walk at the pace of the dead, and don’t look to either side, and people will let you go where you are going.” He paused at the head of the stair. “It is not a thing I can do for you. I would if I could. It is only a thing I can tell you.”

He went down. She heard him cross the yard, and the street door open and shut, and then nothing.

She was alone in the loft.

The fire was burning well. She banked it, so that it would last. She put the kettle at the side of the hearth where it would keep warm and not boil dry. She set out the blanket on the bench by the fire, folded, where somebody coming in cold could reach it. She did these things slowly and carefully and in order, and when she had done them she stood in the middle of the loft and looked round it, as Tavin had, and did not laugh.

She took off her coat.

In the inside pocket on the right there was Ailsa’s letter, in its oilcloth, with the strip of blue cloth folded into it. In the inside pocket on the left there was Lyra’s scrap, and the stub of a letter she had never finished to Glenruan, and nothing else. She took Ailsa’s letter out of the right-hand pocket and held it for a moment. Then she put it into the pocket on the left, with Lyra’s scrap, and buttoned the pocket.

In the pocket on the right she put the square of linen.

It was very small. It had been washed since Tigist gave it to her, and a corner mended, and she had folded it smaller and smaller over the years until it was the size of her palm. She had carried it in that pocket all the way north from the dead city with a small hard shape inside it, and all the years since with nothing inside it at all. She put it in, flat, at the bottom of the pocket, and put her coat back on, and buttoned it to the throat.

Then she went to the table.

Amari’s book lay there, and Lyra’s beside it, with the candlestick between them and the rest of the table. She did not touch either of them. She looked at the slip of paper sticking out of the brown cloth, with its three letters of Lyra’s clear upright hand. Then she looked at the plates, which had been cleared and stacked, and the bones of the goose on a board, and the eight cups, which nobody had washed, standing in a row at the end of the table where Tavin had put them after he counted.

She counted them. She could not help it.

Eight.

She took Remorse down from its peg by the hoist door and wrapped it in a length of sacking, and put it under her arm like a bundle of laths. She blew out the candle, and went down the stair, and out through the yard and the street door into the tanners’ street, where it was already dark and the first Midwinter fires were being lit on the corners, and walked towards the washing yards, where Maheut would be waiting with a basket of sheets.

Chapter Twenty-Four — Midwinter Eve

The guard at the west gate said Aine back to her when she said it, and looked at a slate, and let her through.

It was dark already. The carts had lanterns hung from their shafts, and the women pushing them had put on their best aprons for the feast, and one of them had a sprig of holly in her cap. Riarhin had a basket of sheets on her hip that Maheut had given her at the washing yards an hour before, with a look that went over her from her boots to her scarf and stopped at her face and said nothing. Under the sheets, flat along the bottom of the basket, wrapped in a length of sacking, lay Remorse. The knives were where they always were. She wore her own coat against the cold, under an apron, as the other women wore theirs; she had moved Ailsa’s letter to the pocket on the left that evening, and left the pocket on the right empty but for a folded square of linen.

In the kitchen passage the woman with the book at the foot of the linen stair was not Maheut. Maheut was at the top of it, with another book, counting the women up. The woman at the foot looked at Riarhin’s basket, and at her face, and at the slate beside her, and wrote Aine and a number and said go up.

Riarhin went up.

The new wing was empty of everybody but the women and the beds.

She had never been in it without the household. She had been in it under Bea’s face, at suppers, with music in the next room and the fair company laughing and Tylan at a window with a broken toy in his hands. Now it was a row of doors standing open onto dark rooms, and in each room two or three women were stripping a bed by candlelight, fast, without talking, and carrying the old linen out to the stair head in armfuls, where Maheut stood with her book and counted it down.

Maheut put Riarhin in the Queen’s bedchamber with two others.

“Corners,” she said. “The way they like them. Aine knows.” She looked at Riarhin. “Aine learned in a palace.”

The two others laughed. Riarhin did not.

The bedchamber was large and plain, plainer than she had expected. A great bed with dark hangings. A hearth with a fire laid and not lit. A table under the window with papers on it, weighted with a stone. A chair by the hearth with a shawl over its arm. A door in the far wall that stood a little open onto a smaller room lined with presses: the Queen’s dressing room, with the linen presses on one side and the gowns on the other, and at its far end another door, shut, that led, Riarhin knew, to the nursery.

She made the bed. She made it properly, with corners, the way she had seen it done in the Rocke house by Elspeth, who had opinions. The other two women worked the other side and did not talk to her. When it was done they stood back and looked at it and one of them said that’ll do her, and they picked up the old linen and went out to the stair head.

Riarhin picked up her basket, which still had one folded sheet on top and the sacking beneath, and went into the dressing room to put the sheet in the press.

She did not come out.

She heard Maheut count them down.

She could hear it from inside the linen press, where she sat folded among the Queen’s spare sheets in the dark with Remorse across her knees and the door drawn to on its latch. She could hear the women going down the stair with the last of the old linen, and Maheut at the stair head saying their names as they went: Jehanne. Isolde. Perrette. Marie. Eleven names.

A pause.

“Aine?”

Riarhin sat very still.

“Aine?”

A longer pause. She heard footsteps come back along the passage from the stair head, slowly, and stop somewhere near the bedchamber door. She could not see anything. She could hear breathing. She could hear, very faintly, the sound of a book being opened and shut again.

The footsteps went back to the stair head.

“Eleven,” said Maheut’s voice, flat, to somebody below. “And the one I hired. Twelve. All down.”

The footsteps went down the stair and did not come back.

Riarhin sat among the Queen’s sheets with her hands flat on Remorse. Maheut had stopped at the bedchamber. She had called the name twice. Below, the gate shut behind the carts. Riarhin kept listening for her to come back.

The Queen came up at the tenth bell.

Riarhin heard the household first: a great many feet on the main stair, and voices, and a woman laughing, and doors, and then the voices going away along the passage towards the other rooms of the wing, and only two sets of feet coming on. The bedchamber door opened. Candlelight came under the door of the dressing room in a thin yellow line.

“—three days,” said a woman’s voice, “and you’d have thought it was three weeks.”

Riarhin knew the voice from the supper table, asking where she had found the gift. The clerk’s account of the granary at Serrat had come to her on the shingle at Scarrow in another man’s voice. She had supplied this one as he read.

“She’s three,” said a man’s voice. “Three days is a very long time when you’re three.”

“She didn’t know me.”

“She knew you. She was cross with you. Those look the same at three.” A chair creaked. “She was cross with me for a week after Acasia. She hid my boots.”

The Queen laughed. It was the laugh Riarhin remembered, the one she had heard in the lighted room with the green case against the black ribbon: warm, tired, pleased.

They talked. They talked for a long time, in the bedchamber, by the fire someone had lit, with the door to the dressing room standing an inch open, and Riarhin sat in the press in the dark and listened, because she could not do anything else, and because she could not stop herself remembering every word.

They talked about the child. About her cough, which was better. About the nurse, who was too soft with her about almonds. About the feast tomorrow, and who would sit where, and whether Hale would be sober. Tylan said the valleys were quiet. He said it in the voice of a man who has been told something good and does not believe it.

“The Tarne is quiet,” he said. “The whole line. Not a raid since the frosts.”

“I said it would be.”

“You did.” A pause. “There were seventeen thousand on the roads by the end. Veyrac counted. He’s still counting. He says they’re in the towns now, in the camps, and they’re quiet too.”

“Then it was right.”

Tylan did not answer at once.

“It was quick,” he said.

“It was kinder.”

“You said so. At Serrat.” The chair creaked again. “I’ve been thinking about the ones in the camps. They’re quiet because there’s nothing to be loud about. They haven’t got anything. You can’t raid from a camp.” Another pause. “I keep thinking, what do they do in the spring. When we’ve gone south to Ecktoria. When there’s nobody standing over them.”

“We’ll leave somebody standing over them.”

“Yes,” said Tylan. “We will.”

Something was put down on a table. Paper moved.

“Don’t,” said the Queen, quite gently. “Not tonight. It’s Midwinter. Come and sit by me and don’t be the Marshal for an hour.”

Tylan laughed, low, and the chair creaked, and after that they were quiet.

Riarhin closed her eyes. The press smelled of lavender caught in the folds of the sheets. Beyond its door Tylan said something too low for her to catch, and Veralyn laughed into his shoulder. Riarhin shifted her cramped foot without letting the heel touch wood.

At the eleventh bell there was a knock.

A servant’s voice at the bedchamber door, anxious. The iron doors to the old range were still open, madam, sir. They were to be locked at the eleventh bell for the vigil. Captain Denisot had not come to lock them.

“Then send to Denisot,” said Tylan.

“We have, sir. He doesn’t answer his door.”

“His gout.” Tylan sounded tired and faintly amused. “He’s taken his draught and fallen asleep in his chair. Knock louder.”

The servant went away.

Riarhin counted. It was what she had. She counted the bells, the steps, the breaths; she counted the servant going along the passage and down the stair to the little room by the iron doors where a man with a key on a chain round his waist was sitting at his table with his feet up and his wine beside him. She did not know if he was dead. She did not know if Aurine’s powder had gone up in the fourth-bell paper, or if Aurine was three miles down the south road behind a load of peppercorns. She knew the doors were open.

The servant did not come back.

Somewhere far off, beyond the new wing, towards the old range, there was a sound. It was faint and flat and came through the walls in a short run, like somebody knocking on wood a long way away, and then stopped, and came again. It could have been anything. It could have been a door. It could have been somebody splitting kindling for the vigil fires.

She thought of Amari on the far side of the covered bridge with his back against an oak door that opened outward.

She could not go and see. There was nothing on the other side of that sound that she could reach without leaving this room, and everything that the others were doing tonight had been done so that she would be in this room when the bell rang midnight. She sat in the press with her hands on the sword and listened to the knocking come again, and stop, and not come again, and did not know what that meant, and did not let herself guess.

Midnight came, and the bell did not ring.

It should have. It rang every Midwinter at the turn of the year, in the tower of the old range, twelve slow strokes and then a pause and then the long peal for the new year; every child in Valora knew it; Tavin’s old ringer had told him it had not failed in forty years. Riarhin heard the hour come in the only way she could, in the dark, by counting, and when she had counted to it there was nothing.

The silence went on.

In the bedchamber a chair scraped.

“The bell,” said Tylan.

“They’re late.”

“They’re never late. Not the Midwinter bell. Jory’s been sitting on that rope since noon.” He was on his feet; she heard him cross the room. The door to the passage opened. “Hello? Who’s on the stair?”

Nobody answered.

“Tylan,” said the Queen.

“The doors are open,” said Tylan. His voice had changed. It was the voice of a man who had been in a great many camps at night and had just heard, somewhere out in the dark, the silence where a sentry’s challenge ought to have been. “Denisot hasn’t come. The bell hasn’t rung. There’s nobody on the stair.” She heard him take something from a chair: a belt, the sound of a buckle. “Bar the door behind me. Don’t open it to anybody who isn’t me.”

“Where are you going?”

“Matylde first. Then down. There’s a courier from Serrat in the lower hall with six riders; they came in at the tenth bell with the Veyrac dispatches, I saw their horses. If something’s wrong I want them on the stair, and I want one of them on his horse to the garrison with my seal before anybody else knows it.” The buckle closed. “Bar the door.”

He went out.

Riarhin heard the Queen cross the room and the bar go down across the bedchamber door. She heard Tylan’s feet go along the passage outside, fast, towards the nursery, and the nursery door, and his voice, low, saying the nurse’s name.

She had not known about the riders.

Six men from Serrat. They had come up the dry road a day early with dispatches and ridden in at the tenth bell, while she sat in the press listening to a woman and a man talk about their daughter’s cough, and they were in the lower hall now, armed, awake, fed, forty feet below the nursery, with fresh horses in the court. Nobody had sent for them. They had arrived.

She opened the door of the press.

The dressing room was dark. The door at its far end, the one to the nursery, was shut. Beyond it she could hear Tylan’s voice and the nurse’s, and a child, half woken, beginning to cry.

In a moment he would come out of the nursery with the child or without her, and go down the stair to the six men in the lower hall, and one of them would ride for the garrison with the Marshal’s seal, and it would not matter who else was dead.

Riarhin stood up among the Queen’s sheets. Her knees had stiffened. She let them. She took Remorse out of its sacking and let the sacking fall, and put the sword into her left hand, and went to the nursery door, and did not open it. She went past it, along the dressing room, to the other door, the one that gave onto the passage outside, between the nursery and the head of the stair.

She stepped out into the passage just as Tylan came out of the nursery with a candle in one hand and his sword in the other, and turned towards the stair, and saw her.

Chapter Twenty-Five — Tylan

He knew her at once.

He lifted the candle towards her face. It was in his right hand, the sword in his left. For a moment his mouth opened as though she had arrived at supper. Then his eyes went to Remorse.

“Riarhin,” he said.

“Tylan.”

He did not ask why she was there. He looked past her, once, at the head of the linen stair behind her, and then at the sword in her hand, and then back at her face. He had understood the whole of it in the time it took to look.

Behind him, in the nursery, the child was crying.

“Bar it,” he said, over his shoulder, not loud. “Bar the door, Anne. Now. Don’t open it for anybody.”

The nursery door closed. The bar went down on the other side of it. The crying went on, muffled.

Tylan set the candle down on the chest against the wall. He did it carefully, so that it would not fall. Then he shifted the sword into his right hand, and stood in the middle of the passage between her and the nursery with his feet apart, and she saw what he was going to do before he did it.

He was not going to come to her.

She had chosen the place. He knew that. He had seen the stair behind her and the passage in front of her and the chest and the window embrasure and the hanging on the far wall, and he had seen that she had stepped out exactly where the passage narrowed at the stair head, where a man with a long sword and a long arm could not swing without striking plaster. He had been in a great many fights. He was not going to walk into one she had arranged.

“Serrat!” he shouted. “The stair! Serrat, to me!”

It went down the linen stair behind her, past her, into the dark. She did not know whether it reached the lower hall. The linen stair came down into the kitchen passage, and the kitchen passage had a door at the end of it, and the lower hall was beyond the door. She had walked it a year ago with Orrel. It was forty feet and a door. She did not know if the door was shut.

Tylan did not wait to find out. He came forward two steps, not to her, to the window embrasure, and put his back to the wall beside it, where he could see her and the stair head and the nursery door all at once, and waited.

He had made it her problem. If the riders heard, they would come up the stair behind her, and she would be between them and him. If they did not hear, he could wait there until somebody else noticed that the bell had not rung. Every moment she stood still was his.

She went to him.

He had the reach. She had known he would. He was a head taller and his arm was longer and his sword was longer than Remorse by the width of a hand, and he held it the way a man holds a thing he has held every day of his life. When she came in along the wall he met her with the point, not the edge, a short straight thrust at her face that would have ended it if she had been where she had been a moment before.

She was not. She had turned her wrist and let the thrust slide past on Remorse’s flat, angled, and gone on round, towards the window, so that he had to turn to follow her and the embrasure was at his back instead of his side.

He checked his turn at once, stepped clear of the embrasure and cut at her forward leg. She withdrew it. His point was back between them before she could answer.

He was good. She had known he was good. She had not known how patient he was.

They went round the passage twice. He never came farther from the wall than a single step. He kept her away from the nursery and the open side of the embrasure. When she pressed him he gave ground a hand’s width and no more, and when she stopped pressing he did not follow. His breathing was even. He watched her feet.

She knew what he was doing. He was waiting for the stair.

She stopped trying to reach him.

She stepped back, out of his distance, two steps, three, towards the stair head, and lowered Remorse a little, and let him see her breathing hard.

He looked at her. His eyes went to the stair behind her. He called once more to the men below. No answer reached them. Behind him the child was crying; Riarhin could hear the nurse trying to quiet her.

He came.

He came well. He came off the wall at last with his sword out in front of him and his weight low, not a rush, a long gliding step that took the whole width of the passage and left her no room to go round. She gave ground. She went back to the stair head and onto the top step, and down, one step, two, with his point following her face all the way, and he came to the head of the stair and stopped there, above her, with the whole of his reach and the whole of his height and the high ground as well.

He could have waited there. He did not. He had seen the stair behind her, dark, going down to the lower hall, and he had seen that she had given it to him, and he wanted it. He wanted to be down it. He wanted a rider on a horse with his seal.

He came down after her.

The linen stair was narrow and screened and it turned on itself halfway down, and the treads were new. Somebody had taken out the old boards and put in new ones and shaved the lowest of them to fit, and a carpenter had knelt at the bottom a year ago with a shallow box of tools and said mostly. There was a rail on one side now, fixed, and on the other the screen, lath and canvas, stretched to hide the stair from the passage below so that a man coming from the far door would see feet before he saw faces.

She knew every tread of it. She had carried a basket up it three hours ago, counting them. She knew that the sixth from the top was a finger higher than the fifth, because the new wood had not quite been planed to the old, and that the turn was sharp and the rail stopped short of it by a hand’s breadth, and that the screen at the turn bellied out a little where the canvas had stretched.

Tylan shortened his step as he reached the turn. He was looking past her shoulder towards the lower passage.

He came down behind his point, keeping her below him. She gave another tread, caught his next cut obliquely and let it slide clear. At the sixth she stepped sideways into the turn. He followed with the point before moving his foot; she struck his blade across towards the screen, forcing him to recover to that side. His rear heel came down on the edge of the high tread.

He caught the rail with his left hand and brought his sword back up. His foot found the tread. She had the time it took him to do those things.

She stepped inside his point.

She went in beside the rail.

Remorse held his blade aside as she reached the step below his and cut across the inside of his sword arm above the elbow. The blade bit. He grunted and did not drop the sword. He brought the hilt down at her head and she took it on the shoulder, hard, and her right arm went heavy to the elbow. She was inside now and he could not get her out.

He tried. He was strong and he was not afraid, and he let go of the rail and got his left hand on her coat and threw her against the screen, and the canvas gave behind her and she went half through it and felt the lath crack against her back. His sword came round short, the edge, not the point, the only thing he could do at that distance, and it went across her right side over the ribs, and she felt it go in.

It did not hurt yet. She knew it would.

She came off the screen. She came off it into him, low, with her right shoulder against his chest and Remorse in her left hand coming up underneath his guard, between the arm she had cut and his body, and she put the point where she had put it before in other places, under the ribs, upward, and she committed.

It went in.

He stopped.

He did not fall at once. He stood on the stair with his back against the rail and her against him and the jian in him to the guard, and he looked down at her. His breathing had changed. His sword hung from his hand.

She had known him for a very short time. Three evenings at a table, a broken toy horse mended at a window, a man laughing when he thought nobody was looking. He had been kind to her in all of those. She had watched him take his daughter from her mother’s tired arm.

He said something. She did not hear what. It might have been the child’s name. It might have been the Queen’s.

Then his knees went, and she went down with him, onto the turn of the stair, and held him so that his head would not strike the tread.

He was dead before she had got her breath.

She knelt on the linen stair with Tylan across her knees and the blood running down the new treads towards the kitchen passage, and listened.

Below, at the foot of the stair, beyond the screen, there was nothing. No feet. No voices. Nobody had heard. Or somebody had heard and the door at the end of the kitchen passage was shut, and they were eating the Midwinter supper in the lower hall with their dispatches, and in a little while one of them would get up to piss and find the blood running under the door.

Above, the child had stopped crying.

Her side had begun to hurt. It came up slowly, the way she had known it would, a line of heat across the ribs that got wider when she breathed. She put her right hand to it and it came away wet. Her right shoulder would not lift properly. She tried it and it would not.

She could not stay. She knew she could not stay. She had done the thing she had come up the stair to do, and it was one thing of two, and the other was behind a barred door at the end of the passage above her with the fire lit and a sword on the chair.

She laid Tylan down on the turn of the stair. She closed his eyes, which she had not meant to do, and found she had done. She did not take his seal. It was on his right hand, a heavy gold ring with the Marshal’s mark cut into it, and she looked at it for a long moment, and left it where it was, and closed his hand over it.

Then she got up. It took her two tries. She pulled Remorse free and went back up the stair, past the sixth tread, which she stepped over, to the passage, where the candle was still burning on the chest where he had put it, carefully, so that it would not fall.

The door to the bedchamber was open.

The bar was up. It lay on the floor of the passage. The Queen was standing in the doorway with the firelight behind her and a sword in her hand, and she was looking at the blood on Riarhin’s coat, and then down the passage at the head of the stair, and then back at Riarhin’s face.

She did not ask where Tylan was.

Chapter Twenty-Six — Shadowsteel

The Queen had not wasted the time.

Riarhin saw it before she saw anything else. The bedchamber behind Veralyn had been changed. The chair by the hearth had been pushed back against the wall. The table under the window had been dragged aside, and the papers from it lay in a heap on the bed. Every candle in the room was lit: on the mantel, on the table, on the floor in the corners, a dozen of them, so that there was no shadow anywhere a person could stand in. The fire had been built up high. The Queen had taken off the long black robe she had worn to the chapel and stood in her shift and a dark kirtle, with her sleeves pushed back to the elbow, in soft indoor boots. The robe lay over the end of the bed.

The sword in her hand was not large. Riarhin had seen it lie along a table at Veralyn’s right hand; she had seen people look at it before they looked at the Queen. It was a longsword, plain-hilted, not heavy. Veralyn held it low and easy in both hands with the point towards the floor, the way a woman holds a thing she has held so long she has forgotten it has weight.

The green case lay against the black ribbon at her throat.

“You brought it to me,” said the Queen.

“Yes.”

Veralyn looked at her for a moment longer. Her face was very still. Then she stepped back out of the doorway into the lit room, one step, two, and waited.

Riarhin knew what the room was. She had been given the terrain, as she had given it to Tylan, and she did not want it.

It was all floor. The Queen had seen to that. There was nothing to go round, nothing to put between them, no step and no rail and no screen. There was light everywhere, even and yellow, and the fire at the Queen’s back, which meant that if Riarhin came in she would come in with the fire in her eyes and the Queen’s shadow thrown forward across the floor at her feet. There was the length of the Queen’s arm and the length of the sword in it, which together were more than Riarhin’s arm and Remorse by a hand and a half. There was the Queen herself, rested, unhurt, standing on a clear floor she had cleared.

And there was Riarhin, with a cut across her ribs that had begun to bleed in earnest now, into her shirt and down under her belt, and a right shoulder that would not lift, and her breath going short.

She could stay in the doorway. The doorway was narrow. It was the one piece of the room that was hers.

She could stay there until somebody came. Until a man from Serrat found the blood under the kitchen door, or the nurse took the bar off the nursery, or somebody noticed that the Midwinter bell had not rung. The Queen could wait longer than she could. The Queen was not bleeding.

Riarhin went in.

The first thing Veralyn did was try to finish it.

She did it without any warning at all. She had been standing still with her point low, and then she was not standing still; she came across the clear floor in two long steps and the sword came up from the floor in a rising cut at Riarhin’s forward knee that turned, halfway, into a thrust at her body, and it was so fast and so plain that Riarhin only just got Remorse to it. She took it on the flat, angled, and let it go past her right side, the cut side, close enough that she felt the edge tug at her coat, and the Queen was already back out of her reach before Riarhin could answer.

The Queen recovered into the same low guard. Riarhin had barely set her foot when the point rose again.

They went round.

Veralyn did not wait on a wall as Tylan had. She came at Riarhin again and again, in short hard runs, each one a question, each one withdrawn the moment it was answered. The light sword came in high, low, at the face, at the leading hand. Riarhin gave ground, turned, angled the blows away, went round the room with her back to the candles and the fire flaring at the edge of her eye. She did not try to go in. She could not. Every time she looked for the way in, the Queen’s point was already there.

She was getting slower. She could feel it. The breath would not go deep enough, and the side was wet and hot, and the shoulder she could not lift was pulling her whole body a little off its line. The Queen could see it too. The Queen’s runs were getting longer.

On the sixth run, Veralyn gave her the throat.

It was a high cut, overhand, the whole weight of the Queen’s body behind it, and it came down at Riarhin’s head, and Riarhin took it on Remorse and turned it away, and the Queen’s arms went up and over with the force of it, and for the length of a heartbeat her throat and the whole of her chest were open under the lifted blade.

It was a real opening. Riarhin saw it the way she saw everything, exactly, in the candlelight: the soft place under the jaw, the pulse, the black ribbon, the little brass case.

She saw too where the Queen’s feet were, and where her weight was, and how fast the light sword could come back down from where it was. She had watched it come back six times. If she went for the throat now, she would reach it, perhaps. And the sword would come down across her head or her neck in the same breath, from above, with the Queen’s whole weight behind it. Saikou might be bringing Margery through the house already. Riarhin had no word from either of them.

Her weight stayed over the rear foot. She let the throat go.

And in the same breath, because the Queen’s arms were still up and there was one thing within reach that she could finish, she brought Remorse across in a short flat cut, not at the throat, a hand’s breadth below it, and the black ribbon parted under the edge.

The Queen’s sword came down.

She had chosen the ribbon. She turned her head and shoulders out of its line. The light sword cut across the outside of her left upper arm. The impact jarred her grip open. She caught the hilt against her thumb and closed her fingers before it could fall.

The green case fell.

It fell between them onto the clear floor, and struck, and rolled. Riarhin heard it go. She did not look at it. The Queen did.

It was very small. The Queen’s eyes went down to the floor for no longer than a blink, and came back up. Riarhin did not know what the Queen felt, or whether she felt anything; she did not know whether anything in the room had changed. She had not expected to be able to tell. Nothing flared. Nothing went out. The candles burned exactly as they had. The fire at the Queen’s back went on roaring up the chimney. The brass case lay against the leg of the table by the window, beyond the Queen’s left foot.

The Queen stepped back to her mark.

The outside of Riarhin’s left arm burned. She could close her fingers on the hilt, but raising the point made the arm shake. The cut across her ribs was pulling at every breath. She had perhaps one more thing in her. Perhaps two.

The Queen could see that too.

She came again. Not a run this time. She came slowly, with her point level at Riarhin’s chest and her feet very careful on the boards, walking her back towards the fire. She had seen the arm. She was going to take her time now.

Riarhin went back. She went back with the fire behind her and the Queen coming on, one step, another, with the candles on the floor in the corners and the heat of the hearth coming up through the back of her coat. She lowered her right hand beside her thigh and bent the wrist. Toothpick’s hilt pressed against the heel of her hand inside the sleeve. She worked it free with her fingers.

The Queen advanced another step. Riarhin gave ground until heat struck the back of her calves, then turned along the edge of the hearth. She could not raise the right arm. From beside her hip she flicked the knife upwards at Veralyn’s face.

It was a bad throw. It was the best she had. Veralyn turned her face and beat it aside with the sword. Her leading foot came down beside the raised hearthstone, towards the place Riarhin had just left.

The knife went ringing into the corner among the candles. One went over.

The Queen’s point was up, and away.

Riarhin drove off her rear foot from the Queen’s sword side. Remorse’s point rose beneath the breastbone. She kept her left elbow close and carried the thrust with her hips, entering shoulder to shoulder before Veralyn could turn clear of the hearthstone.

Veralyn tried to bring Shadowsteel down. Her forearm struck Riarhin’s back; the hilt hit between her shoulders and drove the breath out of her. Riarhin stayed against her. The blade scraped the hearth and fell.

They went down together, by the hearth.

The Queen was on her back on the clear floor, with the firelight on her face and her hair come loose, and Riarhin was on her knees beside her with her hand still on the hilt, because she could not make it let go. The Queen’s breath was coming short and wet. Her eyes were open.

She did not look at Riarhin. She looked past her, at the door at the far end of the dressing room, the one that gave onto the nursery.

Riarhin did not say anything.

The Queen’s lips moved. There was no sound. Then there was a little sound, and her eyes stayed on the nursery door, and did not move again.

Riarhin knelt by the hearth for a long time.

She could not have said how long. The fire burned. The candle that had gone over in the corner had gone out. Somewhere beyond the wing, a long way off, a dog was barking. She could hear her own breath, which would not go deep enough, and the blood in her ears, and nothing else. Nobody came. Nobody had come.

When she could, she let go of Remorse, finger by finger. Her fingers had cramped round the hilt. She eased them open against the guard.

The green case lay by the leg of the table where it had rolled.

She looked at it for a while. Then she felt in the inside pocket of her coat, on the right, the pocket where she had carried it all the way north, and found the piece of linen that was still there, because she had never taken it out. It had been washed since Tigist gave it to her, and a corner mended. She had folded it smaller and smaller over the years until it was a square the size of her palm.

She did not touch the case with her hand. She laid the linen over it on the floor and folded the corners under and picked it up in the linen, and it was a small hard shape the size of a coin, and no warmer than her hand had made it.

She put it in the pocket on the right.

Then she tried to stand, and could not, and sat down on the floor by the Queen with her back against the dragged table and Remorse across her knees, and waited for her breath to come back, and it did not come back very far.

Part Six — Those Who Arrive

Chapter Twenty-Seven — Those Who Arrive

She heard them in the passage before she saw them. Two sets of feet, one quick and one very even, coming along from the head of the main stair, not from the linen stair. They stopped at the bedchamber door.

Riarhin got up.

She did it by the table, with her right hand on its edge, and it took most of what she had. She got her feet under her. She tried to straighten. Her next breath would not go deep enough, and she stayed as she was, bent a little at the waist, with Remorse hanging from her left hand because the hand would not do anything else.

Margery stopped in the doorway.

Saikou stood behind Margery. She put out a hand without looking at him, and he stopped too.

He had the grey cloth still over his head and shoulders. It had slipped back; his face was very white. Margery wore a dark cloak over what looked like a nightgown, and her hair was down, and there was mud on her shoes to the ankle. She had come across the river road on foot. She had come through the palace in her nightgown behind a man in a mourner’s cloth, past whatever there was to pass, and she had stopped in the doorway of the Queen’s bedchamber and put out her hand to stop him, and now she was looking.
