She looked at the floor. At the Queen on it, by the hearth, with her hair loose and her eyes open towards the far door. At the light sword lying where it had fallen. At the table dragged aside and the papers heaped on the bed and the candles burning in every corner, one of them overturned. She looked at all of it. She did not make a sound.

There was blood drying in the creases of Riarhin’s hand. She could not tell whose it was.

“Matylde?” she asked.

“I’ll go to her.”

Margery’s eyes moved to the sword, then back to Riarhin’s face. Riarhin remembered her moving a lamp so that a guest would not have its light in her eyes.

She lowered the point until it touched the floor.

Margery came past her. She did not go round. She came straight across the lit floor past the Queen’s body without looking down at it again, through the dressing room, among the presses and the gowns, to the far door. At the door she turned.

“Wash before she sees you.”

Riarhin looked at the hand holding Remorse.

Margery knocked on the nursery door and said a name, and the nurse’s voice answered, frightened, and Margery said her own name, and something else, quietly, that Riarhin did not hear. The bar went up. The door opened a hand’s width and Margery went through it and it closed behind her.

Through it, after a moment, Riarhin heard the child. She was not crying. She was talking, in the high cross voice of a child woken in the night, about almonds. Margery’s voice answered her, low and even, the way it had answered a screaming girl in a loom-room: hush, hush, I know, I know.

Riarhin stood in the Queen’s bedchamber with the sword touching the floor.

There was a ewer on the washstand by the bed, and a basin. She went to it. It was four steps and she took them one at a time. She laid Remorse along the washstand. The ewer was too heavy. She leaned its lip over the basin with both hands and let a little water out.

The water went dark at once. She poured it into the slop pail and poured more. She rubbed at the creases of her hands. Blood remained under the nails. She wet the corner of a towel and brought it towards her face, but the right shoulder stopped her halfway.

When she turned round Saikou was still in the doorway, where Margery had stopped him.

He had not come in. He was looking at her.

“You’re hurt,” he said.

“Yes.”

“How badly?”

“I don’t know. The side. The arm.” She looked down at herself. Her coat was dark all down the right side, and the left sleeve was dark from the shoulder, and she could not tell where one stopped and the other began. “I can walk.”

He came in then. He came across the room to her, past the Queen, and he looked down at the Queen once as he passed, and did not stop, and did not look again. He took the linen off the top of the Queen’s washstand, a folded towel, and pressed it hard against Riarhin’s side under the coat, and took her right hand and put it over the towel.

“Hold that,” he said. “Hard. Harder.”

She held it.

He looked at the left arm. He opened the torn sleeve with his long fingers and looked, and his face did not change, and he took the black robe from the end of the bed and tore a strip from its hem, and bound it round her arm above the cut, tight, and knotted it with his teeth.

“There,” he said. “That will do until it doesn’t.” He looked at her face. “Is it in your pocket?”

She knew what he meant.

“Yes.”

“Wrapped?”

“In the linen. I didn’t touch it.”

He let out a breath.

“Good,” he said. “Leave it there. Don’t give it to me. Not now.”

By the fallen candle he found Toothpick. He wiped the wax and blood from it, then waited while she turned her wrist to let him slide it into the sleeve sheath.

He picked up Remorse from the washstand, wiped the blade on the Queen’s robe where it lay over the end of the bed, and put it into her left hand, and closed her fingers round it, because she could not. “Now we go.”

They went down by the main stair.

Saikou said the linen stair was blood to the bottom, and there was a man on it, and she said she knew, and he said nothing else. The main stair was dark and empty. At the foot of it the lower hall was shut, and behind its door there were voices, men’s voices, raised, and a table going over, and somebody shouting a name. The riders from Serrat had found something. Riarhin did not know what. She did not stop to learn.

The ground-floor passage to the old range went through the iron doors. They stood open. Beside them, in the doorway of a little room with a table and a chair, a man sat in the chair with his feet up on a stool and a cup of wine at his elbow and his chin on his chest, as though he had fallen asleep after the ninth bell with his gout. The little room’s lock hung from one screw. A key lay beside the cup with its cut chain still attached. Somebody had reached him, then been called away before taking it.

Saikou walked past him at a mourner’s pace, and she walked beside him, and neither of them looked.

Beyond the iron doors there was a short passage and a stair going up, on the left, to the covered bridge.

The door at the top of that stair was split.

It was oak, and it opened outward, towards the old range, and it had been held shut from this side, and somebody on the other side had broken it with an axe. The top half hung by one hinge. Through the hole where the lower panels had been there was the covered bridge, dark, with the cold coming through it, and nobody on it. Whoever had broken the door had gone on, down, into the new wing, somewhere else. Or back. There were bootprints in blood on the bottom step of the stair going both ways.

At the top of the stair, in the doorway, with his back against what was left of the frame, sat Amari.

Saikou stopped.

He stopped at the foot of the stair, and stood there, and for a long moment he did not do anything at all.

Amari was sitting with his legs out in front of him on the landing and his broad shoulders against the splintered frame and his head a little on one side, as he had sat on the deck of Davra’s ship with the book open on his knee. The cudgel was under his hand. The vintner’s cap lay on the landing beside him. There was a great deal of blood on the landing. He had held the door. He had held it until it came to pieces round him, and then he had held the doorway.

Saikou went up the stair.

He went up slowly, one step at a time, and knelt on the landing in the blood, and put his hand on Amari’s shoulder, the way he had put it there once on the deck of the ship and taken it away again. He did not take it away this time. He knelt there with his hand on his friend’s shoulder and his head bowed, and Riarhin stood at the foot of the stair holding the towel against her side, and did not go up, because there was not room on the landing for anybody but the two of them.

After a while Saikou picked up the vintner’s cap from the landing. He looked at it. Then he put it inside his own coat, against his chest, and buttoned the coat over it.

He came down the stair.

“We have to go,” he said. His voice was quite level. “There is nothing on the bridge. They have gone past him. They will come back.”

“Saikou—”

“Later.” He took her right arm, above the elbow, the arm that was holding the towel, and held it so that she would not let go of the towel. “I will write him in. I said I would. Later. Now we go.”

The old range court was full of torchlight.

They came out into it through a door at the foot of the stair and stopped in the shadow of the arch, and Riarhin saw men everywhere: young men in shirts and breeches with swords in their hands and torches, running, shouting, going in and out of doorways. Two of them had the brindled dog on a rope; it was barking, straining towards the harness room. Four more were standing in a knot by the chapel door round something on the ground that Riarhin could not see, with their torches held low. One of them was on his knees.

Nobody was looking at the arch.

Across the court, the chapel tower stood up against the sky. Its little door at the foot was open. There was light in the ringing room. As Riarhin watched, somebody inside it put his weight on a rope, and above them, in the dark, after a pause, the bell began to ring.

It did not ring twelve and a pause and the long peal for the new year. It rang without any pause at all: the long ringing, on and on, stroke after stroke after stroke, the ringing that meant arms. Every bar in the palace would be going down now. Every man in the household would be coming out with a sword. In the new wing a man with a key on a chain was supposed to be going round and locking the iron doors.

The key had been left beside his cup. She could not count on it staying there. Riarhin stood in the arch and listened to it ring, and did not know whether Tavin had seen to the rope so that it could be put right, as he had said he would, and somebody had put it right; or whether Tavin was in the ringing room still, or had gone, or was somewhere else. She did not know whether it was the boy on the rope. She could not see from the arch.

“The river gate,” said Saikou.

It was on the far side of the court. It was open. Nobody was near it. The men with the torches were all at the chapel door and at the harness room and at the foot of the tower.

Saikou drew the grey cloth forward over his head. There was blood on the hem of it from the landing.

“Behind me,” he said. “Stay in the shade. If they speak to you, I can’t answer for you.”

He released her arm.

“Slowly. Like somebody walking behind the dead.”

She walked.

Saikou crossed at a bier’s pace. Riarhin followed a few steps behind, along the shadow of the wall. A man with a torch ran past him and turned towards her. She bent over the towel at her side.

“You there—”

Someone shouted from the tower. The man lifted his torch, looking between Riarhin and the open tower door. Then he ran towards the shout. She straightened enough to follow Saikou. He had not turned.

They went out through the river gate onto the river path, into the dark, and the bell went on ringing behind them.

Chapter Twenty-Eight — The Man with Na’ar’s Sword

Cadan was waiting in the loft.

He was sitting at the table with his hands folded on it, exactly where he had sat when he asked her whom she intended him to kill, and there was a candle lit at his elbow, and he had washed. His sleeves were wet to the elbow. There was a long cut on the back of his left hand that he had bound with a strip of his own shirt, neatly.

He stood when they came up the stair. He looked at Riarhin, and at the towel under her hand, and at Saikou behind her, and at the space behind Saikou where nobody was.

“Sit her down,” he said. “There. By the fire. I’ll heat water.”

He heated water. He did it without asking anything. Saikou got her coat off and her shirt off and the towel away from her side, and looked at what was under it, and said something in his own language that she had never heard him say. Cadan brought the water and a clean cloth and a needle and thread from Tavin’s things, and held the candle while Saikou worked, and did not look away from what Saikou was doing, and did not look at her face. It hurt a great deal. She held on to the edge of the table with her right hand and did not make much noise.

When it was done, and the arm was done, and she was sitting by the fire in Amari’s spare shirt with a blanket round her, Cadan sat down across from her again and folded his hands.

“It is done,” he said. “As you said.”

She looked at him.

“Tell me.”

He told her.

He had shut the brindled bitch in the harness room with a bone before the evening office. She had gone in willingly; she liked him. He had waited in the stable passage. Guichard had come round just before the eleventh bell, alone, with a lantern, walking the round as he always did, and had seen Cadan in the passage and smiled and said you’re late for the vigil, brother, and Cadan had said that he was, and Guichard had come close to say something else, and Cadan had killed him. Quietly. He had put him in the empty stall at the end, under straw.

“He did not know,” said Cadan. “Until the end. I want you to know that. I did it so that he would not know until the end.” He looked at the candle. “He was a good officer. He asked after my knees.”

Riarhin said nothing.

“One of his men came into the passage while I was moving him,” said Cadan. “A boy. Seventeen. He had come to find the lieutenant because the vigil was starting. He saw me. He did not draw. He stood in the passage with his mouth open.” Cadan turned his bound hand over on the table. “You said, if one of his men comes on you and does not draw, you walk away. You do not silence a witness. So I walked away. I walked past him, out of the passage, across the court, to the river gate. He did not follow me for twenty paces. Then he began to shout.”

“And your hand?”

“That was another one. At the gate. He had heard the boy shouting. He drew on me.” Cadan looked at the cut. “You said, what I must to stop him drawing again, and no more. I broke his arm. He will mend.” He paused. “I came here. I have waited. I did not go back. I heard the bell. I did not go back.”

“No.”

“I would have liked to,” said Cadan. “I want you to know that too. I sat here and heard the bell ringing arms and I would have liked very much to go back and see. It was not my judgment that kept me here.” He met her eyes. “It was yours. You said it to me clearly.”

“I know.”

“Good,” said Cadan, and smiled at her, and got up to put more wood on the fire.

Aurine’s note was on the table under the candlestick. Cadan said a carter’s boy had brought it at the tenth bell and gone away without waiting for anything.

It was short. It was written in a small, even hand on the back of a paper that had once held gout powder.

It went up at the fourth bell. He took it at the ninth. I watched the apothecary’s window go dark and then I got into the cart. It smells of pepper. I am not coming back. Tell Saikou I will write, and he may decide whether he wants to know where from. Tell him the lady will hear nothing of this from me. That is the only gift I have for him and I would like him to know it is one. A.

Saikou read it standing by the fire. He read it twice. Then he folded it and put it inside his coat, with Amari’s cap.

“Thank you,” he said, to nobody.

Later, while the water heated again, he told Riarhin how he had fetched Margery, because she asked, and because he said Amari would have wanted it written down.

The porter at Margery’s gate on the river road had not wanted to wake her. It was the middle of the night; it was Midwinter; the man at the gate had a grey cloth over his head and would not give his name. Saikou had not argued. He could not; the cloth did not survive a conversation. He had taken it off, and stood in the lane in his own face, and said to the porter that he was the southern scholar who had sat up with the girl Rosine, and that he had come for the sake of a child, and that his mistress had said she would open the door and listen. The porter had gone in. Margery had come down in her nightgown with a cloak over it and her hair down, and stood in her own doorway, and listened. She had not asked which child. She had asked whether it was now. He had said yes. She had gone back in for her shoes, and come out in the first pair she found, which were the stout ones she kept for the ground under the fruit trees, and they had gone.

“The river gate was open for the laundry carts,” said Saikou. “The iron doors were open. I kept the cloth back once Margery was beside me. People looked at her. We passed the lower hall. The men inside were shouting. One of them came out into the passage with a lamp, and saw Margery, and knew her, and stopped. She said to him, the Queen has sent for me. In a voice I had not heard her use. He stood aside.” Saikou was quiet a moment. “He let her pass. I followed before he could think to ask about me.”

The magpie came at first light.

Riarhin was not asleep. She was sitting by the fire with the blanket round her, because she could not lie down without the side pulling, and she heard it at the hoist door: a scrabbling of claws on the sill and a harsh, anxious chatter. Saikou got up and opened the hoist door, and the grey light came in with the smell of the river, and Penny came in on it and landed on the table.

She was alone.

She walked up and down the table, the length of it, twice, with her head going from side to side. Then she stopped in front of Tavin’s place, where there was a crust of bread from the night before, and did not eat it. She stood and looked at it.

Nobody said anything.

After a while Cadan put out his hand, very slowly, palm up, on the table, and the magpie looked at it, and then walked onto it, and he drew his hand in against his chest and sat with her there, not moving, while the light came up.

They came through the gap in the wall an hour after dawn.

Riarhin heard them before she saw them, as she had heard Margery and Saikou in the passage: footsteps, two sets, one slow and uneven with a stick in it, one lighter and dragging. Saikou was at the head of the stair at once with a knife in his hand. Cadan put the magpie on the table and stood up.

A man came up the stair into the loft.

He was a young man, worn thin about the mouth. He was not tall. He had a stick in his left hand and he leaned on it hard, and he had a sword on his back, wrapped in oilcloth, the hilt showing. He had his right arm round a woman. She was thin, in a plain linen shift with a man’s coat over it, and her hair was loose, and she was walking because he was making her walk, and her face was grey.

Riarhin knew the woman.

She was on her feet before she knew it, with the blanket falling and the side tearing at her, and across the loft, and she had Lyra by the shoulders, and Lyra looked at her with enormous dark eyes in a face that had gone thin and old, and said, “Oh,” and then, “You came,” and then did not say anything else, because Riarhin was holding her and she was holding Riarhin and neither of them could.

Afterwards, when Lyra was by the fire in the blanket with a cup of Cadan’s hot water in both hands and her eyes shut, Riarhin looked at the man.

He was standing by the head of the stair where he had stopped. He had not sat down. He had put Lyra into Riarhin’s arms and then stepped back, and stood there, leaning on his stick, watching them, with an expression Riarhin could not read.

She did not know him.

His face, his hands: neither familiar. But the hilt over his shoulder was Na’ar’s. She knew every turn of that wrapping.

“Who are you?” she said.

The man looked at her. Something happened in his face.

“It’s me,” he said.

She waited.

“Berent,” he said.

She heard it. She heard the word quite clearly. She knew the name; she knew it very well. It was the name of a knight who had come to Ubral with a letter, who had ridden the rear of Ewen’s train, who had been carried up the plank at Scarrow with Na’ar’s sword across his knees, who had counted the tannery stair in forty. She knew all of that. She looked at the man in front of her, and the name went into her and did not attach to anything. It slid off him. It was like being told that a stranger in the street was your brother. You heard the words. You looked at the face. Nothing joined.

“Berent went with Nessa,” she said. “To the old range. For Lyra.”

“Yes.”

“Where is he?”

The man closed his eyes.

“Here,” he said. “I’m here. Riarhin. It’s me.” He opened them. “You don’t know me. She said you wouldn’t. She said nobody would.”

He told it standing, because he would not sit.

The man on the stool had not looked up. Nessa had sung, and he had listened, and Berent had gone up the back stair slow, as he had practised, and lifted the bar, and Lyra had been awake, sitting on the bed in the dark in her shift, because she had heard the vigil go by below her window and the house go quiet, and had known something was wrong. He had brought her out. He had got her to the head of the stair.

And the two men from the stair above had come back.

They had heard shouting in the court on their way to the chapel and turned back to see to their prisoner, and they had come round the turn of the stair below Berent and Lyra with their swords out and a lantern, and the man with the lantern had held it up, and looked at Berent’s face, and known him.

“He said my name,” said the man by the stair. “He said, that’s the traitor’s knight. That’s Elyas’s man. He knew me from the Mound’s house. The night we got Mara out. He’d been in the yard with a torch.” His voice was quite steady. “He said, there’s a price on him. And they came up.”

He had had Lyra on his arm and a stick in his hand and a stair under a leg that would not go fast. He had not been able to draw Na’ar’s sword without letting go of one of them.

Nessa had been at the foot of the passage. She had come up behind the two men. She had said, very clearly, over their shoulders, Berent. Do you want to live? And he had said yes.

“She said, your name, Berent. As we spoke of it. I said yes again. Then she sang.”

“It was the door song,” said the man. “The one she always sings. With the ending she found. It went up and turned and came down.” He stopped. “And they looked at me. The one with the lantern held it up and looked right at me. And his face went—” He did not finish. “He didn’t know me. He’d said my name a moment before, and he looked at me and he didn’t know me. He said, who are you? And I didn’t say. And he said to the other one, where’s the knight? And the other one said, what knight?”

Nobody in the loft moved.

“Nessa was still singing. I pulled Lyra down behind me. The man with the lantern caught the cloak Nessa had brought for her.”

Lyra’s fingers tightened on the cup.

“The clasp,” she said. “It caught at my throat.”

“She got the clasp open,” said the man. “He had the cloak and the lantern and his sword under his arm. Nessa pushed the other one into him. I got Lyra round the turn. We heard them coming, but she shut the kitchen door behind us and dropped the bar. They knew their prisoner was gone. They were shouting for her when we reached the yard.”

“And Nessa?”

He looked at Lyra, by the fire, with her eyes shut.

“There was a man on the wall,” he said. “In the yard. With a crossbow. He wasn’t looking for us. He was looking for anybody. He loosed at the first thing that moved. She was the first thing that moved.” His voice did not change. “I carried her to the wall. There was a gap. I put my coat round Lyra while Nessa leaned against the stones. She said, go on. Take her. Go on. She said it three times. She was still saying it.” He looked at the floor. “I went on.”

Riarhin stood in the loft and looked at the man by the stair who said he was Berent, and she believed every word he had said, and she did not know him.

She did the only thing she could think of.

There was paper on the table. There was the stub of pencil in her sleeve, beside Toothpick’s hilt. She sat down at the table, which pulled at her side, and she wrote on the paper in large plain letters:

The man with Na’ar’s sword is Berent. He brought Lyra out. Nessa sang the door song on the stair to save his life, and nobody can know him now. Nessa is dead. Believe him.

She read it. She looked up at the man by the stair.

She held it out to him.

He came across the loft, slowly, on his stick, and took it, and read it. His hand was not steady.

“Keep it,” said Riarhin. “Show it to me. Whenever I—” She did not know how to say it. “Whenever you need to. I’ll know my hand. I’ll believe it.”

He looked at the paper for a long time. Then he folded it, carefully, along the middle, and put it inside his coat, over his heart, and buttoned the coat.

“She told me my name might stop belonging to me,” he said. “Before we went. I thought—” He took out the paper again without unfolding it. “I thought it would be the guards.”

Lyra had opened her eyes. She was looking at him. She had been looking at him, Riarhin realised, for some time: at his face, carefully, the way Lyra looked at a return that did not agree with itself.

“I don’t know you,” said Lyra. Her voice was cracked from disuse. “I’m sorry. You carried me down a stair and I don’t know you.” She held out her hand. “Tell me again. What’s your name.”

He went and crouched by her, awkwardly, with his bad leg out to the side, and took her hand.

“Berent,” he said.

Lyra listened to it. Riarhin watched her listen, and saw it go in, and saw it not hold.

“Berent,” Lyra repeated. She said it very carefully, as she had once, Riarhin thought, said the names of people on a bench at a river house whom she would not write down. “Berent. I’ll say it every day.”

He bowed his head over her hand.

After a while he stood up and went back to the head of the stair and sat down on the top step with his stick across his knees and Na’ar’s sword on his back and the paper over his heart, and looked out through the hoist door at the river, and did not say anything else for a long time.

Chapter Twenty-Nine — Paid on Delivery

Saikou told her on the second night, when the searches had moved to the other side of the river.

They had not left the loft all day. They could not. The bell had rung arms until dawn and then stopped, and after it stopped the city had gone very quiet, and then in the middle of the morning the gates had been shut, all of them, and men in the Queen’s colours had begun going through the old town street by street. They had come down the tanners’ street at noon. Tavin’s landlord with no teeth had stood in his doorway and told them that he let anything to anybody and asked nothing, and they had looked at the smell and at him and gone on. In the afternoon they had gone across the bridge to the new town, and the sound of doors being knocked on had gone with them.

Lyra slept most of the day by the fire. The man with Na’ar’s sword sat on the top step of the stair. Cadan kept the fire in and fed the magpie, who would eat now from his hand and from nobody else’s. Riarhin sat at the table with her side stitched and her arm bound and did nothing at all, because there was nothing she could do.

Saikou wrote.

He sat at the other end of the table with Amari’s book open beside him and a sheaf of his own papers in front of him, and wrote, in his small neat hand, for most of the day. He did not show her what. Once she saw him stop and sit with the pen lifted for a long time, looking at nothing, and then go on.

In the evening he covered the mirror, and then did not. He sat with it on his knee, uncovered, and she watched the glass go grey from the rim inwards, as it had at Fairport, as a window does when somebody breathes on it from the other side. He looked at it for a long time. Then he covered it, and put it away, and came and sat down across from her.

“I have to tell you something,” he said, “and you are going to be angry, and I would rather you were angry now than later, when there is more to do.”

He told it to her plainly, in order, as he told everything.

At Fairport, in Mother Harte’s kitchen, he had seen her burn a list. He had seen a name on one of the pieces before it went black: Mairi. He had seen the word harbour on another. He had known about the woman in Ubral since Carrickane; he had known that there was a letter in Riarhin’s coat that she touched eleven times a morning and would not open. He had known that she had tried to send a warning and had not been able to send the list, because the list was a map.

“So I sent it,” he said.

“You—”

“Not the list. I did not have the list. I had Mairi and harbour and the name of the house at Glenruan, and what Berent had told me on Davra’s ship about the road he had taken and whose door he had knocked on. It was enough.” He folded his hands on the table. “I sent it to my mother.”

Riarhin sat very still.

“Through the Witness,” said Saikou. “With the glass. My mother knows where to have testimony heard. I asked that mine be carried to her.” He looked towards his satchel. “I spoke the names I had. I put my own to the request. I could not afterwards pretend that I had asked for something smaller.”

“What did you ask her?”

“For a ship. To Kilrenny. To take whoever in the northern houses would go, before the spring, to somewhere she had no house.” He did not look away. “She wanted to bring them to Kassamira. She has a great deal of house in Kassamira. She has a great deal of everything in Kassamira. People who go to Kassamira under her seal stay there as long as she likes. I said no. I said Silkstrand. She has no house in Silkstrand. She has an agent who owes her money and a warehouse she does not own.” He paused. “She did not like that. It took some time.”

“And?”

“And they came in at Silkstrand thirteen days ago,” said Saikou. “The confirmation came an hour ago, through the Witness. Her factor had given his account of their arrival. They are in a lodging on the harbour, paid for a season. They may leave it when they like.”

Riarhin found that she had stood up. She did not remember doing it. Her side had torn at the stitches and she could feel it bleeding again under the bandage, and she did not care.

“Who?” she said.

“Ailsa,” said Saikou.

She held on to the edge of the table.

“Did she agree to go?”

“Yes. My mother’s factor put in at Kilrenny with a letter under our seal, and it said that passage was paid, and where to, and that anybody who came aboard could leave at the other end and owe nothing. It said that it was sent on account of the knight who had come to Glenruan. It said it could not say more.” He looked at her. “Ailsa went to the house at Glenruan with the letter and they talked for a day. The factor waited. Your cousin Tavish had had your letter the week before, about the road the knight took. He had read it in the workroom and not in the hall.”

“Who else?”

“Iona. Tavish sent her. His heir. He said somebody from the house ought to be where nobody could ask them anything.” Saikou’s voice was very even. “Mairi and her son. Mairi said she was not staying where men might come asking about a knight who had slept in her workroom. Donnel’s girl, who works for Ailsa; her father said she might, and she wanted to.” He paused. “Tavish and Elspeth stayed. Ailsa’s children stayed with their father. He would not let them go and they did not want to. The rest of the Rocke house stayed. Tavish said a house that runs is a house that has been told it is guilty, and he would sooner be asked.”

Riarhin stood with her hand on the table.

“And Ailsa can leave. The lodging. The factor. She’s not—”

“She is not anybody’s,” said Saikou. “She is in a lodging on the harbour at Silkstrand with Iona and Mairi and a boy and a girl who talks the whole time, and the rent is paid for a season, and she may walk out of the door this evening and go wherever she likes, and nobody will stop her or ask her for anything.” He looked at her. “That was the whole of what I asked for. I required her answer to be witnessed too.”

It went through her like cold water.

She sat down. She did not mean to. Her knees went and she sat, on the bench, with her hand flat on the table, and for a long moment she could not think of anything at all except a room over a chandler’s at Kilrenny, with a window that looked at the boats, standing empty, full of willow.

Ailsa had left it. Ailsa had had a letter that said I don’t know when I can come. I want to, and a week later a factor with a seal, and she had gone to Glenruan and talked for a day, and got on a ship to a city she had never seen, because of a knight who had knocked on a door.

She was safe.

Riarhin put both hands over her face. Her shoulders shook, and shook again, and she did not try to stop them. Saikou did not move or speak. When at last she took her hands away, he was looking at the fire, and he went on looking at it until she had wiped her face on the blanket.

She will be safe.

And the price for that safety?

Not borne by you.

Riarhin looked across the table at Saikou.

“What did it cost you?”

He did not answer at once.

“A great deal,” he said at last. “More than I would like to say. My mother does not do things for nothing, even for me. Especially for me.” He turned one hand over on the table. “She will want something. She will tell me what when she has decided. I agreed before she told me. That was the only way she would do it quickly.”

“You agreed to a price you don’t know.”

“Yes.”

“For my—” She could not find the word. “For people you’ve never met.”

“For people you would not have been able to look at,” said Saikou, “if the men came up the road in the spring.” His voice did not change. “I did not want you to add to your regrets. And I need you for what comes next.”

“Is that the price of their freedom?” she said.

“No,” said Saikou. “That was paid on delivery.”

The anger came then. It came up through the cold and she let it.

“You had no right,” she said. “You had no right to meddle.”

He looked at her.

“According to whom?”

She opened her mouth.

Her side pulled at the stitches. She pressed her hand over the dressing and waited for the pain to settle. Saikou left his hands folded on the table.

“I’m still angry,” she said.

“I know.”

“I don’t know when I’ll stop.”

“That is all right,” said Saikou. “I am in no hurry.”

They sat for a while without speaking.

Amari’s book lay open on the table between them. It was open at the last page he had written in, and his neat hand was there. The last line was the one he had written in the yard doorway with the book against his arm: Going home. Above it was three and eleven, and a button. On the page before, squeezed in between two other entries in a smaller hand, as though there had not been room, there was a line Riarhin did not understand: Fourth night after. T. Not argued. Beneath Going home there was nothing.

Saikou looked at it.

“He would have liked to know,” he said, “that it was paid on delivery.” He put his long finger on the page, beside the last line. “He would have wanted it in the book. What it cost, and who paid, and when. He always said the difference between a good commissariat and a bad one was whether anybody wrote down what the thing had cost after it was done.” He was quiet for a moment. “I will write it in. Under his. In my hand. It will look wrong.”

“Write it anyway.”

“Yes.”

He picked up the pen. A drop fell onto the page before he set the nib down. He touched it with the edge of his sleeve, then put the pen aside and covered his mouth.

Riarhin stayed at the table. When he reached for the pen again she moved the lamp closer. He wiped his face with the heel of his hand and wrote a single line beneath Amari’s.

She did not read it. It was not hers.

Chapter Thirty — What Is Left to Do

The boy came on the third morning, following the magpie.

Riarhin heard him before anybody else did. She had been awake since before light, sitting by the fire with the side pulling, and she heard a child’s voice in the tanners’ street below, calling, not a name, a sound: the chirrup Tavin used to make with his tongue against his teeth when he wanted Penny to come to his wrist. It came again. On the table, where she had spent the night asleep beside Tavin’s crust, the magpie lifted her head.

Cadan opened the hoist door. The magpie went out through it like a thrown stone.

When Cadan brought the boy up the stair a few minutes later, Penny was on his shoulder with her beak in his hair.

He was twelve. He had a thin face and chapped hands and the smell of candle-tallow and brass polish about him, and he stood at the top of the stair and looked round the loft at all of them, at Riarhin by the fire and Lyra in her blanket and Saikou at the table and the man with Na’ar’s sword on the step, and his face crumpled, and he did not cry, because he was twelve and had decided not to.

“She came to the tower,” he said. “The bird. Yesterday. She sat on the rope. I knew she’d go back to wherever—” He stopped. “I followed her. I’m sorry. I didn’t tell anybody. I don’t know who you are.”

“Jory,” said Riarhin.

He stared at her.

“He told us about you,” she said. “The man with the bird. He said you told her about the bell. Every stroke.”

The boy’s face held for a moment longer and then did not.

He told them when he could.

He had been in the ringing room with Tavin and the bird from the evening office. Tavin had brought a sugared bun and had knotted the rope on the third turn above the grip, a knot Jory had never seen, very tight, very neat, so that the rope would not run through the guide when it was pulled. Tavin had showed him the knot. He had made Jory undo it and do it up again, twice, so that Jory would know how.

“He said, so it can be put right after,” said Jory. “He said, so they don’t turn you off because your bell didn’t ring. He said, if anybody asks you, it was a knot you didn’t know about, and you found it, and you undid it, and you rang.” The boy looked at the floor. “He said to say it was his knot.”

Midnight had come and the bell had not rung, and Jory had sat on the floor of the ringing room with the magpie on his knee and cried a little, because it was the first Midwinter in forty years it had not rung, and it was his rope. And Tavin had sat beside him and told him about a ferryman who charged eleven marks and a hat, and he had laughed.

Then the men had come.

They had come across the court from the covered bridge, four of them, running. Jory had heard shouting there since before midnight. Then the bell failed to ring, and somebody called up to the tower to ask what was wrong. They had come to the tower to find out why. Tavin had heard them on the stones.

“He opened the door,” said Jory. “The little door. At the bottom. He put me out of it. He said, go round by the chapel wall, go on, go now, you’ve done nothing, you’re a boy who was frightened and ran. He said, leave the knot till they tell you. And he gave me the bird.” He put his hand up to Penny on his shoulder. “And then he shut the door with him inside. And they came to it. And he didn’t open it.”

He stopped.

“I was by the chapel wall,” he said. “I heard. They broke the door. It took them a long time. He had it barred with the bench. And then they were inside and there was a lot of noise and then there wasn’t.” He looked up. “And then the sacristan found me by the wall and shouted at me to get in there and ring arms, so I went in. He was on the floor by the rope. They’d—he was—” He could not say it. “I undid the knot. I did it like he showed me. It came undone in one breath. And I rang arms. Because they told me to.”

Nobody in the loft said anything.

“They didn’t turn me off,” said Jory. “The sacristan said I rang it well. He said I did right.” His face crumpled again. “I don’t know if I did right.”

Riarhin got down, slowly, because of her side, until her face was level with his.

“You did what he told you,” she said. “Exactly. He wanted you to have your place after. You have it.”

“He said to say it was his knot.”

“Then say it was his knot,” said Riarhin. “It was.”

Jory had heard the proclamation too. It had been read at the chapel door at dawn on the day after Midwinter, and again at noon, and it had been nailed up at every gate, and he said it over for them the way he said the strokes of the bell, all of it, because a boy who rang a bell had to have a good memory.

Her Majesty the Queen and the Marshal Consort were dead by treason in the night of Midwinter eve, in the Queen’s own house. The Princess Matylde lived, and was unhurt, and was in safe keeping in the household of Mistress Margery by the leave of the council, until the council should otherwise determine. The council summoned for Midwinter would sit notwithstanding, under the presidency of the Chancellor, and would take order for the realm. All gates were shut until further proclamation. A reward was offered for the woman called Riarhin, sometime of the Queen’s household, and for all who had harboured her. And the council had resolved that commissioners should go north with the spring into Ubral, to inquire into the households there that had received the knight Berent, lately the servant of the traitor Elyas, in the year before.

His singsong faltered at their faces. He waited for someone to tell him why.

Riarhin sat by the fire.

Commissioners should go north with the spring into Ubral.

She heard it in Enid’s flat voice, over the needle: His road does not disappear because you take another.

Across the loft, on the top step, the man with Na’ar’s sword had gone very still.

“They mean me,” he said. “The knight Berent.”

Jory looked at him without any recognition at all.

“They’ll look for him,” said the man. “In Ubral. At Glenruan. They’ll ask Tavish whether the knight Berent slept in his hall, and he’ll say yes, because he did, and they’ll ask what he looked like.” He was quiet. “And they won’t be able to tell them. Nobody will. Not even Tavish.”

“That won’t stop them asking,” said Riarhin.

“No,” said the man. “I know.”

Jory had saved another name until last.

“They’ve taken up the laundress,” he said. “For the Queen’s linen. Maheut. From the washing yards. My gran knows her mother. They came for her the morning after.” He looked at Riarhin. “Because there was a woman in her book that nobody could find after. A hired woman. For the beds.” He frowned. “Everybody says she must have known. My gran says she never. My gran says Maheut’s straight as a stair-rod and she’d not put a stranger in the Queen’s book for all the silver in Valora. But they took her up anyway. Her boy’s at my gran’s.”

Riarhin did not move. Then she got up.

It hurt. She did it anyway, and went to the table, where Saikou was sitting with Amari’s book, and held out her hand.

“Paper,” she said.

He gave her a sheet without asking.

She sat down at the table and took the stub of pencil from her sleeve, beside Toothpick’s hilt, and wrote. She wrote it plainly, in her own hand, set out the way she had seen the Valoran clerks set out a statement, so that a clerk could read it without having to guess.

To the Chancellor and the Council.

The woman entered in the linen book of the Queen’s house on the eve of Midwinter as Aine, hired for the night, was myself, Riarhin. I went to the house of the laundress Maheut under false pretences, and did her mother a kindness in order to be trusted, and lied to her about my name and my purpose. She was short of women for the Queen’s beds and hired me for one night because she believed me honest. I did not tell her who I was or what I meant to do. I concealed myself upstairs while she was taking the other women down. She is guilty of nothing but needing a pair of hands. Whatever was done in the Queen’s house that night, I did it, and I answer for it, and she does not.

She signed it. She signed Riarhin, in full, left-handed, which she could just manage with the arm bound, so that the letters were large and uneven and could not be mistaken for anybody else’s.

Then she sat and looked at it.

She read the line about concealing herself. Maheut had called twice, then said all twelve were down. Riarhin could give Pellam her own acts; she could not testify to what Maheut had understood at the empty door. She left the line as it stood. It might get Maheut let go. It would not give her the place back.

“Who will carry it?” said Saikou.

Lyra had woken. She was sitting up in the blanket by the fire, looking at the sheet on the table. Her face was grey and thin and very tired, and her eyes were awake.

“Pellam,” she said.

Riarhin turned.

“Send it to Pellam,” said Lyra. Her voice was still cracked. “Not the council. Not Fayd. The chancellor.” She drew the blanket closer. “He’ll enter it. With the date. In the chancery’s own book. So that if anybody ever tidies the household’s account of that night, there’ll be another place where somebody can see what was said.” She almost smiled. “He calls it duplicate custody. I taught him the phrase.”

Riarhin looked at her for a long moment. Then she folded the sheet and wrote on the outside Master Pellam, the Chancery, by hand, and gave it to Cadan, and Cadan took it down the stair to the landlord with no teeth, who would carry anything to anybody for a coin and ask nothing and not remember a face.

After that she went and sat on the top step of the stair, beside the man with Na’ar’s sword.

He moved over to make room. He did it without looking at her. She sat down carefully, with her side, and they sat together on the step, looking out through the hoist door at the river, where the light had come up grey and flat and a barge was going down on the current with a boy asleep on its cargo.

She did not know him. She looked at his hands on the stick, and his face in profile, and she did not know them.

After a while he took the paper out of his coat, the one she had written, and unfolded it, and held it out to her without a word.

She read it. The man with Na’ar’s sword is Berent. He brought Lyra out. Nessa sang the door song on the stair to save his life, and nobody can know him now. Nessa is dead. Believe him.

It was her hand. She knew her hand. She believed it.

“Berent,” she said.

He nodded, very slightly, and folded the paper, and put it back over his heart.

Part Seven — Names to the River

Chapter Thirty-One — The Gate

It was Berent who said he would go and look.

He said it in the afternoon of the third day, when Jory had gone down the stair to his gran’s and come back up again because he did not want to be at his gran’s, and was sitting by the fire with Penny, telling Cadan about the knot. Riarhin had been sitting at the table since the statement went, doing nothing with her hands. She had asked Jory, once, as gently as she could, what had been done with the man in the tower. Jory had said he did not know. He said they had carried him out through the chapel. He said the sacristan had told him not to look.

Nobody had said anything about Amari, or Nessa. Nobody in the loft knew where they had been taken.

“I’ll go,” said Berent.

He was sitting on the top step, where he had sat for most of three days. He did not turn round.

“They’ll have put them somewhere,” he said. “They always do, after a thing in the Queen’s house. Somewhere people can see. So people know it’s done, and so anybody who knew them comes to look, and can be asked.” He looked at the river. “Somebody ought to go and see where.”

“You can’t,” said Riarhin.

“I’m the only one who can.”

She looked at him. He did turn round then, on the step, awkwardly, with his leg out, and looked back at her.

“There’s a reward for you,” he said. “There’s a reward for all who harboured you. The monk broke a man’s arm at the river gate and there’ll be a boy who saw his face. Saikou walked the Queen’s woman through the palace and a man in the lower hall knew her. Lyra can’t stand up.” He said it all in order, flatly, as he had once reported the weather of a posting-house yard. “There’s a proclamation on every gate with my name on it. The knight Berent. And there isn’t a man in Valora who could pick me out of a crowd of two.” He almost smiled. “You tried. Three days. You’re the best there is at faces and you can’t do it.”

Riarhin did not answer.

“I’ve a stick,” said Berent. “There was a lieutenant at Harrowmere who wrote a lame man from the north road in a report. I know. But half the men at the gates of this city are lame. It’s been a long war.” He turned back to the river. “I’ll go with the boy. He has to go to his gran’s by dark anyway, and his gran lives by the west gate. I’ll be a man walking with a boy. Nobody looks at that.”

“Nobody’s looked at you for three days,” said Riarhin.

It came out harder than she meant. Berent was quiet for a moment.

“No,” he said. “So they won’t start now.”

He went with Jory at the fourth bell. Riarhin watched them from the hoist door go along the river path below the tanneries: a thin boy with a magpie on his shoulder, talking with both hands, and a man with a stick beside him, going slowly, bending his head to listen. She watched until they turned the corner by the boathouse. Afterwards she could recall the hand braced on the stick and the way he bent to hear Jory. She could describe his face. She still could not find Berent in it.

Lyra asked for the book while he was gone.

She had not asked for anything in three days. She had slept, mostly, by the fire, in the blanket, and woken to drink what Cadan gave her, and slept again. She had said Pellam that morning, and a little about duplicate custody, and then slept for six hours. She had looked at the man on the top step, from time to time, carefully, and said Berent under her breath, and looked away.

She had not looked at the table. Riarhin had noticed that. The book lay on it where Riarhin had put it on Midwinter eve, squared to the edge, with the torn spine towards the stair and the slip of paper sticking out a third of the way through, and Lyra had not once let her eyes go to it.

Now, in the grey of the afternoon, with Berent gone and Saikou writing at the far end of the table and Cadan asleep sitting up against the wall, Lyra said, from the blanket, “Is that mine?”

Riarhin looked up.

“The book,” said Lyra. “On the table. I saw it when he brought me in. I thought I was dreaming it. I didn’t want to look again in case I wasn’t.” Her voice was still cracked. “Is it mine?”

Riarhin brought it to her.

Lyra took it in both hands. She did not open it. She turned it over and found the torn place on the spine with her thumb, the linen tape lifting at the end, and ran her thumb along it, exactly as Berent had in the deaf woman’s room at the ropewalk.

“I tore that,” she said. “On Pellam’s desk. I was late for a meeting and I pulled it out of my satchel too fast and it caught on the corner. I was so cross.” She looked at the tape. “Ridow mended it. Badly. He’s very bad at glue.”

“He kept it for you. Under a rug. They sealed the rest.”

Lyra looked up.

“Ridow did?”

“He sat on it,” said Riarhin. “When they came to take the list. He said it was the only brave thing he did all year and it was sitting down.” She hesitated. “He asked me to tell you. He didn’t answer my letter because he was frightened. Not because he forgot. He wanted you to know it was fear.”

Lyra sat with the book in her lap for a long time.

Then she opened it at the slip.

Riarhin did not look at the page. She looked at Lyra’s face, which was thin and grey and very tired, bent over the book in the grey light from the hoist door. She watched Lyra read the slip, which was in her own hand. She watched her read the page it marked. Her lips moved a little.

“It’s an exercise,” said Lyra. “They’re all merchants’ letters. The whole second volume. It’s the dullest grammar ever written. Every sentence is somebody writing to somebody else about a cargo.” She put her finger on the page. “I was translating the fourteenth. I’d got halfway. Then they came for me and I had to put it down.”

“What does it say?”

Lyra read it. She read it first in Kahfagian, slowly, under her breath, a long soft run of sounds that Riarhin did not know. Then she looked at her slip, where the first half was written out in her small upright hand, and read that aloud.

“The house of which you wrote is still standing,” said Lyra, “and the garden, and I have—” She stopped. That was where the slip stopped.

She looked at the Kahfagian on the page for a while.

“—and I have kept your room as it was,” she said, “and will send word by the next ship whether I can come.”

She closed the book on her finger.

“That’s all,” she said. “That’s the fourteenth. The fifteenth is about pepper.” She leaned her head back against the bench. “I’ve been in the middle of that for more than a year. I used to lie in the steward’s wing and try to remember the verb. I couldn’t. It’s the wrong mood. I’d have got it wrong.” She shut her eyes. “Can come. Not will. The grammar says you use the doubtful mood when you don’t know yet whether you’ll be let.”

Riarhin sat on the floor by the bench.

At the end of the table Saikou had stopped writing. He did not look up. After a while he began again.

Berent came back after dark, alone.

He came in by the gap in the wall and up the stair slowly, more slowly than he had gone up it in thirty-eight on the morning of Midwinter eve, and stood at the top and got his breath, and then came to the fire and sat down on the bench beside Lyra without being asked, and put his stick across his knees.

Nobody said anything. Cadan got up and put the kettle on.

“The west gate,” said Berent.

He told it looking at the fire.

There was a cart under the arch of the west gate, a flat hay-cart with its shafts propped on a trestle, and on the cart there were three people laid out on boards, side by side, with their faces uncovered and their hands at their sides. There was a canvas over the arch against the weather. There were four men of the household in dark coats with lances, one at each corner of the cart, standing very still. There was a table at the side of the arch, under the canvas, with a justiciar at it in a black gown, and a clerk with a book, and a notice nailed to the post beside them.

“It said they were taken in the Queen’s house on the night of her death,” said Berent. “Three unknown persons. It said that anybody who knew them, or their names, or where they had lodged, or who they had kept company with, was to come to the table and say so. It said there would be a reward. It said that anybody who knew them and did not come forward would be taken for one of them.”

He was quiet for a moment.

“There was a line,” he said. “People go past. They always go past. It’s the thing to do, after. You go and look, so you can say you looked. The line went round the square and down the lane by the washing yards.” He moved his hands on the stick. “I stood in it with the boy. It took an hour.”

“Amari was on the left,” said Berent.

Across the loft, at the table, Saikou did not move.

“They’d cleaned his face,” said Berent. “Somebody had. They’d put his hands straight. He looked—” He stopped. “He looked like he was waiting for somebody to bring him the book.”

Saikou put his pen down on the table very carefully, so that it would not roll.

“Tavin was in the middle,” said Berent. “They hadn’t cleaned his. The boy looked at him and didn’t say anything and held on to my coat. And Nessa was on the right.” His voice did not change. “She had her shawl. Somebody had put it over her. Up to the chin, like a blanket. I don’t know who.”

Lyra had opened her eyes. She was watching him.

“The justiciar asked everybody,” said Berent. “Every single one, as they went past the table. Very polite. Do you know these persons, goodman? Do you know their names? People said no. Nearly everybody said no. They were telling the truth. They’d never seen them.” He looked at the fire. “There was an old man in front of us. Deaf. The ringer. The boy’s grandfather. He stood at the end of the cart and looked at Tavin for a long time, and the justiciar asked him, and he said what? And the justiciar asked him again, louder, and he said what? again. And again. Four times. He had his hand behind his ear. He was crying. The justiciar gave up and waved him on.”

“He could hear,” said Jory’s voice, from the stair.

Nobody had heard the boy come in. He was standing at the stair head with Penny on his shoulder. He had gone to his gran’s and come back again.

“My granda,” said Jory. “He can hear if you shout. The justiciar shouted.” He came across the loft and sat down on the floor by Cadan’s feet, with his knees up. “He knew Tavin. He drank with him. He’s been saying all week how he had a new friend who bought him cider.” He put his chin on his knees. “He said what till they let him go.”

“The vintner’s carter knew Amari,” said Berent.

He said it after a while, when the kettle had boiled and Cadan had made something hot and given it round.

“He came along the line from the other end. He wasn’t in it; he’d come in with a load and they’d stopped his cart at the gate to search it. He saw the hay-cart and stopped to look, the way you do. And he saw Amari, and he said out loud, that’s the big man who rolled my casks.” Berent turned the cup in his hands. “He didn’t mean anything by it. He was surprised. He said it the way you’d say it if you saw a man you knew in a market.”

Riarhin shut her eyes.

“They took him to the table,” said Berent. “The justiciar was very polite to him too. He asked him where he’d got the big man. The carter said at the Bell, from a fellow he played cards with, who said he knew a man wanted a day’s work. The justiciar asked him the fellow’s name. The carter didn’t know it. He said the fellow had a bird. The justiciar asked him to come and look at the middle one.” Berent looked at his cup. “He looked. He said yes. They took him away through the gate. I don’t know where. He was still saying that he’d lost at cards to the man three nights running and never thought anything of it.”

Nobody said anything.

“He’ll say where the Bell is,” said Berent. “And the Bell will say who Tavin drank with. And somebody at the Bell will say where Tavin slept.” He did not look at the stair, or the hoist door, or the three ways out. “Not tonight. Maybe not tomorrow. It’ll take them a day or two to be thorough.”

“The landlord,” said Riarhin.

“Asks nothing and remembers nothing,” said Berent. “That’s what Tavin said. I hope Tavin was right.” He drank. “He usually was, about people like that.”

“Nessa,” said Riarhin.

Berent put the cup down.

“The kitchen woman came,” he said. “From the old range. While I was in the line. She came to look because the men from the old range had all been told to go and look, in case any of them had seen anybody in the passages that night, and the kitchen woman came with them because she’d lost her mother on Midwinter morning and she wanted to get out of the kitchen.” He looked at the fire. “Her mother died on Midwinter morning. Nessa sang to her the night before. The last thing she heard. The kitchen woman told me so. In the line. She didn’t know who I was. She was just talking, the way you talk in a line. She said a woman came every evening for a month and sang her mother the old northern songs and her mother died happy.”

He was quiet.

“She went past the cart,” said Berent. “She looked at all three of them. She looked at Nessa. Right at her face, with the shawl up to her chin. And she went on to the table, and the justiciar asked her, and she said no. She said she’d never seen any of them in her life.” His voice did not change at all. “She wasn’t lying. I watched her face. She didn’t know her. She’d had her in her kitchen every evening for a month.”

Lyra put her hand out, from the blanket, and laid it on Berent’s arm. He did not move.

“Nobody knew her,” he said. “Nobody in the whole line. An hour of people going past. The justiciar asked them all and nobody knew her.” He looked down at Lyra’s hand on his arm. “I did. I knew her. I stood at the end of the cart and looked at her and I knew every line of her. And the justiciar asked me.”

“What did you say?”

Berent looked at Riarhin.

“He asked me if I knew them,” he said. “I said no. And then he asked me my name, for the book. He was asking everybody who came from the north; he could hear it in my voice. And I told him.” He looked back at the fire. “I said Berent. The same name that’s nailed to the post beside his table on the proclamation. I said it straight to his face.”

The loft was very quiet.

“He wrote it down,” said Berent. “Then he looked at the proclamation. Back at me. He asked who I served.” He picked up the cup. “I said I was taking the boy to his gran’s. Jory told him the street. He let us go.”

Riarhin looked at the stick beside his knee.

“Don’t try that again.”

“No,” he said.

He rubbed his eyes and told the rest.

A newer notice beside the proclamation deferred reconsideration of Margery’s wardship until after the Queen’s burial. Somebody had already written in chalk underneath it, God keep the little one, and somebody else had rubbed out little one and written Queen. There had been a third notice, from the Hall, which said that the court of honour behind the Hall of Dawning would not sit after Midwinter, by order of the council, in the time of mourning, and that all challenges entered in its book were void. Two names had been chalked beneath it and struck through.

“And the lieutenant,” said Berent.

Across the loft, by the wall, Cadan had gone quite still.

“They carried him through the west gate while I was in the line,” said Berent. “On a bier. From the old range to his father’s house outside the walls. His men carried him, eight of them, with their coats on inside out for mourning, and the whole line took its hats off. There was a dog behind the bier.” He did not look at Cadan. “A brindled bitch. On a rope, with a boy holding it. She kept stopping and looking back at the palace. The boy had to pull her.”

Cadan did not say anything. He sat against the wall with his hands folded on his knees and his eyes open, looking at nothing.

After a while Jory, on the floor by his feet, reached up without looking and put Penny on Cadan’s knee. The magpie walked up his arm to his shoulder and sat there, and Cadan let her, and did not move.

Later, when the others slept, Riarhin sat by the fire with Berent.

He had not lain down. He sat on the bench with Lyra asleep against his shoulder, which she had done at some point without waking, and he had not moved her. He was looking at the fire.

“You shouldn’t have told him your name,” said Riarhin.

“No.”

“Why did you?”

Berent was quiet for a long time.

“Everybody else at that table was saying no because they didn’t know,” he said at last. “I was saying no because I did.” He moved his head a little, carefully, so as not to wake Lyra. “I wanted to say one true thing before I went past.”

Riarhin looked at him in the firelight: his face, his hands on the stick, the sword leaning against the bench beside him.

“Tomorrow,” she said. “Or the day after. They’ll come to the Bell.”

“I know.”

“We can’t stay.”

“I know.” Berent looked down at Lyra asleep against his shoulder. “She can’t walk to the gate. I can’t carry her there. The gates are shut. They’ll open for carts in a day or two, for food, and they’ll look in every cart.” He looked back at the fire. “I don’t know how we go. I thought about it the whole way back. I don’t know.”

Riarhin did not know either. She sat by the fire and listened to the river going by under the hoist door, black and quick in the dark, towards the sea.

Chapter Thirty-Two — What to Tell Her

The girl came along the river path on the fourth morning with a basket of washing on her hip, and stopped below the hoist door, and looked up.

Riarhin was at the door. She had been at it since first light, watching the path, because Berent had said a day or two and she had not been able to sit still with it. She saw the girl come round the corner by the boathouse, thin and small, in a brown cloak, walking the way a girl walks who has been told exactly where to go and is counting doors. She saw her stop below the tannery and tilt her head back and look straight up at the hoist door, and at Riarhin standing in it.

The girl did not wave. She lifted one hand, a little, and put it down again.

It was the girl from the loom-room. Riarhin had never seen her. She knew her anyway, from Saikou’s telling: the chilblains on both hands, the thin face, the way she stood as though she expected the ground to move. Rosine.

“Saikou,” said Riarhin.

He came to the door and looked down.

“Yes,” he said, after a moment. “That is her.” He was quiet. “I told Margery a man at the tannery dealt in night terrors. I did not tell her which tannery. There are eleven on this reach.” He looked at the girl on the path, who was still looking up. “She has counted them.”

Cadan went down and brought her up by the gap in the wall.

She came up the stair with the basket still on her hip, as though she had been told not to put it down for anything, and stood at the top and looked round the loft: at Riarhin by the hoist door, and Saikou beside her, and Lyra in the blanket, and Berent on the bench, and Jory on the floor with a magpie, and the monk. Her eyes went to Saikou and stayed there. Something in her face let go.

“Master,” she said. “It was you. She said it would be you.”

“Rosine,” said Saikou gently. “Put the basket down.”

“She said not to put it down till I’d found the right one.”

“You have found the right one.”

She put it down. Her hands, without it, went to each other and held on. There were chilblains on both of them, and on the backs of her fingers the red marks of a girl who worked at a loom.

“You’re sleeping,” said Saikou.

“Yes, master. Facing the window. She wakes me before the hour and tells me the weather.” The girl looked at the floor. “She hasn’t been, though. The last three nights. She’s been at the palace. I’ve been waking myself.” She looked up. “I do it very well now. I wake up and I say, it’s cold, and clear, and the wind’s from the river, and then I go back to sleep. I don’t even need her.”

Saikou did not say anything for a moment.

“That is very good,” he said.

“She said you’d say that.” Rosine bent to the basket. Under the folded shirts, at the bottom, there was a letter, sealed with plain wax and no mark, and she took it out and held it in both hands and looked at the room again. “It’s for the woman,” she said. “Not for you, master. She said you’d be there and there’d be a woman, and I was to give it to the woman and nobody else. She said I’d know which.”

She looked from Riarhin to Lyra and back.

Then she crossed the loft and held it out to Riarhin.

I do not know your hand. I know your face, both of them. That will have to do.

She asks where her mother is. I have told her the truth, which is that her mother is dead, and her father. I told her the first night, in the nursery, with the door barred, before anybody else could come and tell her something else. She did not understand it. She asked when they would come back. I said they would not. She asked again in the morning, and I said it again. She has asked every morning. I say it every morning. On the third morning she did not ask. She asked instead who did it.

I have not told her.

The council has given her to me until it decides otherwise. It has not decided anything else. They are quarrelling over the burial already. The Everflame say she was theirs and must lie in the cathedral with the fire. The Light say she was named in the chapel of the washerwomen by her grandmother’s wish and must lie there with the lamps. The Chancellor says she will lie wherever the council says, when the council says, and until then she lies in the Hall, and he has put guards on the Hall doors against both churches. Nobody has asked me. Nobody has asked the child.

When she is older she will ask again. She is Everblood. She will not stop asking. I will have to have an answer that is true, because if I give her one that is not, somebody will give her the true one later, and it will be somebody who wants something from her.

You will not be here. You are going. I know that much. I don’t want to know where.

So write it down. What you did, and why. Plainly. In your hand, so that she will know it was you who wrote it and not somebody who says they know. I will keep it for her. I will read it before I give it to her. She has already been left enough that she must make sense of alone. If she never asks it that way, I will burn it on the day I die.

One more thing, and then Rosine can come home.

When they brought him up from the linen stair his seal was not on his hand. The Marshal’s ring. Everyone is asking where it is. The council has had the stair searched and the passage and the kitchen. It is not there. The men from Serrat say they never saw it. I do not know whether you took it. If you did, keep it from anyone who wants to borrow it. If you did not, somebody else has it.

M.

Riarhin read it twice, standing at the hoist door with the cold coming in on her back. Then she folded it and held it.

Nobody asked her what it said. Lyra, by the fire, was watching her face. Saikou was watching the girl, who had sat down on the floor beside Jory without being asked, and was looking at the magpie on his shoulder with the wary interest of a girl who had never been close to a bird.

“She wants an answer,” said Riarhin.

“Yes,” said Saikou.

“She wants it written. For the child. For when she’s older.” Riarhin looked at the folded letter in her hand. “She means to read it herself.”

“She will have to answer the questions after,” said Saikou. He turned back to the girl on the floor. “Rosine. Are you hungry?”

“Yes, master.”

“Cadan will find you something. Then you will wait. The woman has a letter to write, and it will take her some time.”

It took her all day.

She sat at the table in the loft with paper Saikou gave her and the stub of pencil from her sleeve, and a candle, though it was daylight. He sat at the far end of the table with Amari’s book and his own papers and wrote in his small neat hand, and did not look up, and when she tore a sheet in half he did not look up either.

The first one she wrote in the morning, fast, in an hour. It said everything. It said the Tarne, and the seventeen thousand, and the villages behind Serrat, and the man in the Fairport market with four children on his cart, and the spring muster against Ecktoria, and the grain that arrived the week it was needed and the bridge that went out in a flood and the duke who fell down a stair, and a brass case the size of a coin on a black ribbon that Riarhin had carried north in her own coat. It said why. It went on for three sides.

She read it through when she had finished.

It was all true. She could have stood beside every line of it in front of any court in the realm.

It was not written to a child.

She put it in the fire.

The second one she did not finish.

It began I am sorry. She wrote that and sat looking at it for a long time. She added for what I have taken from you, and stopped. The sentence asked something of the child. She crossed it out.

She put that in the fire too.

In the afternoon Lyra said, from the blanket, “What are you trying to tell her?”

Riarhin did not turn round.

“What I did,” she said. “And why.”

“Which is harder?”

Riarhin thought about it.

“Why.”

“Then do the what first.” Lyra’s voice was cracked and tired and clear, the voice that had once gone through a return line by line to find the place where it disagreed with itself. “Put down what you did. Just that. Where and when and how. As if you were writing it for Pellam. So that nobody afterwards can say it was otherwise.” She shifted in the blanket. “Then tell her why. You can use another sheet.”

Riarhin sat for a while.

Then she took a new sheet, and began.

She wrote it slowly. It took the rest of the afternoon. When she had finished it the light in the hoist door had gone grey and Cadan had lit the lamp, and Rosine was asleep on the floor with her head on the basket and Jory beside her, and Penny had gone to sleep on the table with her head under her wing, next to Amari’s book.

It was one side of one sheet. She read it through once, and did not change anything.

To Matylde, daughter of Veralyn and Tylan.

My name is Riarhin. On the night of Midwinter eve, in the year you were three, I came into your mother’s house with a sword. I had others with me. They killed men of your household so that nobody could come in time. Then I killed your father on the linen stair outside your nursery, and then your mother in her own room, by the hearth. I did both. Nobody else did either.

Your father was trying to reach the men below who could have saved your mother. He barred your door first. The last thing he said might have been your name. I did not hear it clearly. I will not tell you it was, in case it was not.

Your mother was looking at your door when she died.

I did it because your mother had driven seventeen thousand people from their homes in the Tarne in the first frosts of that year, and meant to go to war in the spring, and would not have stopped. Other people could tell you whether that was so. You should ask them, and not only me. Ask people who loved her. Ask people who were driven out.

Before I went in I sent for Margery, so that she would be the one who came to you. Nobody told me to. I did it because I did not want you to be alone with the people who would come after.

I am not writing this to be forgiven. I am writing it so that you know it was me, and that I knew what I was doing, and that I have not pretended otherwise to anyone. If you want to find me when you are grown, I will not hide from you.

Riarhin.

She folded it, and sealed it with a drop of candle-wax, and pressed nothing into the wax, because she had no seal. Then she sat with it in her hands.

Saikou had stopped writing. She did not know when. He was looking at her down the length of the table.

“It is short,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Good.” He did not ask what was in it. He looked at the folded paper in her hands for a long moment. “You wrote the last line.”

“You can’t see the last line.”

“I can see your face,” said Saikou. “You wrote something at the end that you had not meant to write when you began, and then you did not cross it out.” He picked up his pen again.

She did not answer.

She woke Rosine at the sixth bell, gently, and the girl sat up with her hair on end and said at once, before she was properly awake, “It’s cold, and clear, and the wind’s from the river,” and then looked round the lamplit loft and went red.

“Yes,” said Riarhin. “It is.”

She gave her the letter. The girl put it at the bottom of the basket, under the shirts, exactly where the other one had been, and folded the shirts back over it with great care.

“There’s something else,” said Riarhin. “For Mistress Margery. Not in writing. You’ll say it to her yourself.”

Rosine looked up at her.

“Tell her,” said Riarhin, “that I don’t have the ring. Tell her I left it on his hand and closed his fingers over it. Tell her that when I came back up the stair it was still there. And that after that I went into the Queen’s room, and I don’t know who went down the stair.”

The girl said it back to her, word for word, with her lips moving. She said it twice more.

“And tell her—” Riarhin stopped.

Rosine waited.

“Tell her thank you for the lamp,” said Riarhin. “A year ago. She’ll know which.”

Cadan took the girl down to the river path and saw her as far as the corner by the boathouse. Riarhin watched from the hoist door. The girl went away along the path with the basket on her hip, in the dusk, walking the way she had come, counting the doors backwards.

When she had gone round the corner Riarhin stayed in the door.

Behind her, in the loft, Lyra said, “Somebody took his ring.”

“Yes.”

“The Marshal’s seal. The one that sends the garrison.” Lyra’s voice was very quiet. “Somebody went down that stair after you went up it, and stepped over a dead man, and took the thing that could move an army, and went away with it, and nobody saw.” She was quiet for a moment. “Enid said that, didn’t she. Your woman on the hill. You told me on the second night. She said some men would be very quick to pick up what the Queen left.”

“She said she didn’t know which men.”

“No.” Lyra drew the blanket closer. “Nobody ever does. That’s what makes them quick.”

Across the table Saikou had stopped writing again. He was looking at the lamp.

“The riders from Serrat,” he said. “In the lower hall. Six of them. They came in at the tenth bell with dispatches from Veyrac and they were forty feet from that stair when the bell rang arms.” He turned the pen in his fingers. “Veyrac holds the Tarne line. Veyrac counted the seventeen thousand. Veyrac is still counting. You heard the Marshal say it, in her room, on the last night.” He set the pen down. “I do not know that it was one of them. I am only saying who was on the stair.”

Riarhin looked out at the river.

It was full dark now. The water went by under the hoist door, black and quick, and on the far bank the palace stood up against the sky with its windows lit, all of them, every window in the new wing, as they had not been lit since Midwinter eve. Somewhere in the Hall, under guard, between two churches, a woman lay in state.

Riarhin shut the hoist door.

Chapter Thirty-Three — The River

Saikou found the line when he opened Amari’s book to write.

He had not written in it since the second night. He had written in his own papers, all day, every day, and Riarhin had not asked what. But when the girl had gone and the hoist door was shut he drew the lamp towards him and opened Amari’s book at the last page, where Going home was, and sat with the pen lifted, looking at it.

Then he turned back a page, and looked, and turned back another.

“Riarhin,” he said.

She came and stood at his shoulder.

He put his long finger on the page. It was the page before three and eleven, and a button. The entries on it were the ordinary ones, the lamp oil and the bread and the cider for the deaf ringer, in Amari’s broad upright hand, one under another. Between two of them, squeezed in afterwards in a smaller hand as though there had not been room, there was a line she had seen on the second night and not understood.

Fourth night after. T. Not argued.

“After what?” said Riarhin.

“I thought it was a price,” said Saikou. “On the second night. I thought it was something Tavin had bought for after, and paid four nights’ rent on, and I did not want to know what. I did not look at it again.” He did not take his finger off the page. “It is the fourth night after.”

They looked at each other.

Behind them, at the top of the stair, somebody coughed.

It was the landlord.

Riarhin had never seen him above the ground floor. He was a small bent man in a leather apron gone black with old tanning, and he had no teeth, and he stood at the top of the stair with one hand on the wall and looked round the loft at all of them as though he had never looked at a face in his life and did not intend to start. His eyes went past Riarhin and Saikou, and past Lyra in the blanket, and past Berent, and stopped on the table, where Penny was asleep next to Amari’s book.

“Bird’s still here,” he said. His voice was a soft gummy mumble. “Thought so. He said the bird’d know.”

“Who said?” said Riarhin.

“Your friend. With the bird.” The landlord did not look at her. “He paid me a month. Said I wasn’t to tell. Said if he wasn’t here the fourth night after the feast, I was to come up the stair at the eighth bell and tell whoever was.” He scratched his neck. “It’s the eighth bell.”

“Tell us what?”

“Boat,” said the landlord. “If the river was open, he wanted a boat under this door tonight. It wasn’t open this morning. Now the council’s lifting the chain for the grain barges, and Mab Tulley says she’ll take her hides through with them. Eleventh bell. Fairport market.” He looked at the floor. “He paid her half. He said the rest was when you got there. He said you’d have it.” He turned to go down. “I don’t know anything about it. I don’t know you. I let anything to anybody.”

He went down the stair one step at a time, with his hand on the wall.

Nobody in the loft said anything.

Berent, on the bench, had put his head in his hands.

“He said—” Riarhin stopped. Then she said it, and heard it in Tavin’s voice. “There’s always an after. People forget to buy a boat for it.”

Saikou looked down at the line in Amari’s book. “He did not tell me,” he said.

They had three hours. They used them.
