Cadan went down to the drying floor and brought up the hoist rope and looked at the block. It was a block Tavin had bought in the old town in their first week in Valora and hung from the hoist beam over the river door without saying why. Cadan looked at it. Then he tested it with his weight, and it held.

Riarhin packed. There was not much. Remorse, which she wrapped in oilcloth. The knives she had left, which were in her sleeves. Toothpick still had wax in the seam of its grip. She picked it out with her thumbnail before sheathing it. Lyra’s grammar, which Lyra asked for and put inside the blanket against her chest. Amari’s book, which Saikou put inside his own coat beside Aurine’s note, and buttoned.

Lyra could stand now, for a little. She stood by the fire with Berent’s arm under hers and her face set, while Cadan measured her against the hoist sling with his eye. She did not ask how they were going to get her down into a boat from a loft door thirty feet above the water. She looked at the block on the beam and at Cadan and said, “All right.”

Jory came at the ninth bell.

He came up the stair from the gap in the wall with his face red from the cold and his breath short, and stood at the top of it, and looked at them all packed and standing, and at the hoist door open on the river and the rope going down into the dark, and his face did what it had done on the third morning.

“You’re going,” he said.

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

“Tonight.”

“Yes.”

He looked at the table, where Penny was.

The magpie was awake. She had been awake since the landlord came up, walking up and down the table the way she had on the first morning, from end to end, with her head going from side to side. Cadan had held out his hand to her twice, and twice she had looked at it and not come.

Now Jory stood at the top of the stair, and Penny stopped walking.

She looked at Cadan. She looked at the boy. Then she opened her wings and went across the loft, low, and landed on Jory’s shoulder, and put her beak in his hair.

Jory stood very still.

“She’s decided,” said Cadan. He lowered his hand. He did not seem surprised. “That is her right. She was never anybody’s. He told me so. He said she only stayed with him because he kept the biscuits in his left pocket.” He looked at the boy. “Which pocket do you keep yours in?”

“I don’t have biscuits,” said Jory. His voice had gone thin.

“You will need to get some,” said Cadan. “She prefers the kind with caraway. Keep them in the left pocket. She will not trust the right.”

Jory put his hand up to the bird on his shoulder. She let him.

“I’ve to ring tomorrow,” he said. “For the Queen. They’re burying her at noon. The Chancellor said. Not in either church. In the Hall chapel, where nobody has to walk first.” He swallowed. “I’ve to ring the passing bell. Nine and nine and nine, and then the long one. The sacristan said it had to be me because I knew the rope.” He looked at Riarhin. “Is that all right?”

Riarhin looked at him for a long moment.

“Ring it well,” she said.

“I always ring it well,” said Jory.

He stood a moment longer with the magpie on his shoulder. Then he turned and went down the stair, fast, without saying anything else, and they heard him cross the yard and go through the gap in the wall.

Mab Tulley’s barge came under the hoist door a little before the eleventh bell.

Riarhin heard it before she saw it: the soft knock of a pole against the tannery wall below, and the creak of timber, and a woman’s voice saying something short and rude to somebody else. Then a lantern came up out of the dark on the water, shaded to a slit, and swung, and a face looked up into the light from the hoist door.

It was a broad red face under a man’s hat, and it was not pleased.

“Tavin’s,” said the woman. “Is it?”

“Yes.”

“Where is he?”

Riarhin did not answer.

The woman on the barge looked up at her for a long moment with the lantern in her hand. Then she spat into the river.

“Thought so,” she said. “Heard about the three at the gate.” She set the lantern down. “He was the only man I ever played cards with who told me after when he’d cheated. Every time. Said it made it fair.” She put her hands on her hips. “He paid me half. The other half’s not come. And it’s double, now, because they’ve put a justiciar’s clerk and four lances on the chain boat since he paid me, and they’re sticking an iron down into every load that goes through, and I’ve a husband.”

“We haven’t got it,” said Riarhin.

It was true. She had gone through it in her head while Cadan tested the block. The Valora heap was gone. The other heap had gone south in a pepper cart with a woman who was never coming back. She had four pence and a button.

Mab Tulley looked up at her.

“Then you’re not going anywhere,” she said, “are you?”

Saikou came to the hoist door beside her.

He had a sheet of paper in his hand, and his pen, and the little flat case of sealing wax he kept in his satchel, which Riarhin had seen him use once, at the chancery, on a letter of introduction. He leaned out of the door into the lantern light.

“Mistress Tulley,” he said. “You go down to Fairport.”

“I do.”

“You know the factor of House Ira on the Fairport quay.”

Mab Tulley took the pipe from her mouth and looked up at him. She kept it in her hand while he spoke.

“Everybody on the Fairport quay knows the Ira factor,” she said. “She’s the one who wins.”

“She is.” Saikou put the paper against the door frame and wrote three lines on it, and signed it, and held the wax to the lantern the woman lifted for him until it dripped, and pressed his thumb into it, because he had no seal with him but the one his mother’s house would know. “This is an order on her. For what you have asked. And the same again. Payable at Fairport, when you have put us ashore there.” He folded it.

He held it down out of the door. The woman looked at it in the lantern light without taking it.

“That’s a lot of money on a piece of paper.”

“It is my mother’s paper,” said Saikou. “She has never once failed to pay what she put her name to. It is the thing she is proudest of.”

Mab Tulley took it. She read it. She read it twice, holding the lantern close, and then she put it inside her coat and buttoned the coat over it.

Riarhin looked at Saikou. He was putting the wax away. His face was quite calm.

“That’s hers,” she said quietly.

“Yes.” He closed the satchel. “One more line will not change very much. It will only change the day she reminds me of it.” He looked out at the dark river. “Tavin would have argued her down to half. I have not the gift.”

“Get your people down,” said Mab Tulley. “The chain lifts at the half hour and I’m not missing it for anybody.”

They went down the hoist.

Lyra first, in the sling, wrapped in the blanket with the grammar against her chest, with Cadan paying out the rope over Tavin’s block and Berent at the door calling down to her in a low steady voice the whole way, nearly, nearly, there, there’s her hand, let go. Then Riarhin, because she could not climb down a rope with her side and her arm, and would not be carried, and the sling was the only thing left. She sat in it with Remorse across her knees and went down past the drying-floor windows of the tannery into the cold, with the black water coming up towards her and the barge’s timbers under it, and a broad red hand came out of the dark and took her by the collar and swung her in over the side.

Then Saikou, on the rope, hand over hand. Then Cadan, who came down it the way he had come out of the breach at Harrowmere, as though it were a stair.

Berent came last.

He could not come down a rope. He had known that since the landlord came up. He had not said anything about it. He stood in the hoist door with his stick and Na’ar’s sword on his back and looked down at them in the barge, and then along the river path below the tanneries, which was empty in the dark.

“I’ll walk,” he said. “Along the path. To the chain. I’ll come on board at the chain boat, as a hand. With the lantern.” He looked down at Mab Tulley. “You need a hand?”

“I’ve got a husband,” said Mab Tulley.

“Where is he?”

“At home with the ague, the idle sod.”

“Then you need a hand,” said Berent. “I’ll be at the chain. Lame man with a stick. I’ll hold your lantern and say I’m your cousin from the north. Call me Rob.” He looked at Riarhin, down in the barge, in the dark. “Nobody’ll know me. You said so yourself.”

He shut the hoist door on them from inside. They heard him cross the loft, and the stair, slowly, one step at a time, and the yard, and the gap in the wall.

The hides lay in the well of the barge in a stack as high as a man’s chest, salted and folded, and they stank.

Mab Tulley made a space for them under the stack at the stern, where the barge had a second floor laid a hand’s width above the first, with a gap between, and the gap had been made for something that was not hides. Lyra went into it first, and then Riarhin beside her, on her good side, with Remorse along her body. Saikou and Cadan went in at the other end. The boards went down over them. Then the hides, one on another, heavy and wet and cold, until the dark was complete and the smell was so thick that Riarhin breathed through her mouth and still tasted it.

Lyra’s hand found hers in the dark and held on.

The barge moved.

She could not see anything. She could feel the river under the double floor, the long easy pull of it, and hear the pole going in and out, and Mab Tulley’s breathing, and the creak of the timbers. She counted the strokes of the pole. She could not help it. She had counted a hundred and eighty when the barge slowed, and bumped against something, and stopped, and she heard voices.

There were a great many of them. The barges were stacked up three deep at the chain, Mab Tulley had said, waiting for it to lift: grain barges going down empty to fetch the city’s bread from the coast, hide barges, a lime barge, a barge of wine for the burial. The voices went back and forth over the water, arguing about who had been there first and whose charter said what. Somewhere above them a bell was tolling, slow, one stroke and a long pause and another, and Riarhin knew it without having to think: the cathedral bell, tolling for the Queen lying in the Hall, because the Everflame would not wait for noon.

Then, close, a man’s voice, bored and cold. “Manifest.”

“Hides,” said Mab Tulley. “Tanners’ quarter. For the Fairport market. Same as every winter.”

“How many?”

“Sixty. Salted. Count them if you like. I’ll hold your nose.”

“Who’s he?”

A pause.

“Cousin,” said Mab Tulley. “From the north. Come down for the feast and got shut in. He’s going home. He can hold a lantern. That’s all he’s good for.”

“Name?”

“Rob,” said Berent’s voice. It was quite close, above her, somewhere near the stern. It was steady and a little bored, a man who had been asked his name a great many times that week.

There was a silence.

Under the hides, in the dark, Lyra’s hand went very tight on Riarhin’s.

“Hold your lantern up, Rob,” said the cold voice. “I want to see the stack.”

Light came, faint, through the gaps in the hides above them. Then something struck the stack. It came down through the hides from above, hard, an iron rod, with a sound like a spade going into wet earth, once, twice, three times, and stopped, and was pulled out. It had come down a foot from Riarhin’s head. She had felt the boards over her jump with it.

“God’s teeth,” said the cold voice. “What do they salt them with.”

“Piss, mostly,” said Mab Tulley.

The iron came down again, at the other end of the stack, near where Saikou and Cadan were. Once. Twice. It did not come down a third time. Somebody above coughed and spat over the side.

“Go on,” said the voice. “Get it out of my sight. Get it out of the city.”

The barge moved.

They let her out from under the hides a little below the chain, where the river ran on under the city’s west wall towards the open fields.

She sat up on the stern boards in the cold with the blanket round her and breathed. The air smelt of frost and mud and river, and after the hides it was the sweetest thing she had ever breathed in her life. Lyra lay beside her with her eyes shut and the grammar against her chest. Cadan sat on the gunwale with his hands folded. Saikou sat with his back against the hides and Amari’s book on his knee, unopened, looking back at the city.

Berent was at the tiller with Mab Tulley, holding the lantern. He did not look round.

Behind them, up the river, the bell was still tolling. It came across the water very faint, one stroke and a long pause and another.

They passed the west gate.

It stood on the bank a little way back from the river, where the washing yards were, a dark bulk of stone against the lights of the city, with a torch burning under its arch. Riarhin could see the torch from the water, and the shape of the canvas stretched over the arch against the weather, and under it, very small, the flat shape of a cart with its shafts propped on a trestle, and four men standing very still, one at each corner.

Nobody said anything.

Mab Tulley, at the tiller, took her pipe out of her mouth. She leaned over the side of the barge as they came level with the gate, and said something low and short into the water, the way a person says something they have said a great many times before. Then she straightened and put the pipe back.

“My da,” she said, to nobody. “Went in off the chain boat in the flood year. You say it into the river going past. The river takes it down to the sea and it doesn’t come back up again and bother anybody.” She drew on the pipe. “It’s a Belworth thing. My ma was from the Belworth. Everybody on this river does it now.”

Saikou had closed his eyes.

He got up from against the hides, slowly, with the book in his hand. He went to the side of the barge and knelt there on the boards, and leaned out over the black water going by, and said something into it, very low, that Riarhin did not hear. Then something else. Then a third thing.

He stayed there a while, kneeling, with his head bowed over the water.

Then he came back and sat down against the hides, and opened Amari’s book at the last page, where Going home was, and took the pen from his coat, and began, by the light of Berent’s lantern, to write.

A long time afterwards, when the gate was out of sight and the bell had stopped, Cadan got up from the gunwale.

He went to the side of the barge where Saikou had knelt, and knelt there himself, stiffly, in his grey sleeves. He leaned out over the water. He said one word into it, quite clearly, in his mild voice, so that Riarhin, sitting three yards away, heard it.

“Guichard.”

He knelt there a moment longer. Then he got up, and went back to the gunwale, and folded his hands.

Nobody said anything. Saikou went on writing.

Riarhin sat on the stern boards with the blanket round her.

She could feel the side, pulling. She could feel the arm, which had begun to stiffen in the cold. In the pocket on the right side of her coat there was a small hard shape the size of a coin, wrapped in linen, that she had not touched with her hand and had not given to anybody. In the pocket on the left, where she had moved it on Midwinter eve to make room, there was a letter in a broad crowded hand, with a strip of cloth folded into it the colour of a loch in the morning when there is no wind.

The river went on under the barge, towards Fairport, and the sea.

She took the letter out. The strip of blue cloth had worked loose from the fold, and she pushed it back with her thumb. Ailsa had packed for another sea and left the room at Kilrenny; Riarhin had still to tell her where she was.

At the other end of the barge Saikou had stopped writing. He sat with the pen lifted over the page, looking at what he had written, in his small neat hand, under Amari’s broad one.

“Saikou,” said Riarhin. “Is there more paper?”

He looked up from Amari’s book.

“There is always more paper,” he said. “Who is it for?”

“Silkstrand.”

He tore a clean sheet from the back of his own sheaf and held it out to her across the stern boards, and she took it, and put it on her knee on top of Remorse in its oilcloth, and smoothed it flat with her right hand. Berent held the lantern a little lower without being asked. She took up the stub of pencil.

A., she wrote.

Reader’s Companion

A selective reference to the people and terms in this volume. Descriptions identify relationships and roles without recounting the ending.

Dramatis Personae

Ailsa — Riarhin’s partner in Ubral.

Amari — Saikou’s longstanding companion.

Aurine — A member of Riarhin’s company, with obligations of her own.

Berent — Elyas’s former squire and Riarhin’s companion.

Cadan — A member of Riarhin’s company, devoted to Ikasha.

Enid — The hag whom Riarhin consults.

Lyra — Riarhin’s friend, imprisoned in Valora.

Mab Tulley — A barge-woman; distinct from the patron Mab.

Margery — A member of the court whose relationship with Matylde matters to Riarhin’s plans.

Matylde — Veralyn and Tylan’s daughter.

Nessa Vey — A singer whose workings have a cost in memory and recognition.

Riarhin — A half-Lethai traveller and former scout, left-handed by preference.

Saikou Ira — An invoker and exorcist of the Kahfagian shrines.

Tavin Rusk — A thief and member of the company.

Tylan — Her consort and Marshal.

Veralyn — Queen of Viterra and wearer of Kalrantha.

Places and Terms

Ikasha — The patron to whom Cadan is devoted.

Kalrantha — The amulet worn by Veralyn.

Remorse — Riarhin’s double-edged elven sword.

The Last Blade — Riarhin’s other Ykrul duelling knife, distinct from Toothpick.

Toothpick — Riarhin’s name for the knife once called Fate’s Edge.

Valora — The royal city and the destination of Riarhin’s company.
